## "Blessings From Reverend Bobo"

Dr. Santosh Gupta M.A. Ph.D. the learned author of this philosophical treatise is well known to me. She is a devoted spiritualist and a good scholar. She has put forth considerable efforts for a long time in producing such a nice book which has earned laurels for her in the form a university doctorate (Ph.D.).

Lord Chaitanya's philosophical doctrine of Achintya bedhabedha must have left deep impression on the mind of this author. Every student of philosophy in India and abroad must welcome this book for its philosophical fragrance and spiritual utility.

Sri Chaitanya descended into this world about five centuries ago at a time when India was encircled by the clouds of Shankar a's Advaita vada, Lord Buddha's emphatic denial of the existence of a permanent soul and carvaka's assertion denying the existence of God with his country wide followers carrying his banner of atheism.

Sri Chaitanya spread the moon light of Prema Bhakti in India by his Nama Kirtan movement which made even wild animals dance in ecstasy! The philosophy of Prema Bhakti in this book purifies all the three worlds as declared by Veda Vyasa in Srimad Bhagavata. The philosophy of Prema Bhakti followed by the Gopis of Vrindavan emphasised in this book serves as a beacon light in dispelling the dark forces of Godlessness rapidly spreading in this world.

May this book enjoy the wide circulation in the world by the divine grace of Sri Radha Rani!

## Foreword

The concept of bhakti can be seen in seminal form in the Visnusukta (I, 154) of the Rgveda wherein it has been said that the god Visnu resides in heavenly abode which is the source for the flow of the nectar of bliss (madhva utsah) on the earth. Fortunate beings who succeed in elevating themselves to that level enjoy the nectar of heavenly bliss in the gods' company. The Chandogyopanisad refers to Ekavana Vidva which probably represents a distinct school of spiritual thought advocating bhakti as a means for the achievement of salvation together with the enjoyment of bliss. The mention of Sandilya Vidya in the Upanisads also testifies to of the existence of the cult of bhakti in the Upanisadic period. There are indirect references in some texts indicating that the Bhagavata tradition existed as a religious current in as early as 4th Century B.C., though no literature belonging to that period is available now. The Naravani-Upakhyana occurring in the Santiparvan of Mahabharata (Chapters 335 onwards) refers to Pancaratra tradition in the Svetadvipa which was brought down to this land by the saga, Narada. The Pancaratra school, in fact, represents that particular school of religious thought in the post-vedic period in which bhakti holds prominent position in the religio-spiritual discipline laid down in that school. It exerted good deal of influence on later schools of Saivism and Vedanta e.g. Advaita Saivism of Kashmir, Ramanuja's School of Visistadvaita Vedanta, Madhva's School of dualistic Vedanta, Vallabha's School of Sudhadvait Vedanta etc. The Pancaratra Agamas served as a source of inspiration to the development of Vaisnava Tantric tradition in which bhakti occupies central position. The four schools of Vaisnava thought emanated hi course of time from the ancient cult of bhakti enshrined in Pancaratra Agama and Vaisnava Tantras. These are Sri Vaisnava tradition, Brahma tradition, Rudra tradition and Sanaka tradition advocated by

Ramanuja, Anandatirtha or Madhva, Visnuswami and Vallabha and Nimbarka respectively. Many Vaisnava acaryas who flourished later and propagated the religio-philosophical thought of these schools revolving round the central doctrine of bhakti, contributed in a large scale to the development of Vaisnava thought that resulted in the production of huge corpus of valuable literature in later times.

The concept of bhakti as a philosophical tenet however, reached the zenith of its development in the Srimad-Bhagavata Purana which is looked upon by the Vaisnava acaryas and saints as the main source for the Vaisnava thought. The Bhagavata has influenced the philosophical thoughts of Vallabha and Gaudiya Vaisnavas most who have drawn their inspiration mainly from this important Vaisnava work. Almost all Vaisnava schools have attempted to explain the true purport of Srimad Bhagavata in the light of their wisdom, the most popular being the Bhagavata tatparya Nirnava by Anandatirtha or Madhva, the Subodhini by Vallabhacarya, the Brhad Vaisnava tosini by Sri Sanatana Gosvami the Krama sandarbha by Srijiva Gosvami, both belonging to the Gaudiya school & Sridhari by Sridhara Svami etc.

Historically speaking, Gaudiya Vaisnavism is held to be an offshoot of Madhva's school of Vaisnavism but the Gaudiya Vaisnavites led by Sri Caitanya do not subscribe to the philosophy of dualism advocated by Madhva. They propagate instead the philosophy of acintya bhedabheda (monism of a special kind) and in this way they differ substantially from their predecessors in their approach to philosophical thought and the formulation of their metaphysical thought of distinct kind.

Dr. Santosh Gupta in her valuable book "The conception of Bhakti in Gaudiya Vaisnava philosophy" has thrown light on the contribution made by Sri Caitanya and his followers, particularly Sri Sanatana, Sri Rupa and Sri jiva Goswami in their learned treatises exposing Sri Caitanya's thoughts. She has started the discussion with the examination of the concept of Purusartha in ancient Indian thought in historical perspective as it is a cardinal concept formulated by Indian philosophers in socio-ethical backdrop. She has traced the development of this concept from the earliest times up to the rise of Gaudiya Vaisnava School as this has relevance in the spiritual discipline to be followed by individual beings.

She has then, taken up a comparative study of supreme goal in man's life according to the different schools of Indian philosophical thought, both orthodox and heterodox. This discussion is very appropriate because, according to Gaudiya Vaisnavites, the achievement of salvation does not result in the merger or the extinction of the individual being in to the Supreme Being or God. It leaves room for the enjoyment of the divine bliss in the company of God. The cultivation of bhakti is essential for this enjoyment and for the attainment of salvation which is only a bye-product.

Her main thrust in this book, is on Gaudiya Vaisnava concept of Bhakti which is unique in the religio-philosophical literature of this country. Different acaryas have defined bhakti in different ways, for the Gaudiya Vaisnavites bhakti is most important principle for the union of the individual being with the Divine which has been exposed elaborately by the Gaudiya Vaisnava writers elevating it to the level of rasa. Bhakti as rasa alone enables the spiritual adept to taste the nectar of the divine bliss which is the ultimate destiny of man. The author deserves all praise for the manner adopted for exposing different facts of the Gaudiya Vaisnava concept of Bhakti which is their unique contribution to the spiritual heritage of India.

I am sure the discerning readers interested in knowing more about the spiritual philosophy of the Gaudiya Vaisnava school, particularly bhakti that overwhelmed common people hankering for peace and happiness in their worldly life, and which inspired the production of devotional literature in North India in mediaeval period and the people in the west in the modern period, would profit from reading this small but valuable book by Dr. Gupta.

Calcutta

25th Dec., 1998

(Debabrate Sen Sharma)

Ex-Professor and Head

Sanskrit Deptt.

K.U., Kurukushetra

## Acknowledgements

Vaisnavism is a powerful religious movement that has influenced both directly and indirectly the entire range of religious literature from ancient to modern times. Its impact is clearly discernible in the medieval literature when the Vaisnava Saints started moving about and spreading the message of bhakti among the masses. Consequently, a number of Vaisnava thoughts grew up at different places in the country. In course of time, Mathura Vrndavana, the Vrajabhumi became a center of Bhakti movement in the Northern India.

Born in the family of devout Vaisnavas, I developed keen interest in the study of Vaisnava literature. After doing my post-graduation in Sanskrit, I decided to study Gaudiya Vaisnava system under the feet of Gaudiya Vaisnava Acaryas at Vrndabana. I also got an opportunity to take up my research work at the Institute of Oriental Philosophy, Vrndabana under the guidance of Late Dr. S.K.Maitra, the then research Professor at the Institute. However, due to sudden demise of my guide I had to discontinue my research work there. Later on, I got myself registered at the University of Kurukushetra and completed this work under the able guidance of Dr. D.B. Sen Sharma of Sanskrit Department.

Although a great deal of work on Gaudiya Vaisnavism in general, and life of Sri Caitanya, in particular, has been done but most of it is in Bengali script which is out of the reach of people not conversant with the language. Among the works, published in this School in English, mention may be made of Sisira Kumar Ghosh’s ‘Lord Gauranga’, Dr. Dinesh Cander Sen's Vaisnsva literature of Medieval Bengal', Caitanya and his Companions’, and ‘Caitanya and His Age’, Sir Jadunath Sircar’s Caitanya’s Life and Teachings’, Kennedy’s ‘The Caitanya Movement’, B.C. Pal’s 'Bengal Vaisnavism’, Prof. G.N. Mallik’s ‘Philosophy of Vaisnava Religion’, Dr. R.G. Bhandarkar’s ‘Vaisnavism, Saivism and Minor Religious System, Sh. Bhaktivinod Thakur’s ‘Caitanya Siksamrta’ and Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, His life and Precepts’, Swami Durga Caitanya Bharati’s Sri Gauranga - the Man, Prof. N.K. Sanyal’s ‘The Erotic Principle and Unalloyed Devotion, Dr. S.K. De’s ‘Doctrine of Avattara in Bengal Vaisnavism’, Dr. S.C. Mukherji’s ‘A study of Vaisnavism in ancient & Medieval Bengal'. They deal with a variety of subjects beginning from the life of Shri Caitanya, his message to metaphysical tenets of Gaudiya Vaisnavism. The most valuable and informative books on the subject, however, are Dr. S.K. De’s ‘Early History of Vaisnava Faith and Movement in Bengal’, Dr. S.C. Cakravarti’s ‘Philosophical Foundation of Bengal Vaisnavism’ and Dr. A.K. Majumdar’s Caitanya, His life and Doctrine, a study in Vaisnavism’. Dr. S.N. Das Gupta has contributed a chapter on Gaudiya Vaisnavism in his ‘A History of Indian Philosophy’ which gives a brief but valuable account of the main tenets of philosophy of Gaudiya Vaisnavism. Dr. Radha Krishnan and Dr. J.N. Sinha too, have dealt with Gaudiya philosophy briefly in their volumes on History of Philosophy. A large number of articles on different aspects of Gaudiya Vaisnavism have appeared in different journals as well as in studies in Philosophy from time to time. Special mention may be made of Dr. S.K. Maitra’s learned article on ‘Mukti’ and ‘Bhakti’ as highest values in Indian Philosophy and Culture Vrndavana, 111(1964) pp. 14-28, and a chapter on ‘Acintya-bheda-bheda vada’ published in the history of Philosophy, Eastern and Western’. The most learned and voluminous work on Gaudiya system in Dr. Radha Govinda Nath’s Gaudiya Vaisnava Darsana’, in 5 vols. in Bengali.

In all these works published earlier, study has been made of different aspects of Gaudiya Philosophy including the concept of Mukti and Bhakti, but in none of them, these concepts have been considered critically in the background of the concept of liberation and bhakti as has been expounded in other system of Indian Philosophy- (Orthodox, unorthodox, Vaisnava and non-Vaisnava). I have, therefore, taken up for a through and critical examination of the concept of Bhakti as means to God realisation as well as supreme end in-itself in the background of the concept of Mukti in all schools of Philosophy. I have thus, made a comparative study of the mode of sadhana laid down by different systems of Indian Philosophy while considering the supreme goal and the means to achieve this goal. It is true that a Sadhaka chooses the mode of sadhana in accordance with his inclination towards a particular ideal, but I have shown that the different ways do not necessarily lead a sadhaka to the same goal, and that the realisation leading the Ultimate Destination is not always the same.

Being a student of Sanskrit, I relied wholly on the original Sanskrit Texts for my study, but I have been greatly benefitted by the work of Dr. De’s ’Early History of Vaisnava Faith and Movement’, Dr. Chakravarti’s Philosophical Foundation of Bengal Vaisnavism’ and Dr. Majumdar’s Caitanya, His life and Doctrine, a study in Vaisnavism’, to whom I acknowledge my indebtedness. I have not consulted Bengali texts on the subject for obvious reasons. I am also, indebted to other authors mentioned above whose works I consulted freely.

An apology is due for my using Sanskrit terms freely in this work. I deliberately, of course, did not translate the technical terms at number of places as the Sanskrit technical terms have deeper significance which can not be adequately conveyed by English equivalents. It is more true of the religious terminology used by the Gaudiya writers.

It is my sacred duty to remember late Dr. S.K. Maitra, a great scholar and philosopher, former Professor on George-V Chair, Department of Philosophy, Calcutta University, from whom I received great inspiration and valuable guidance at the early stages of this research work.

It is my proud privilege to acknowledge my indebtedness to Dr. Dev Brata Sen Sharma, M.A., Ph.D., Professor, Department of Sanskrit, Kurukshetra University, Kurukshetra. He is a profound and unassuming scholar from whom I received not only valuable guidance but also constant inspiration without which the present work could not have seen the light of this day.

I do not find adequate words to express my deep sense of gratitude towards Late Bhaktimati Usha Ji Bhatnagar

M.A., who had been the perennial source of inspiration, guidance and encouragement throughout the sphere of my life and in the field of my education, spiritual and mundane both from the very beginning.

My acknowledgements are due to Prof. R.N.Sharma, Former Chairman, Deptt of Linguistics, and Kurukshetra University, Kurukushetra my favourite and affectionate teacher, for his inspiration, encouragement and active help in my research endeavours and specially in preparing the press copy of this book.

I am also very thankful to you Dr. A.L.Bhatt, Bhagavata Bhusana, a prominent scholar of Gaudiya philosophy presently living at Vrindavan for throwing light on aspects of Gaudiya metaphysics.

I can never forget to acknowledge my sincere thanks to dear Vipan Aggarwal, Chartered Accountant of Delhi, who took keen interest in getting this book published. Many others have made comments, raised questions and offered suggestions which helped me to shape the present work. I, therefore, feel it my pleasant duty to express my sincere thanks to all those who directly or indirectly helped me in completion of this investigation.

I am aware of my limitations and defects that might have crept in this work for which I can only crave the indulgence of my readers.

## Introduction

According to ancient tradition there were two parallel currents of religio-philosophical thinking that emanated from the Supreme God-Head and they were known as the Nigama and Agama. The former includes the Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas and Upanisads etc. and also the later orthodox schools of Indian philosophy that are based on them. The Agama current again, was divided under three heads, (i) the Vaisnava-agamas. (ii) The Sanskrit agamas, and (iii) The Saiva-agama. Both these currents lire said to be anadi, i.e. without beginning.

Historically speaking, however, these currents have d .i I able origin. The Vaisnava-current, for instance, is said to have developed from the Bhagavata-current, the latter being in existence in fourth century B.C. Dr. R.G. Bhandarakar in his book, "Vaisnavism, Saivism and Minor Religious System’, has referred to certain literary and archaeological evidences to show that the worshippers of Vasudeva, who were called Bhagavatas, existed about three or four century before Christ. The Narayaniya-section of Mahabharata gives detailed description of such religious worshippers of Narayana, Krsna, Vasudeva etc. and bhakti as their mode of worship, through certain stories. There is also an evidence of existence of vast Iiterature all over the country belonging to their particular school of thinking viz. Bhagavata Sampradaya. Pancaratras and Satvatas were two parallel streams of thought which enriched the Bhagavata-religion.

Vaisnava-current is relatively modern in the sense that this term is not found in the ancient literature. It is generally believed that Vaisnava-way of thinking developed out of Bhagavata-stream which includes Pancaratras, Ekantins and Satvatas etc. It is true that nothing can be said finally about this, as there is a great deal of controversy among scholars.

For the purpose of our present study, we take Ramanuja to be the starting point of the Vaisnava faith. Since it was he alone, “who integrated the Vedanta philosophy with the Pancaratra doctrine and initiated the massive Vaisnava movement of devotion, faith and philosophy” . It was he who raised the Vaisnava movement to the level of philosophy by writing scholarly work on the subject, full of dialectical reasons. He was followed by Nimbarka, Madhva and Vallabha. These four school of Vaisnava-religion came into existence. They propagated Visistadvaitavada. or qualified monism, Dvaitadvaitavada that is dualism-cum-monism Dvaitavada. i.e. dualism and Suddha-dvaita-vada, i.e. pure-monism, respectively.

The Gaudiya Vaisnavism though, a later development, has vast literature, which to begin with, was in Sanskrit and later in Bengali. The present work deals with the concept of Bhakti which was considered by the Gaudiya Vaisnavas as the sole means of attaining the supreme goal of life. The first chapter takes up the concept of Purusartha for examination. In the chapter entitled, "The Concept of Purusartha in Indian Philosophy" the meaning of the element artha in the compound purusartha has been discussed. Incidentally, the concept of anartha as distinct from artha has also been examined. It has been shown that the term artha as is regarded in the Sabara-basya on Jaimini’s sutra has different connotations when compared to artha in a generic sense and one of the four purusarthas as distinct from Dharma, Kama and Moksa. Thus the definition of artha in the subodhinitika on Sabarabhasya implies that which is sukhadhikaduhkhajanaktvam i.e. whatever does not cause more pain than pleasure is Artha. This definition, however, applies to Nyaya and Samkhya view of moksa as absolute freedom from pleasure (sukha) and pain (duhkha).

The nature of four purusarthas, viz., Dharma, Artha. Kama and Moksa have been critically examined. The subject matter of all the purusarthas has been dealt with their relative importance as being primary and secondary ends of life. Lastly, the concept of Moksa in different schools of Indian Philosophy has been discussed in the next chapter.

In the chapter entitled, “Moksa (salvation) in Indian Philosophy”, a critical examination of the different conception of Moksa would show that these conception can broadly be classified under two heads, viz., one accepting the existence of a permanent soul, while other, denying the same. Those who believe in the existence of a permanent soul postulate theories of Moksa different from those who deny the existence of a permanent soul. Again, the group accepting the permanent soul have conceived Moksa either as positive state or as negative state in keeping with their philosophical views.

The Buddhists deny the existence of a permanent soul, hence, their theory of Nirvana has been examined first.

As regards the group, accepting the existence of a permanent soul, it has been shown that the negativistic views of those, e.g. Nyaya-Vaisesikas, Samkhya and Yoga, are not satisfactory. As such, a negative conception of the state of Moksa cannot be regarded as a desirable end to be pursued. Further, the conception of Moksa in the Advaita Vedanta of Sankara which is a positive conception has been discussed next. It has been shown that the Advaita view is also inconsistent in as much as, it leads to the complete identification of the self with the Absolute. It also goes against the authority of the scriptures, according to which, the self, in the state of moksa is not at all, identified or lost in Brahman, the absolute. This, however, leads to the examination of the conception of Mukti according to the Vaisnavites such as Ramanujists, Madhvites, Vallabhites and Nimbarkist, which is a higher and wider conception when compared to that of other systems.

“The conception of Mukti in the theistic schools of Vedanta” (i.e. Vaisnavites) has been taken up for discussion in the next chapter. As the conception of Mukti in different theistic schools cannot be fully understood without their metaphysical ground, hence the metaphysics of the different theistic schools has been briefly mentioned, especially the concept of soul, its continuance even in the state of Moksa its being of limited nature and its dependence on the personality of the God-head.

Coming to our analysis of the different kinds of Mukti admitted by these theistic schools, it has been found that all the five varieties of mukti has been accepted by all of them. Coming to the Gaudiya school, we have found that they also, speak of five kinds of mukti of which, sayujya mukti has been badly deprecated by them as there being no occasion of serving the Lord in any possible way. It has been shown that the Gaudiyas in fact, do not attach much importance to all these varieties of mukti. This is because, they propagate much higher value of life, viz., Bhakti or the love of the Lord as the supreme end of life. It has been fully discussed in the next chapter.

In the chapter entitled, “Prema-Bhakti As Pancama Purusartha”, It has been shown how the joy of mukti pale asunder as compared to the joy of Bhakti or love of God. It has been illustrated by citing examples from the Bhakti Sandarbha and the Bhagavata Purana and Priti Sandarbha. The Supremacy of Bhakti over Moksa, the fourth end of human life has been shown fully and this however, leads to know about the nature of Bhakti as it is, and this has been taken up in the next chapter.

An attempt has been made to trace the origin and development of the cult of Bhakti in the chapter entitled, “The Concept of Bhakti According to the Theistic Schools of Vedanta”. The origin has been traced right from the Vedas to Sutra - literature and the Bhagavata Purana. This enabled us to make a clear distinction of the cult of Bhakti as has been conceived and developed by the four major schools of Vaisnava thought. Bhakti has been regarded, according to these systems, as only a handmaid of Mukti. Further, it has been pointed out that Bhakti is always mixed with Jnana-Karma and Yoga etc., in all these systems. Though, they also hold Bhakti as superior to Jnana and Karma etc. but it has been conceived as an instrument for the achievement of Mukti only. This view has been severely criticised by the Gaudiyas on the ground that they could not raise bhakti to such an extent that it could be pure and undefiled.

From above, we have found that the Ramanujists and Madhvites seem to follow the Vaidhi - type of Bhakti, while Nimbarkists and Vallabhites lay a greater emphasis on Rnganuga-way of Bhakti. The doctrine of Pustimarga according to Vallabhites has been examined in detail. Though all these systems consider Mukti to be the highest end of life, where Bhakti stands only as means to that end, Vallabhites however, appear to have broken the orthodox Vaisnava tradition to some extent. This is probably due to indirect influence of Sri Caitanya - (his contemporary). It is significant to note that the views of Vallabha on Bhakti as developed in Pusti-Marga bear a close resemblance to those of the Gaudiyas. The phalarupa Bhakti of the Nimbarka-school has been mentioned in this chapter which bears similarity with the Prema- Bhakti of the Gaudiyas.

The Gaudiya conception of Bhakti which forms the subject matter of the present work has been taken up separately in the chapter entitled “Concept of Bhakti in Gaudiya Vaisnavism”. This discussion opens with the concept of Uttama-Bhakti as given by Sri Rupa-Goswami. Sri Jiva-Goswami has elaborated this concept by describing its different varieties, which have been mentioned in detail. Sadhana-Bhakti and Sadhya-Bhakti has been discussed in detail with their sub-division of (1) Vaidhi and Raganuga, and (2) Bhava and Prema -bhakti along with their further varieties. Of all these varieties, Premabhakti is the highest one. It is the culminating point of one’s sadhana. The sadhaka attaining the stage of Premabhakti assumes an eternal form of serving the Lord, according to his mode of worship.

The chapter, entitled, “The Concept of Bhakti as Rasa, According to the Gaudiyas” deals with the contribution of the Gaudiyas on the theory of rasa, as a religion. Prior to the Gaudiyas, we find that the conception of Bhakti as a means or sadhana has been highly developed by the other Vaisnava schools, similarly, the conception of rasa has been equally developed by the school of Alankarikas. But the two lines have remained separate, i.e. neither the Bhakti school, nor the alankarikas have ever thought that Bhakti has anything of rasa in it. The Gaudiyas, however, may be said to have opened altogether fresh grounds by amalgamating the two separate trends by formulating the conception of Bhakti as rasa. Different theories regarding the locus of rasa have also been critically examined from the point of view of the Gaudiyas. Then, the question of enumeration of Rasa has been dealt with. The five primary Bhakti rasas have been explained in detail with their attendant feelings, while seven secondary rasas find passing reference only. Further,

Madhura Bhakti Rasa being the most important among all the rest, has been explained with various characteristics and stages of development of madhura-rati into sneha, mana, pranaya etc.

In the concluding chapter, the sadhaka's upward journey after transcending the mundane plane-has been described on the basis of Brhad-Bhagvatamrtam. The different Lokas that lie beyond Viraja the Karana-samudra up to the Krsna-loka or Goloka has been described briefly.

Further, the conception of Manjari which is the special contribution of the Gaudiyas has also been mentioned in brief.

## Chapter I - The Concept of Purusartha in Indian Philosophy

Indian philosophy differs from both ancient Greek philosophy and modern Western Philosophy in its approach to the perennial philosophical problems. While both Greek and Modern Western Philosophy investigate philosophical problems in a purely intellectual perspective, the end being philosophical knowledge of Reality for its own sake. Indian philosophy is not concerned with knowledge for the sake of knowledge. According to the Indian point of view, knowledge is not worth acquiring unless it serves the basic interest of life and facilitates man’s proper adjustment to reality. Therefore, all Indian philosophy starts with an examination of the basic values that govern the aspiration of mankind. A preliminary enquiry into the Purusarthas or the values of life is thus a necessary presupposition of all philosophical inquires in Indian philosophical tradition.

Now, before we try to dwell on the ends that are consciously pursued by man, it will be better if we first try to analyse the nature of an end as a possible object of pursuit. Such an attempt, we find, has been made by the Indian philosophers by trying to define Artha i.e., an object of desire. It is, no doubt, the fact that a man pursues many ends, but the question is what makes such ends desirable or pursuable on the face of it? What appears is that the objects are such that they are desirable in themselves and any further question in this matter is redundant? But it is not so; for example, we find that any object that causes only pain to us is not desired. Or, in other words, none of the ends which one chooses should be such as would produce only pain. This means therefore, that there are some basic conditions which determine the nature of the ends as possible objects of our choice.

The first attempt to define Artha i.e. an object of pursuit, has been made by Vatsyayana - the commentator on the Nyaya-Sutra of Gautama. An Artha is defined by him as either pleasure or pain and whatever is the cause of the either\[3\]. The pleasure is artha in the sense that everybody desires to attain it. Pain is also artha, in the sense that everybody desires to get rid of it. The one is artha as heya or something desired or desirable, the other is artha as heya or something undesirable and unwanted. The cause of pleasure is also artha as instrumental in the attainment thereof. The more precise designation would be the naming of pleasure as upadeya and therefore, an artha, and of pain as heya and therefore, anartha, and the cause of pleasure and pain would thus, have instrumental significance as instruments of Artha and Anartha. Therefore, from Vatsyayana’s point of view, we have either intrinsic values or disvalues and also instrumental values or disvalues.

It may be remarked here, however, that the definition given of artha which occurs first in the Gautama’s Nyaya-Sutra does not square with the Nyaya-view of Paramapurus artha or Moksa - which is defined in negative terms as complete and absolute freedom from all kinds of pain. Since, according to Nyaya, pleasure being inseparably related to pain must be treated as pain by the Mumuksu or the seeker of liberation. Moksa as paramapurusartha is thus a state of absolute freedom from pain as well as pleasure. How can then, Moksa be a paramapurusartha? Paramapurusartha must be artha of one kind or other. And where there is neither pain nor pleasure i.e. artha in the positive or negative sense as in the Nyaya conception of Moksa or liberation, it cannot, strictly speaking, be called as artha at all.

Before we go into a detailed exposition of Artha, we must first distinguish two senses in which the word artha has been used by Indian Philosophers. In the first place, the word Artha appears as an element of the compound word Purusartha; and Purusartha, again, is described as consisting of Dharma, Artha, Kama and Moksa, in which Artha appears again as one of the four purusarthas distinct from Dharma, Kama and Moksa ns the other three purusarthas. If, therefore, the latter three are arthas as being purusarthas, they are not purusarthas in the sense of the first which is artha as distinct from Dharma, Kama and Moksa. Therefore, we must distinguish between the generic sense of Artha which applies to all the four purusarthas and the use of Artha in a specific sense which applies to the artha only among the four purusarthas and not to Dharma, Kama mid Moksa.

A definition of artha as given in Subodhinitika on Sabara Bhasva thus applies to Artha as it occurs in the compound purusarthas and not to Artha in the specific sense. The Subodhinitika defines artha as what is “sukhadhika-duhkajanakatvam”. In other words, whatever does not cause more pain than pleasure is artha. This is a negative definition. But, despite being negative, it has a merit of being very comprehensive, applying as it does, to all the four varieties of purusarthas viz., Dharma, Artha, Kama and Moksa. According to this view, therefore, a state of freedom from pleasure as well as from pain is artha too. For, here, there being neither pleasure nor pain, that is, there is also no excess of pain over pleasure. Further a state in which the pain is exactly equal to pleasure is also artha. Thirdly, a state, in which pleasure exceeds the amount of pain is also artha. Fourthly, a state of pure pleasure is that where pain is also one in which there is no excess of pain over pleasure. It can also however, be derived from above that when the pain exceeds the pleasure produced, or where pain alone is what is experienced and no pleasure whatsoever, it is called anartha or negation of artha. It may be noted that Mimaihsaka’s definition of Artha described above, has the advantage over Nyaya in providing scope for different grades of values and disvalues. The above definition of artha provides room for Moksa or liberation as an artha either in the Nyaya or Sainkhya sense of a state of complete freedom from pleasure as well as from pain; and also, for the Advaitaand Bhattaconception of Moksa as a state of unalloyed eternal bliss (i.e. niratisayanandasvarupatvam or nityasukhabhivyakti). In other words, the Mimamsakas definition makes room for ordinary values of life where, despite the presence of pain, pleasure, exceeds the pain involved and also Parama Purus artha in the sense of Moksa or liberation where either as, according to Nyaya and Sarhkhya, there is neither any pain nor any pleasure, or where, as according to the Advaita Vedanta, the state of that of unalloyed eternal bliss, or as according to the Mimamsakas, one of abhivyakti or manifestation of eternal pleasure that lies dormant in the self in its empirical state.

In this context, we may also refer to anartha or the negation of artha. It, no doubt, logically follows that the very opposite of Artha will be an Anartha and no separate determination of the latter is necessary. That is to say, that a thing which produces more pain than pleasure is Anartha. It will, of course, be an Anartha if, it causes only pain, since pain, in this case, exceeds pleasure. Considered in this light, the Buddhist conception of everything as fraught with pain, means that everything is an Anartha. That is, nothing can be considered as a fit object of pursuit. Just as, Arthas are fit ends of our life, so the Anarthas cannot be regarded as such. Now if, according to the Buddhist ‘sarvam hi duhkham’ i.e. all is pain. That is to say, if there is no such thing as pleasure, then, what can be the position of man as he is faced with anarthas only and has no proper ends for his pursuit? The only course open to the Buddhist is a negative attitude. As there is no Artha for him, he cannot strive after anything which is a proper or fit object for his pursuit. And in as much as, he is laced with only Anarthas, his attitude should be one of universal non-assent, or in other words, he will find himself in a position of rejecting everything as his end. Such a voluntaristic nihilism by itself is however, meaningless. Since, when one has Arthas as also Anarthas, he can reject the anartha and choose the Artha But, when he is faced only with Anarthas, his endeavour becomes quite insignificant for want of any Art ha’ which he can select as against the Anarthas. Thus, it follows that a total rejection of Artha and acceptance of everything as Anartha is not logically tenable. The question of Anartha can be significantly raised only against the background of positive values viz., Arthas, otherwise the position of pure Anartha will be no other than that of mere negation without a positive basis.

In fact, absolute skepticism is self-destroying as ‘Citsukha’ has shown. Consider, for example, the skeptical statement, ‘nothing is real'. If the statement itself is unreal and involved in the sceptic statement, then the statement being unreal, it follows that ‘nothing is real’ cannot be true. Thus, from our consideration of Artha and Anartha, we have come to the conclusion that before we can accept anything as an end of life, we must satisfy ourselves that the same is an Artha or something that at least, does not cause more pain than pleasure, and even Moksa which is regarded as the supreme end of our life is not exempted from such a test. Secondly, none can face his life at the admission of everything as Anartha, since the rejection of everything as Anartha will sap at the very root of all endeavour and acts of will for want of any positive Artha to pursue. If there is Anartha only as the Buddhists claim, then life would be a stalemate.

From the above definition of Artha, it appears that the approach of Indian Philosophy towards life is a positive one. It is based on the acceptance of ends of life which are at least, not more painful than pleasing, and consequently pursuable. The Buddhist school is the only exception in this respect. It accepts nothing as valuable. Its starting point is one of universal dis-valuation or rejection of everything as Anartha as ensuring in an excess of pain over pleasure.

From our discussions about the nature of Artha and Anartha, it has become evident that man’s life can only be significantly understood in terms of pursuit of Arthas or positive ends. If on the other hand, it be basically assumed that life has no positive ends to be pursued as there are no ‘Arthas’ or everything is an Anartha, even the effort to put an end to the Anarthas cannot be logically regarded as an end worthy of pursuit. Since ex hypothesis, according to the Buddhist, “everything is an Anartha” being of the nature of pain or duhkha even the cessation of Anartha must also be an Anartha. In other words, from the Buddhistic stand point, no end can be significantly pursued. In fact, the Buddhist-view is expressed in the words that whatever is, is of the nature of pain or duhkhareduces the cessation of duhkha or pain to a kind of duhkha or pain and thus makes such cessation not an end to be desired. Thus Buddhist view culminates in complete paralysis of all human endeavour. It thus, refutes itself, or in other words, it is self-destroying.

Hence, the general Indian outlook is opposed to a pessimistic view of human existence but is rather optimistic as it is in favour of the pursuit of positive ends such as, the triad of Dharma, Artha and Kama on the worldly plane. It however, proposes a supreme end on the higher plane which it calls Moksa or liberation as in fact, the supreme end in the sense of transcendence of all ordinary ends of life which are all mixed up with undesirable consequences and frustrations. Only there seems to have been some confusion regarding the definition of Moksa or the ultimate end of life (viz., Parama Purusartha). This will become gradually clear as we shall try to define the different ends of Purusarthas which a man is found to pursue.

In Vedic Kalpa-Sutraswe find a threefold classification of the Purus arthas or the ends pursued by man. This is expressed by the epithet ‘Trivarga’ meaning, the group of three consisting of the three gurus arthas viz., Dharma (righteousness), Artha (Economic values), and Kama (carnal gratification). Here, the first of the three purusartha, viz., Dharma or righteousness has been given the foremost place, the other two, occupying a subordinate position. It is not, however, clear whether Moksa or the fourth purusartha was taken as included in Dharma or it was not recognized at all, at the time.Though we miss here, a clear formulation of the final end of life i.e. Parama Purusartha, we are left in no mood of uncertainty as to the inherent superiority of Dharma to Artha and Kama. As M. Hiriyanna refers to the supremacy of Dharma in his ‘an outlines of Indian Philosophy’. According to him, Dharma is under all circumstances be preferred. He that adheres to Dharma, says Apastamba, reaps worldly benefits also, but if he does not, it matters little, for the attainment of the dharma is the supreme aim". The same fact is also stated by Sivaswamy Aiyer in his Involution of Hindu Moral Ideals’ in the following words, "Dharma, though secondary to the ultimate end of Moksa, has acquired a predominance in relation to the other ends”. The superiority of Dharma to the other two ends is also emphasised in the Ramayana. The Bhagavad Gita text, “dharmavirudho bhutesu, kamosmi Bharatarsabha”i.e., in beings, I am the sexual desire not in conflict with virtue or righteousness. Scriptural injunctions support the same truth of supremacy of Dharma over Kama.

In the Mahabharataagain we find the superiority of Dharma over Kama and Artha. The verse says - “I cry with arm uplifted, yet none heedeth, from righteousness (Dharma) flow forth pleasure and profit. Why then do ye not follow righteousness”? Here in, Dharma is stated as the cause of emanation of other two ends viz., Artha and Kama. The implication of bestowing such an importance to Dharma however, is that though Artha and Kama are regarded as legitimate values, yet they are to be cultivated in subordination to Dharma and are to be rejected whenever, be found to clash with the latter. This is to give assent to the fact that man’s life is on the whole, dominated or ruled by Dharma.

The superiority of Dharma over Artha and Kama however, does not mean that the latter have no place at all in the practice of Dharma. The very fact that these are reckoned as separate Purus arthas goes against such an assumption. As subordinate Purusartha these may be taken to occupy an instrumental place in relation to Dharma which is considered to be the supreme end. The other alternative is to suppose that the subordinate or secondary purusarthas are themselves independent values, only these can be sought in so far as they do not conflict with the practice of Dharma. But, a third possibility is not altogether excluded, which means that the subordinate purusarthas or values of life are partly independent and partly instrumental in relation to dharma. The conception regarding Artha and Kama as ends are quite common and are generally agreed upon. But the position is quite different with the notion of Dharma, which has been taken in widely different senses by different authorities; and unless we can clear up the difference of opinions regarding the concept, we cannot hope to get fruitful result on the basis of the suggested possibilities regarding the inter-relation between the different Purusarthas determining the Indian outlook in general. The various conception of Dharma in the different system of Hindu Philosophy, are briefly stated in Rama Krishna’s Siddhantacandrika (commentary on Parthasarathimisras Sastradipika) as follows: samkhyastumanaso vrttivisesamdharmamahuhsakyastu cittasya subhamvasanaih, arhatastu karyarambhakan suksman murtimatrapudgalam dharmamahuh, viseikastu atmano visesagunam.

mimamsakastu ekadesinah apurvameva dharmamahuh, yagadireva dharmas'abdavacy am iti bhattah, that is to say, Dharma or righteousness signifies a specific function of the mind for the Samkhyas an auspicious disposition of the mental continuum, according to the Sakyas, (Buddhist) for the Arhathas or Jains, certain subtle forces in atoms as the causes of specific consequences or effects, while, according to the Vaisesikas, certain specific qualities in the atman but for the one school of the Mimamsakas (i.e. of the Prabhakaras) a transcendental energy or power, for the Bhattas, (the school of Kumarila), the sacrificial acts and the other ceremonies.

But none of the above five different senses in which the term Dharma is conceived in the different schools of Indian Philosophy, is conceived in a manner so as to include the caturtha varga or the fourth purusartha viz., Moksa as the parama-purusartha. In the Manusmrti however, we have Dharma conceived as that enables one to attain not merely worldly prosperity i.e. Abhyudava but also Nihsreyasah or the Summum Bonum. It is not clear however, even from the Vaisesika Sutrai.e. “yato abhyudayanihs'ryasah siddhih sa eva dharma” -whether nihsreyasah means the highest moral perfection or Moksa as the transcendence of all bondage. The difficulty became even greater, when later, the concept of Moksa was added to the trivarga, as the fourth and final purusartha. This eventually led to the replacement of the notion of trivarga by that of caturvarga which has ever since come to dominate Indian thought. Though, the position of Dharma was not compromised in relation to Artha and Kama or the fourth purusartha that came to occupy the supreme position so much so that the Trivarga including Dharma came to be relegated to the relative and mundanesphere, while Moksa was separated there from; as belonging to the supra-mundane sphere of absolute freedom. In trivarga, there was no place for any transcendence of earthly life and its interests. The entire system of purusarthas constituting the trivarga exclusively, to the achievements either in this world or hereafter in heaven, but there was no idea of getting beyond the range of what is worldly or a glorified repetition of the worldly. The idea of heaven was just a duplication of the worldly in terms of only the bright side of the world minus its dark features. In other words, the idea was that of a life of sense satisfaction or carnal gratification either here on this earth or in a better world hereafter.

The conception of Moksa marks a departure from the old pattern given by trivarga. Both the worldly and the heavenly lives are considered to be the states of bondage for the soul. But the supreme achievement of man, is therefore, considered to consist in a state of Moksa or release from that of bondage to earthly interests. Thus in fact, the state of Moksa was conceived as a complete outgrowing of our worldly existence and thereby being free for ever from the cycles of births, deaths and rebirths. Whatever the actual position of such a supra-mundane state may be like, has, no doubt, been debated upon by the different schools of thought, but there is a consensus of opinions that Moksa is a kind of denial of our worldly being and all that it involves. It may be worth noting here that this negative attitude to the flesh is no specialty of Indian Thought, Christianity as a religion considers crucifixion of flesh as Sine-qua-non of the beginning of the life of the spirit. In the concept of liberation or Moksa this negative attitude-fall that appertains to the flesh and earthly life) is emphasised as essential for the realisation of the highest end.

With the introduction of the fourth and the supreme purusartha, the problem of inter relation between the different purusarthas becomes primarily a question regarding the relation between mundane and the supra-mundane, the trivarga forming the mundane side and moksa - the fourth of the varga falling to the other side.It is no longer a question of regulating the claim of Artha and Kama under the laws of Dharma, but one of adjusting I In claims of the tried of Dharma, Artha and Kama relatively to Moksa-the supreme purusartha. Evidently, there is no longer, the question of setting the claims of purusarthas each by itself, or in groups, since with the admission of Moksa as the supreme purusartha, the rest of the purusarthas automatically come to occupy a subordinate place in relation to the former and these can be discussed only as subordinately related to the former. Or in other words, all the four purusarthas come to form a unit, which, in fact, is the idea conveyed by the term caturvarga.

The overall position now come to this, Moksa is the supreme Purusartha which is the dominating principle amongst the four, while the trivarga is dominated by Dharma. Hence it is no longer possible to confine ourselves to the discussion of the triverga by itself i.e. without its relation to the fourth and final purusartha.

It may be mentioned in passing that though, the idea of Moksa as the final purusartha is common amongst the school of Indian Philosophy in general, it has no doubt, been rejected by the Carvakas. According to the latter, the soil is identical with psycho-physical organism which is completely disintegrated in death, accordingly, I here is no question of transmigration and rebirth. There being no rebirth, the only bondage is that of the present body, hence there is no other moksa than the death or I lie release from the body itself, if such a high sounding word can be significantly applied to such a state at all. This view of Carvaka is more easily denounced then logically refuted. To the Indian mind, the proposition like the existence of the soul or at least the laws of karma and rebirth are nothing less than self-evident. The arguments that have been adduced to by one school or other from time to time, are not so much for establishing those propositions as to demolish the claims of nonbelievers. The Indian mind bred in the Vedic tradition as it is, has scarcely given any serious thought over the Carvakas i.e. the materialistic alternative, otherwise reasonably considered the latter position appears as a formidable opponent to the confident pursuers of Moksa that have not been able to engage the serious attention of the scientific mind which in fact, dominate the present day world. The issue raised by the materialistic or the non-believer in the soul or in the doctrine of Karma, cannot be so easily dismissed as most of the Indian Philosophers, as also the religious and idealist philosophers of the West are won’t to think. This is quite unfortunate that large issues are decided on faith which is another name for prejudice, while quite minor ones are openly debated upon and engaged most of our logical brain. Those who are from the very beginning prone to such faiths, as most Indians are, however find no difficulty with the notions of the eternal soul or the theory of rebirth, but for people who lack such faith, the latter has proved to be too bitter pill to swallow. The question of heaven or hell as implied in the notion of Dharma itself falls flat on the logico-empirical minds of the modern man. The possibility of Moksa which can be understood only in supra-logical and spiritual term makes almost an impossible demand on the modern intelligence. The Carvakas in fact, are prepared to accept only two purusarthas viz., Artha and Kama and rejecting the other two as incapable of empirical verification though, they loom so large in the eyes of the propounders of the caturvarga. Man, being a sensuous being, cannot but, own the satisfaction of the senses i.e. Kama as his ends. So is the case with economic value which is a necessary means to his carnal gratification. These in fact, are the only two values which every man holds so dear to his heart. But beyond these two, that is, in respect of Dharma and Moksa, it cannot be said to be on such sure grounds.

It is almost a paradox that while Indian philosophy discusses Moksa in great detail and each school enters into the refutation of the view of other schools in this regard stressing its own view as the only one that answers to all the relevant conditions of the case, there is no attempt anywhere to show how far it is possible for any human being either significantly to pursue this Purusartha or end in life and realise it in experience, in any form. The traditional Indian way, however has been to accept these purusarthas on the testimony of the scriptures or the Srutis and to develop and systematize the concept by putting together the different elements of the concepts so far as they can be gathered from the statements of the scriptures. There are also certain rationalistic schools like Samkhya and Bauddha etc., who have no doubt, tried to develop the concept mainly on rational grounds. We shall however, deal with this point in detail in connection with the analysis of the concept as given by the different schools of thought. For the present, let us revert back to the question of interrelation between the different purusarthas.

The subject matter of the Purusarthas can be dealt with either from the point of view of the distinction between ultimate or primary ends and instrumental or secondary ends, or from the point of view of relative importance of each as compared with the rest. Taking the first alternative, we find that according to some authorities, the third and the fourth ends i.e. Kama and Moksa have been regarded as primary ends or ends in themselves and the first two viz., Dharma and Artha as secondary or subsidiary ends as instrumental to the realization of the primary ends. That is to say, that Kama and Moksa are desired for their own sake, while Dharma and Artha desired not for themselves but as means for the attainment of Kama or Moksa. According to this view, both Dharma and Artha are means to the attainment of sensuous enjoyment or the satisfaction of the senses both in this world and hereafter. By performing the duties, enjoined by the Vedas, i.e. by adhering to Dharma, one earns money as also religious merit or punya. By utilization of money one can get sensuous satisfaction by enjoying the objects thus obtained and by acquiring punya one gets the pleasure of heaven in lives to come. It is also clear how Artha is instrumental in procuring the sensuous enjoyments in the world as well as the joys that are possible in a life here after in heaven or svarga.

It is not however, quite obvious that how far Dharma is conducive to the attainment of Moksa or liberation which is the Parama Purus artha or the highest end. It may be noted, however that while Dharma, in the sense of varnasrama duties which include the Vedic sacrifices as obligatory for the grhastha or householder is regarded as conducive to the merit that matures into the enjoyment of heavenly bliss in the life hereafter, but does not ensure liberation both according to Nyava and Samkhva as also the Advaita school of the Vedanta. According to Ramanuja however, dharma is an essential element in the absolute life which is liberated life that has overcome the bondage of earthly interest. In fact, according to the Ramanuiists, the due performance of the duties of life is obligatory in all the asramas including the jyoti-state in which one has become absolutely free from the bondage of the flesh. The respect in which these are means to Moksa is not so evident as we find it in the case of their being means to Kama. Moksa being considered to be the supreme end, stands in its own light as the end and the question of its being made subservient to the realization of other ends is quite absurd. We have only to be satisfied of the fact that whatever conception of Moksa may be placed before us, must be Purusartha in the first place and must be- higher than all other purusarthas. Now supposing that Moksa is a supreme desirable end, we can say that Dharma and Artha though, not directly utilised for Moksa, are yet helpful indirectly by the engendering conditions for ‘cittasuddhi’ or the purification of the mind-which is the necessary condition for practicing Jnana, Bhakti or Yoga which may be conceived as immediate means to the realisation of the highest end. In this sense, both Dharma and Artha are indirect means to the realisation of Moksa. Though Kama has been regarded as an end in itself, it should however, be clearly borne in mind that it is only so related to our mundane life, and it is no better than a secondary and when compared to Moksa - which is the trans-mundane supreme end of life; that is, in comparison to Moksa, not even Dharma or Kama can be regarded as an end-in-itself worth pursuing for its own account. It is even sometimes said that Kama or sensuous enjoyment is antithetical to the attainment of Moksa being a source of bondage to the flesh. Hence it cannot, in any sense, be regarded as a means to the attainment of Moksa. Kama (lust) only breeds more lust that fires or kindles or inflames the desires of the flesh, and it thus, a polar opposite to the state of liberation.

Some however, think that even sensuous satisfaction may have a legitimate place in the life of absolute freedom or liberation. The sense appetites, left to themselves, grow by indulgence and make the individual a slave to the cravings of the flesh, but the appetites moderately indulged and regulated by reason are a help rather than a hindrance to the realisation of the absolute life. Also, if these sensuous desires be repressed outright, the results will be quite unhelpful as regards the attainment of Moksa and this will create worse confusions in the mind.

In fact, the modern psychology tells us that undue repression of the natural appetite generates complexes and accounts for mental breakdown in numerous instances. Hence the proper course is not the repression of cravings of the flesh, but its harnessing in the service, of the spirit. This is however, the view only of some Indian thinkers, the majority holding that flesh and its cravings have no place in the absolute life which is Moksa or liberation from all earthly interests. It had been enjoined in the Isopanisadthat it is only by enjoying life for a hundred years in a spirit of dedication to the Supreme Reality that one can get rid of the bondage of karma. The real idea that lies in this statement is that a man’s mind is full of impurities, it is full of hankering after Artha and all sorts of sensuous objects. Now such a state of mind is quite unfit for taking up any course for Moksa, but if he regulates his desires in accordance with the injunctions of the scriptures, they can soon come under control and will generate such conditions in his mind which are favourable to the attainment of Moksa. Thus, we find that pravrttilaksana dharma allowing regulated gratification of the senses also makes one fit for such practices as it brings on one’s liberation or Moksa in the long run. Thus, though, in respect of his brute nature one is not gratified for the state of liberation, one may, yet attain the necessary fitness for qualifying for Moksa by subduing and regulating the, raw material of impulses and appetites that constitute his brute nature.

Next turning to the question of relative importance of the different purusarthas, we find that Moksa, as we have already seen, is regarded as the highest end to which every other end is subordinate and functions as a means thereto. Likewise Artha and Kama are taken directly as earth bound and are not directly serviceable to the end of Moksa, though, their indirect instrumentality is not altogether denied at least, by some Indian thinkers. As to Dharma, we have already stated that it is also a means to Moksa, nevertheless, it occupies a regulative position in respect of other two purusarthas. The first place has been assigned to Dharma , the significance of which is that the mundane life of man should be guided by Dharma or moral principles and that it can be serviceable to the highest ideal of life i.e., Moksa. Human life when left to itself is impulse-dominated, and it is in principle no better than that of the animal. Dharma or the regulative law of life has therefore, been made compulsory for everybody so that he may surely rise above the pull of the animal life and make himself fit for the pursuit of the highest end of life i.e., Moksa. Dharma in whatever sense it is understood, implies the regulation of the primitive urges. If it means virtues or righteousness, it is based on the regulation of our selfish desires by ethical standards. At other times, it is taken as the spiritual merit or demerit acquired through the performance of sacrificial rites and duties, still idea of regulation is there. When it is taken as the performance of duties as enjoined by scriptures, the idea of regulation of the lower self is uppermost. Dharma is defined as “that which holds”. It is only desires that let us down, while the regulation of desires according to rational principles, upholds us, or that Dharma protects us (dharmo raksito raksitah). If a man abides by the law of Dharma, he is saved by the same dharma. There is, so to say, no other savior than the Dharma. Self regulation as we have seen, is life while self-gratification is death. Now, the spirit of self-regulation finds its fullest expression in the form of laws by means of which the unruly desires are brought under control. For want of the regulative principles of Dharma, only two courses would be there, one of self-gratification, and other self-destruction. The first course would confine us to the animal level for good. Some would like the second course, but certainly, that is, not the way to true life, since, it would destroy the very spring of life and thereby destroy life also. The only course to uphold life and to raise it, therefore lies in the principles of regulation of desires or as it is said, Dharma alone can uphold life. Now before life can be upheld, the realization of the ends of life including Moksa will be no better than a dream. Thus dharma is not only the first purusartha in point of preference, but its importance in the mundane plane which is dominated by manifold desires is supreme. Just as, the law of Moksa rules supreme in the sphere of all the four purusarthas, so also dharma dominates in that of the first three. Though the Dharmasastras have recognized all the four purusarthas, The Moksasastras, however, seem to recognize Moksa as the only end, worthy of pursuit by man, the other purusarthas being considered as unsatisfactory in so many ways. For example, in Samkhayasastra, it has been argued that no pleasure whether earthly or heavenly can be considered as ultimately satisfying, since, it cannot destroy pain or Anartha totally and unconditionally. That alone can be therefore, considered Parama-purus artha or Moksa where there is a total and unconditional absence of anartha or pain. That is, all other so called purusarthas are partly fraught with pain, accordingly, they cannot be considered as the Parama-purus artha. It is only the unenlightened or the avivekins who delight in the pseudo-purusarthas, and as a result, are denied even a glimpse of the supreme purusartha. It cannot be gainsaid that Moksa is the supreme purusartha, and all other purusarthas lack the super excellence possessed by it. The other purusarthas are not free from pain in the absolute sense, neither they are ends by themselves in as much as they all serve as means to Moksa which is the only true end. Hence the purusarthas included in the trivarga, appear as true purusarthas till they are brought under the scheme of caturvarga including Moksa. Or in other words, we can say that the trivarga is purusartha for the worldly minded men, but in the eyes of the mumuksu or the aspirant of liberation, none of the trivarga appear as a true purusartha which is worthy of pursuit. It is true that the trivarga is purusartha in the sense they don’t cause more pain than pleasure, but they are not altogether free from an admixture of pain. This only means that as arthas these are pursuable, but when compared to the ideal of Moksa these fall into insignificance.

It is to be noted that the concept of absolute life conceived either as Nirvana (as in Buddhism) or as Moksa or liberation (as in the orthodox-Hindu system) or as a life of Bhakti or devotion (as in Vaisnavism) presupposes a prior state which may be called the empirical life or (literally samsara) or transmigration from one embodied life to another, none of which yields the desired satisfaction which everybody seeks the absolute life is considered to be one which is quite different from this unsatisfactory empirical life-a life in which, according to all Indian systems, all our miseries mid without possibilities of recurrence and which, according to some Indian system, is a blessed life, that is a life of eternal blissfulness and joy. The question thus, arises how is this empirical life can be so ended that one becomes forever free from all its accompanying evils. It is obvious that in our embodied life we are closely bound-up with a physical universe of material objects. Now, if this universe be one rigidly determined by the laws of cause and effect, the question of breaking through its chains does not arise. One can escape from the whole of the physical universe if it be not an absolutely determined unbreakable system. But this is a debatable question if the universe be ruled by the caused necessity or be a universe of loose unrelated occurrences, the question of attaining one’s freedom from its hold will not arise. A universe of loose unrelated atoms will be a universe in which everything will occur by sheer chance so that whatever effort one may put forth for attaining freedom from such a universe, one can never be sure whether it will come at all. And the same holds also of a universe of absolute necessity in which one will find oneself destined to live forever at the mercy of an inexorable fate. There is however, a way out of the impasse. We may consider our bondage to nothing but a product of avidya (nescience) or ignorance in which .case freedom may be possible through the cancellation of nescience or avidya by vidya or true knowledge, leaving the physical universe and our embodied life in it, just as it is without any alteration. Knowing is just that which does not add to, alter or subtract from the object known. When I know the table on which I am writing, my knowing does not change the table or effect any alteration in it. And this is true both of true-knowing(vidva) or of false knowing (avidva). When I know the table wrongly as a desk and when a true knowing dispels the wrong knowing, the table become a desk does not recover its original form (the table) thereby. Therefore, in the very nature of true and false knowing we have a secret of our escape from the bondage of our embodied being in a physical universe. This is why avidva or nescience figures as a principle or a root cause of our empirical life in all Indian systems, Heterodox, Orthodox and Unorthodox such as Vaisnavism. In every one of these, avidya or maya or mayasakti is considered to be the cause of our relative temporal life and the way out of it, is held to be science or true knowing in some form or other. It is only avidva or false knowing that can be removed or cancelled by true knowing. Knowing true or false has no power to effect any alteration in the object known, but knowing when true has the power of negating or subletting the false-knowing. We shall see when we consider the heterodox and other accounts of the absolute life in the chapters that follow, how in every system, empirical life or samsara is ascribed to avidva or wrong-knowing in some form or other.

## Chapter II - Moksa In Indian Philosophy

## Introduction

From our discussion on the Purus arthas in the previous chapter, it is obvious that four purusarthas have been recognized by the orthodox schools of Indian Philosophy. The heterodox, however, have accepted only two artha and kama while the Vaisnavites have admitted a fifth and a higher value in addition to the loin ends of human life. Though, all the Vaisnava schools have recognized Bhakti as superior to Moksa. the credit of developing the concept of Bhakti, rather the Prema bhakti as the fifth and final purusartha however, goes to the Gaudiya Vaisnavas.Bhakti is ordinarily taken to be a means (sadhana) for the attainment of Moksa as Jnana (knowledge) and Yoga etc. are. There has been no question of regarding Bhakti as a Sadhya or the supreme object of attainment as the final purusartha, since the place of Moksa as the fourth or the final purusartha has never been questioned. This is how, Bhakti has been understood or misunderstood in schools other than that of the Gaudiya Vaisnavas. Coming to the Gaudiya Vaisnava school, we find, an altogether different trend, firstly, we find a strong denunciation of Moksa, that is to say, Moksa is no longer regarded as the ultimate goal of life, though it is still recognized as standing very high in spiritual value and significance. Secondly, bhakti is no longer regarded as or mere Moksa-Sadhana or means for the attainment of Moksa, but it becomes the supreme goal of life far superior to Moksa. Here in, Bhakti, both ns sadhana and sadhya may be said to become one and the same. Now before we can understand the full implication of the newly developed concept of Bhakti as the supreme ideal of life as advocated by the Gaudiya Vaisnavas, it is necessary to analyse the different conceptions of Moksa as found current in the nonvaisnava schools, and critically estimate the value of Moksa as against Bhakti as the supreme purusartha. We therefore, propose here to deal with different conceptions of Moksa in the light of the criticisms that have been presented by the Vaisnava schools particularly the Gaudiya Vaisnavas, against the conception of Moksa as the ultimate goal of life.

## A. The Buddhist Conception of Nirvana

The Buddhist have conceived Nirvana as the final end of life. It can be very well said that the conception of Nirvana is similar to that of Moksa or Mukti of the Orthodox Indian Schools, in as much as, it has been upheld as the ultimate goal of human life.

Nirvana etymologically, means blowing out, or extinguishing or becoming calmed, and secondarily moksa or liberation from empirical life. This is the central idea of the teachings of the Buddha and is considered to be the Buddhist ‘Summon Bonum’. Nirvana has been described in the following way: “As the vast ocean, O disciples, is impregnated with one flavour, the flavour of salt, so also my disciples! this law and discipline is impregnated with but one flavour, with the taste of (Nirvana)”. It was in order to lead his fellow creatures to nirvana that the Master preached the true law, it was in order to reach Nirvana those monks and nuns renounced the secular life and embraced homeless ascetic life which had one flavour and one passion viz., the passion for nirvana.

Nirvana, the highest goal, the ultimate objective of human aspiration and the summum bonum of rational life, was declared by the Buddha in his clarion voice to the suffering denizens of the three worlds (traidhatuka) as the panacea for the ills and suffering of existence to which all sentient beings from the amoeba to the highest God are subject without exception. This ideal state in which all sufferings and pains are extinguished totally and irrevocably was declared by the Master to be within the reach of all mortals, provided, they elected to pass through the course of discipline which was styled as the Eight Fold-Path (astarigika-marga). The nature and Function of the Eight-Fold-Path has been made clear by the Master himself in his first sermon at Benaras, ‘Dhamma-Chakka-Pavattana Sutta’ addressing the first five Bhikkus, the Buddha said.

There are two extremes, O Bhikkus: which the man who has given up the world ought not to follow the habitual practice on the one hand, of those things whose attraction depends upon the passions, and specially of sensuality-a low and pagan way (of seeking satisfaction) unworthy, unprofitable and fit only for the worldly minded and the habitual practice on the other hand, of asceticism (or self-mortification) which is painful unworthy and unprofitable”.

“There is a Middle Path, discovered by the Tathagate - a path which opens the eyes and bestows understanding, which leads to peace of mind, to the higher wisdom, to full enlightenment, to Nirvana, Verily": It is this Aryan Eight-Fold-Path; that is to say, as follows :

Pali Sanskrit English   1 samma ditthi2 samma samkappa3 samma vaca4 samma kammanta5 samma ajiva6 samma vayama7 samma sati8 samma samadhi samyak drstisamyak samkalpa samyak vacanasamyak karmasamyak ajivasamyak vyayamasamyak smrtisamyak samadhi right viewsright aspirationright speechright conductright livelihoodright effortright mindfulnessright self concentrations

Talking about the four noble truths (Arya saccam)i.e. the suffering, the origin of suffering, the cessation of suffering and the way to the cessation of suffering, the Buddha mentioned once more Eight-Fold-Path which leads to the destruction of sorrow. This Eight-Fold-Path represents the fourth noble truth which embodies the morality of Buddhism. Whatever may be the difference of views regarding the nature of Nirvana, all schools of Buddhism have accepted it as the cardinal principle of their religion and philosophy.

The only way to know the nature of Nirvans is to know it through the earnest practice of the good as preached and revealed in the Sacred canon. This is why Buddhism lays so much stress upon the practice of the Middle path between extreme self-mortification and extreme self-gratification. All attempts to know the real nature of Nirvana have invariably been attended with failure or at least, with very scanty success, while details in respect to training which has to be undergone by one who longs for its attainment are given in the Sacred canon with endless repetitions.

The problem of Nirvana so far as, its metaphysical character is concerned, boils down to a plain issue whether it is a positive state or a pure negative state. The difficulty of the problem has been aggravated by overwhelming emphasis upon the negative aspect, viz; that it is the end of all suffering, limitations and imperfections. We also come across passages which speak of Nirvana as the state of bliss (sukham). good (‘sivam) in which the troubles of phenomenal existence such as disease, death and decay, malice, jealousy, and hatred are extinguished without any chance of their recrudescence. It appears that the followers of the Buddha and also of other sects and schools of thought in India held that life is an unrelieved condition of misery and escape from its clutches was the ultimate goal. Whether this escape is eternal death or a transmutation of consciousness purged of its oddities and angularities, is a moot question. This problem became more and more obscure in Buddhism, because all schools of Buddhistic thought had accepted the cardinal principle of nonexistence of personality otherwise called Nairatmyavada. The Buddha analysed the personal identity into a complex of five aggregates (skandhas)which have no adhesive bond interse. They are respectively (1)Rupaskandha – the sensational aggregate (2) Vijnana – skandha-perceptional (3) Vedana-skandha – effections (4) Snanja-skandha – verbal (5) Samskara-skandha – impersonal or dispositional. A question may well be raised if there be no permanent soul behind the ever changing states of personal life, who is going to be free through the extinguishing of the suffering of life?

Santaraksita holds that Nairatmyavada or non-soul theory is that which distinguishes the teachings of the Buddha from the teachings of all other schools. There is no inner or immutable core in things, everything is influx.A real, for the Buddhist is ksanika (momentary), svalaksana (unique) and hence universals and permanent substances are all thought-constructs (Buddhinirmana). Such thought-constructs have their source in nescience or avidya. The Buddhists have developed their epistemology or ethics in full accord with their metaphysics. Strenuous attempts are made to fit this theory into the doctrine of karma and rebirth, avidya \| ignorance) which is the root cause of suffering, is the wrong belief in the atman, and prajna (wisdom) consists in the eradication of this belief and its attendant evils. The Buddha came to deny the soul, as a permanent substantial entity or as an unchanging eternal soul, impervious to change. It would render spiritual life to lose all meaning, and individual would in this case, be neither the better nor the worse for his efforts. This might lead to inaction (akriyavada). Nay, more, the atman is the root cause of all attachment, desire, aversion, and pain. When we take anything as self substantial and permanent, we became attached to it. sakkayaditthi (Sktsatkayadrsti substance view) is avidya (ignorance) par excellence and from it proceeds all passions. Denial of satkaya (atman or substance) is the very pivot of the Buddhist metaphysics and its doctrine of salvation.

The Buddha places before us the ideal of a state beyond all possibilities of pain and bondage. He emphasises the negative aspect of the state. Nirvana is the annihilation of sorrow. Only when we consider anything as permanent and pleasant as a self, we do get attached to it and averse to other things that are opposed to it, there is then, bondage (Samsara). The atta (atman) is the root cause of all passions, and this notion has to be rooted out completely to attain Nirvana.

The entire development of the Buddhist philosophy and religion is proof of the correctness of the Nairatmya interpretation of Buddhism. There is no school of Buddhist thought which does not deny the atman, and it is equally true that there is no Brahmanical or Jain system which does not accept the atman as an eternal principal in some form or other. It may be concluded that the atman the Buddhists deny, is material-self identified with body or with the particular mental states, and that their denial does not touch the position of the Samkhya or the Vedanta etc. But Buddhism does not seem to accept the reality of the atman as the permanent substantial entity impervious to change. The real for Buddhism, is becoming, and any species of the atman - view must take it as changeless identical substance, the Buddhist schools differed among themselves to a great degree. They had however, one thing in common viz, the denial of substance (atman).

It is suggested that the Buddha was a practical man, he rigorously excluded all theoretical considerations as vain, as not conducive to the spiritual life. Support is apparently lent to this by the Buddha’s rebuke of Malunkya-putta and Vacchagotta. It is concluded that the Buddha inculcated a way of life, but did not care to enunciate a view of reality. He addressed himself exclusively to ethics and left metaphysics severely alone.Votaries of this contention might further say that it was left to the scholastic phase of Buddhism to spin metaphysical theories and distort the teachings of the Master. The Master himself did not preach them, even by implication.

Can we have a way of life which does not imply a view of reality? Is it possible to follow an ideal of conduct which claims allegiance of the entire mankind without raising, by implication at least, questions about the ultimate value, the nature and destiny of the individual undertaking the discipline and his relation Io the ideal? The Mimansa school which is ostensibly concerned with dharma i.e. performance of sacrificial rites was, in the hands of later exponents found to be inescapably bound up with metaphysical and epistemological issues about the self and nature of karma etc.

The Buddha rejected the current philosophical systems and trends of thought as they were thought to be at variance with the path of perfection elaborated by him. This shows that the Buddha was alive to the fact that a way of life implies metaphysics. The practice of virtues and concentration of mind are not ends in themselves. They are meant for giving us insight into the nature of the real. The Buddha speaks against the mechanical observance of rites and virtues (Silavrataparamarsa). His own discipline would be indistinguishable from Silavrata without the insistence on prajna (insight) and it is this attainment of prajna that imparts unity and singlessness of purpose to several isolated acts of morality. Not only this, but it is prajna that perfects the virtues. One cannot be completely virtuous and freed from imperfections without insight. Insight makes spiritual life easy and natural. The Astasahasrika speaks of prajna as the leader of perfections. The other parmitas cannot even be paramitas (perfections or excellences) without praina-pramitas.
