He who is not convinced of the hollowness of things (sunyata )cannot also practice charity and other virtues to the utmost limit. He would have his reservation and hesitation. It is not possible then, to have more practice of virtues without having recourse to a metaphysic.

By far, the most serious objection to the view that the Buddha taught the Nairatmya doctrine, is that it is contradictory to his other doctrines accepted as basic, viz, the efficacy of karma, the adoption of spiritual life and the doctrine of rebirth, karma without a permanent agent who wills and reaps the fruits of his action is inconceivable. What is the value of spiritual life, if there is none at the end of it? The Buddha’s doctrine would amount to acceptance of pain or fatigue of the discipline without anyone who goes through the disciplines and reaps its fruits and a final result (Nirvana) without any individual to enjoy it. Such an absurdity, it might be said, could not have been meant seriously by the Buddha. For appreciating the congruity of the Buddha’s teaching with the Nairatmya - Theory, it will be proper for us to understand the real significance of continuity or flux which is the real nature of a person’s being ? In Arithmetic, the digits in serial order are said to be in continuity, the continuum is organically related, and every succeeding digit takes in its fold the preceding digit. And so is the case here in Buddhism. Here, the one preceding mode or trend of personality is entailed by the succeeding personality of being and so it can be said to be justified if the succeeding one is charged for actions of the preceding one.

We may also illustrate the Buddhist point of view by the following cases in point.

1\. Take the case of learning. Learning say, to ride a bicycle. The first attempt is an absolute failure, but the second is somewhat better than the 1st, and the 3rd is better still. This shows that the second attempt profits by the first which precedes it and the third profits by the second and the fourth by the third, and so on. This shows that every succeeding state carries with itself a residue of what has preceded it. The reals are ksanika-momentary. Every momentary real leaves its mark on what follows. And this is true not merely of reals regarded as extra mental reals (as by realistic schools of Buddhist, Vaibhasikas and Sautrantikas) but also of their mental replicas. Every mental state leaves its vasana or perfume as it were, on the state that follows, and thus the vasanas go on accumulating in a continuous stream. This is what produces the illusion of permanent enduring souls, though in actual fact there are nothing but fleeting momentary conscious states. The point to note is that every state, though, momentary, yet leaves a ghostly life in the state that follows, so that every present moment contains within itself the residues of all moments preceding it. This is also substantially the view of the modern French philosopher Henry Burgson. He also denies a substance that changes subscribing only to a continuum of changing states, but the past somehow prolongs itself into the present so that reality as change may be compared, as Burgson says, “to a rolling snow-ball that gathers volume as it advances”. This is also the thesis of the Buddhist. There is no eternal or permanent self. And the unchanging soul-substance behind the changing states of consciousness is a superfluous concept of the metaphysicians that serves no purpose. As unchanging, it is not affected by the changing states, of what use is it then so far as our ever-changing empirical life is concerned? It has nothing to do with, and does not in any way, explain the ever changing panorama of experience. What matters for practical purpose is how to control this flux of experience so that we may get rid of the ills that flesh is heir to. This is also the problem that Buddha poses in all his teachings. There are differences however, in the Buddhist schools about the character of the state of Nirvana which according to the Buddha will result from the control of the flux of experience through the practice of the Eight Fold Path.

Whatever may be the differences among the adherents of the Buddha regarding the character of the self or personality, they seem to be unanimous in their denial of the reality of the ego. Whether there is any essence behind and beyond this physical flux is the crux of the problem. Among the different schools we come across both positive and negative descriptions of the essence of Nirvana. In the Milinda Panho which, though an extra canonical work, has yet commanded the respect of the orthodox Buddhist, we come across glowing descriptions of the state of Nirvana. Nirvana is described as beauty, sweetness, bliss unaffected by foreign elements as the scholars like Poussin and Oldenburg regard these descriptions as true of the experience of Nirvana in the life time of the saint (sopadhisesa) and not after death nirupadhisesa. Hardy, Burnouf and Childers are definitely of the opinion that after the death of the saint, Nirvana means a blank extinction.

It would appear from above that nirupadhisesa - Nirvana i.e. actual Nirvana is endless death and is very different from the Nirvana one experiences when alive (sopadhisesa). Certainly, if this were the view of the author of the Milinda Panho, he ought to have spoken out the truth that all the epithets that were poured on Nirvana by him, were irrelevant in the final state after death. Of course, the denial of the experiencer confronts one with a puzzle. But there may possibly be an interpretation which will reconcile the two apparently irreconcilable views.

Stcherbatsky, in his ‘central conception of Buddhism’, gives his definite opinion that Nirvana is a negative state. He (Poussin) apparently disagrees with Dr. O. Schroeder, Mrs. Rhys Davids and the other exponents who find a positive background.

Coming to the schools we are faced with a bewildering variety of views. The Vaibhasikas otherwise known as the Sarvastivadins, were realists. Their metaphysical doctrines are a medley of Samkhya and Vaisesika views. They believed in the atoms as the ultimate constituent of the material world. But they also believed that everything is subject to change every moment and yet held as in the Samkhya-view of matter that, change presupposes an unchanging core. So everything regarded from the view point of substance is affirmed to be real for all time (dravyasat). But their modes are momentary which is also the case with Samkhya. But they do not believe in a perjuring subject (jnatr). Consciousness is an accident which emerges on the impact of matter on the senses. Again, consciousness is said to have a necessary reference to an object exactly on the pattern of Nyaya-Vaisesika view. So they do not appear to believe in pure consciousness bereft of subject-object polarization (grahya-grahaka vinirmuktam) as the Vijnanavadins and the Vedantins do. This view of emergence of consciousness in consequence of the impact of matter on the senses is analogous to the epistemological position of the Nyaya-Vaisesika Schools, save the vital difference that the latter believe in an eternal soul substance which though, unconscious by itself, occasionally becomes the substratum of consciousness. According to the Vaibhasikas, Nirvana - ultimate liberation-consists in the mutual disjunction of the different elements or skandhas from one another so that eternal substantive reality underlying the phenomenal modes alone remains. The Vaibhasikas hold that pratisamkhya-nirodha or dispersion of passions and defilements is a condition precedent to Nirvana. This Nirodha-annihilation of the obstructive barrier to what lies behind the emergence of phenomenal life of suffering is attainable only by transcendental intuition (pratisamkhya). This intuition lasts till physical death, after which there is disintegration of the complex of personality into its constituent factors and there is no consciousness thereafter. This state of emancipation is analogous to that of the Nyaya-vaisesika schools, in which, the soul substance bereft of all conscious life persists as a spent up Volcano. There is no experience of pain in it. Thus, the Vaibhasika conception of Nirvana is an unconscious state of existence, lifeless, unfeeling, unthinking inactive and impervious to pain or pleasure. Obviously, final Nirvana (nirupadhisesa) is less appealing than Nirvana, as experienced during the life time of the saint, i.e. sopadhisesa. It can be preferred only by those to whom death has greater appeal than life. The fact that there remains an eternal existent fact, as the bulwark against the experience of phenomenal joy and sorrow has no emotional attraction. The only difference between ordinary death and Nirvana is that the former, is temporary respite and the latter, is eternal death. In spite of the special pleading of the philosopher, an average man will scent in it a tinge of morbidity. Love of Nirvana is practically equated to love of death. It may give consolation to an ascetic mind, the saviour of Cynicism, so far as the ordinary run of people are concerned.

The Sautrantika philosopher is a realist. Though, we do not possess any representative work of this respectable school of Buddhist philosophy, we have to depend on the works of other schools for our information about philosophy advocated by this school. Dinnaga and Dharma-Kirti have referred to their views in their commentaries which can be taken to be the views of Sautrantika school. The cardinal tenent of nairatmya, the doctrine of selflessness or denial of personality, is vehemently, sponsored by them. Everything is in flux and existence is concomitant with momentariness. In one word, nothing exists beyond one moment. One consciousness is followed by another which comes into being and dies beyond resurrection. Their doctrine is known as Ksnabhangavada (the doctrine of flux or momentariness). The exponents of this school have invented subtle logical instruments. But what is the ultimate ideal state which is to be accomplished in the Nirvana, either total extinction or the continuity of the series of consciousness-moments without any content? Of course, there is freedom from pain and that is the only redeeming feature. Apart from metaphysical difficulties which have been pointed out by rival philosophers, the state of perfection embodied in Nirvana, does not yield satisfaction to the ethical and philosophical urge.

The idealism, in which the Sautrantika Realism culminates, as in the school of Dinnaga and Dharmakirti, advocates the momentariness and fluxional character of consciousness. The denial of extra-mental reality is a consequence of their representative theory of perception. Instead of external objects, there are only mental and ideal contents which are asserted to masquerade as reals (objective). There is, however, a fatal objection to this sort of philosophical thought in that, it cannot resist its culmination in solipsism. If consciousness of external object is suspect, that of the external objects as perceived through physical bodies has no a priori validity of its own. The belief in an infinite number of consciousness-centres is more or less an uncritical postulate. In Nirvana we have got pure consciousness-moments succeeding one another in an uninterrupted series, each cognizant of itself and enjoying the blessedness of isolation and solitude. In the Upanisads, we get the account of the Absolute spirit, Brahman, who was alone before creation, feeling discontented with this loneliness, he desired to be many. Loneliness may be desirable in moments of high pressure of work and worries but it is doubtful whether its blessedness will be matter of perpetual enjoyment. Whether there be a multiplicity of consciousness centres or one singal-Impersonal Consciousness or Intelligence in the ultimate analysis. The result is the same. After all, there is no social life, each consciousness moment living its solitary life of pure consciousness without being aware of the existence of other such consciousness moments. To an average man it may appear as a sentence of solitary imprisonment.

The Madhyamikashave proved by their formidable array of logical arguments that all things in this phenomenal world are interdependent and as such have no metaphysical claim to intrinsic reality. The whole world-subjective and objective is an unrelieved appearance as insubstantial as dream. There are no knowing subjects but are creatures of a transcendental illusion. There is no core of reality either in the subject or in the object. Buddhist idealism at any rate, in so far as, it is represented by the school of Dinnaga and Dharmakirti is unrelieved subjectivism. It claims to prove the unreality of objective world like Berkeley who calls his philosophy ‘Immaterialism’. There is much in common between Buddhist subjectivism and Berkeley’s Immaterialism minus the latter’s commitment to belief in God. Buddhist idealism can be compared more closely with the Humean Empericismwhich followed as a logical corollary of Berkeley’s subjectivism. Lately, when Nagarjuna entered the list, he encountered no difficulty in getting rid of the objective world. But he also hoisted the subjectivists on his own pattern.

Nagarjuna started with the postulate that whatever is relative is appearance, pure and simple. An extra mental fact qua an object has no independent existence of its own, apart from the cognition of it. We are aware of the existence of external objects so far as they are objects of our awareness, but as things-in- themselves apart from their relation to consciousness, we cannot have a sure ground for believing in the existence thereof. Belief is also a species of cognition and this makes its objective reference consequential. Nagarjuna’s followers endorsed the line of argument adopted by subjectivists in demolishing the objective real. But whereas, the subjectivists assert the autonomous existence of consciousness, the Madhyamikas join issue with them on the ground that if relativity be the criterion of unreality, consciousness will not fare better. It also has a necessary objective reference. Consciousness must be consciousness of something. Cognition without an object is a chimera. So the argument of Dharmakirtithat the object is identical with cognition, because the two are necessarily perceived together and because, there is no evidence of an object out of relation to consciousness, turns out on closer scrutiny to act as a boomerang upon the .subjectivists. Cognition is always consciousness and it always relative to an object and is never perceived out of relation to it. Granted that cognition and object are identical and also that the object is illusory appearance. It necessarily follows that cognition being identical with the illusory appearance must turn out to be equally illusory. This is the epistemological argument employed by later exponents of Sunyavada against the subjectivists.

Regarding Nirvana, Nagarjuna avers that the concepts of existence and non-existence are irrelevant. The phenomenal world that one encounters in ordinary experience is only a pure unsubstantial dream having no title to intrinsic reality. Accordingly, birth, decay, suffering, death etc. all sail in the same boat and are nothing but unsubstantial appearances, and consequently, the question of real deliverance from this unsubstantial soul is without sense or meaning. From the stand-point of empirical conventional experience, the Buddha is said to have affirmed the way of escape from the misery of existence. But all this is pure imagination when viewed subspecie eternitatis. They are valid only sub specie temporis. Nagarjuna, again is emphatic that the questions, regarding the post-existence of the enlightened one after Nirvana, or regarding the beginning or end of the world, or their opposites or the eternity or the non-eternity of the self are pointless argumentations. They have no logical relevancy or metaphysical validity. Simply because these are all fictitious creations of the philosopher’s imagination. The reticence of the Buddha as recorded in the cannon, when confronted with these questions is the only legitimate attitude, because any answer either in the affirmative or negative would only mislead the enquirer. Nagarjuna compares these notions with those of fictions, like sky-flower or barren mother’s son. These expressions are unmeaning non-sense. They are mere words and there can possibly be no concepts corresponding to them. Nagarjuna affirms that Nirvana can neither be regarded as existent nor as non-existent, because an existent is necessarily subject to birth, decay, and death. If Nirvana were existent, it would be liable to these contingencies and instead of offering a heaven of rest and peace, it would be a case of falling from the frying pan into the fire. Nor, can it be dubbed nonexistent, because, a non-existent is nothing but another existent or a transition from the existent.Nirvana is thus a pure idea or a mere word without a meaning. The sentences and propositions regarding Nirvana are nonsensical utterances.

What about the positive epithets that have been predicated of Nirvana in the Sastras? Nirvana is called Siva (Summun bonum). quasi negatively, it is called the cessation of transmigration. All these characteristics would have no meaning unless Nirvana be a positive state and not a negative blank. Nagarjuna says “Nirvana is the cessation of the phenomenal world, no doubt. But it is only a relative assertion, Sub specie eternitatis, the world does not exist and so the question of its cessation is utterly irrelevant. It is called Siva, that is good, because all experience comes to a cessation. It is a state in which all knowledge of the objective and the subjective world have entirely disappeared. The conception of Nirvana, accordingly, is all imagination compact.” Nagarjuna thus seems to dismiss Nirvana and worldly life with scant courtesy as unfounded illusion from top to bottom”. We also come across analogous assertions in the Upanisads, “the blind man discovers a gem, he stitches it up, though, he had no fingers and a man without a head and shoulder wears it as an ornament.” It is extremely difficult to assert that Nirvana is a transcendental state like that of the Brahman or Absolute of the Upanisads. The Upanisads also declare the ineptitude and irrelevancy of all positive and negative descriptions of the ultimate reality and Nagarjuna seems to follow suit. It is extremely risky to deduce from this procedure however, that they aim at one and the same thing. Whenever the question of Reality – transcendental or otherwise is mooted, Nagarjuna stubbornly declines to entertain it as a correct appraisal The Upanisads, in spite of their negative approach have, no doubt, about the ultimate Reality of Brahman, though, it is repugnant to all conceptual determinations. We therefore feel constrained to conclude that, “it must be left an open question whether the sanyata which literally means voidity is capable of being equated with Absolute Being or non-Being.”

## B The Jaina view of Free Absolute Life

The aim of Jaina Ethics, theology or metaphysics is to liberate the soul from the cycles of births, deaths and rebirths. The attainment of Moksa or Nirvana is the aim of a Jaina mendicant. The state of Moksa is not quite a new thing, it is simply the attainment of something which is inherent in the person from the very beginning. Jainas believe that the soul is really pure, having infinite power, knowledge and bliss, but due to the influx of the Karmic matter, its true nature is blurred, as a mirror is blurred by the collection of dust particles. So in Moksa - the soul regains to its true character, or we may say that the opaque veil of karmas is removed and expelled. The Influx of karmic matter is the cause of bondage. So, in a sense, to stop the influx of karmic matter and effect complete expulsion of accumulated karmic particles from the soul is known by emancipationin Jainism and then only the soul recovers its intrinsic state of being.

The Jainas bear close similarity to the Samkhya school in so far as they believe in the duality of Jiva and Ajiva similar to the Purus a and Prakrti of the Samkhya Philosophy. The state of liberation is the separation and disjunction of these two entities in both the systems, whereas the state of bondage is the unholy union of these two different substances. The union is caused by the contact of karma and is called bandhain the Jaina system. The cause of this bandha is called asrava or flow of karmic matter into the soul. Here we do not propose to go into detailed discussion of the Jaina ontology or cosmology or logic, but we are to limit ourselves to an exposition of the means by which according to the Jainas, the influx of karmic matter may be stopped or in other words, the means by which the soul can attain liberation. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra, it is said that, “by renouncing activity he obtains inactivity, by ceasing to act, he acquires no new karmas and destroys the karmas he had acquired before (by penance and various courses of ascetic life)”. But one should not think that Jainism preaches inactivity, on the other hand, this is a religion of constant endeavour and practice. “By renouncing activity”, here the teacher means the activity which springs from passion, greed, lust, anger and ignorance. The Jaina teachers understood that being a lively member of the society or world, a man cannot master his own passions, greed, lust and infatuation and so they advised the spiritual aspirants to renounce the world and adopt an ascetic life. An ascetic can master his mind, speech and action better rather than a worldly man. A man of the world feels a lot of difficulties in following a maxim strictly, due to his various liabilities and responsibilities as a man of the world. But a mendicant is free from all these fetters and for him, there is no other aim than perfecting himself by strict observance of the spiritual and moral teachings of the teacher.

The karmas which spring from kama, krodha, lobha and moha, blur the real nature of the soul. Such karmas can be destroyed by the performance of austerities. By abstaining (1) from destroying life (2) from lying (3) from taking anything which is not given (4) from all sexual indulgence (5) from having any property and (6) from eating at night (over indulgence to the appetite), the soul becomes free from asravas. It is in other words, a purifying process, purging of the soul from all the dirt that has accumulated on it through participation in worldly life. It is stated that by means of the practice of the five samitis and the three guptis, one becomes qualified for getting rid of the asrava or accumulated karmic matter in the soul. The five samitis, are five forms of observance of carefulness in behaviour so that there may not be any injury or harm to sentient creatures on account of careless conduct. The five forms of samitis or careful behaviour are viz., (i) The use of the trodden path so that no living being should be hurt through inadvertence (irya). (ii) gentle and beneficial talk (bhasa), (iii) receiving alms in a proper manner i.e. without asking lot more than is given, (iv) observance of carefulness in pulling down and removing things (adananiksepa), (v)evacuating in secluded places (utsarga).

The guptisor restraints are of three kinds. They correspond to the yamas of astangayoga and are as follows—(1) restraint of the body, (2) restraint of speech, (3) restraint of mind. The both are conducive to the pm gut ion of the spirit; they free the soul from accumulated karmic matter. We are told that by strict observance of the samitis and the guptis by subduing t he passions, by controlling the senses, by eschewing conceit, and by getting rid of the delusions of the mind, the soul becomes purged of the accumulated dirt of karmas or asravas.

Jainism is emphatic that both love and hatred are a fruitful source of undesirable karmas and have therefore, to be carefully avoided. A monk who strictly avoids both, escapes being entangled in the cycles of transmigration mid rebirth. Dispassion is the key note of the spirit which the Jainas inculcate. A Jaina saint declares that "the quintessence of wisdom consists in not killing any living being”, while Jainism insists on one side on the mastery of the passions and the subjugation of the senses, it also, exhorts equal emphasis on Ahimsa or the cult of non-injury in thought, speech and action. How far it is practical however, is a moot question. Would a Jaina saint save a human life attacked by a fell-disease such as Cholera by destroying the lives of innumerable Cholera Bacilli? Or would he leave the person to his fate and refuse to save him at the cost of the lives of so many living beings viz; the Bacilli? Further, is Jaina Ahimsa possible at all, in a nature “red in tooth and claw?” It cannot be denied that life lives on life and the relation of the stronger to the weaker is that devouring and being devoured. Some animal live on plant life, other animals devavour both animals and plants, as we human beings do. We cannot do without animal or vegetable food. Even milk is animal food and plants also have lives, so there is no getting away from Ahimsa, if one is to live at all.

The Hindu Sastras do recommend some kind of food very suitable and other kinds as being unsuitable and undesirable. But they are so recommended or forbidden because of their good or bad effects on the human psychophysical apparatus and not because they do not involve himsa However that may be, the Jainas do believe that himsa or injury, causes the flow of karmic matter into the soul while Ahimsa - non-injury to creatures is conducive to the stopping of this flow and getting rid of what has already accumulated therein.

Before dealing with the problem of passion and action, we must remove a misunderstanding regarding the karmana-body conception which sometimes leads critics to suppose that Jainism is fatalism. It is said in Jainism that this karmana-body determines audarika-body i.e. the external embodiment. But this deterministic view will make morality and self-improvement impossible. On this point ‘An epitome of Jainism’ makes out that, “the Jaina metaphysics and ethics which are but complementary parts of a singular system of thought are based on the belief in the free will of Jiva who is the maker and maser of his own fate.

The conception of samvara and nirjara stands against the deterministic ideal. By the practice of samvara, influx of new karmic matter is arrested, whereas by the processes and activities whereby the karmic matter enveloping the soul is purged out or their effects completely neutralised is called nirjara. This conception opens before the mumuksu(aspirant) the vast held where he can very consciously utilise his free will and make his future. These conceptions give morality both meaning and significance.

We are now to see the cardinal virtue which helps the mendicant to a greater extent in self-realisation or attainment of perfection which is the Summum bonum according to Jainism. Virtue and vice (punyaand papa) mostly centre round the caritra or conduct, caritra which consists in abstention from evil deeds is the conscious adjustment of the human activities for the attainment of a particular end. According to Jainism, the mental disposition of the moral agent by the preponderance of which he obeys md loves to act in conformity with the Law is termed subjective merit or bhava-punya. and in so far as, he out of his love of good, translates his love in overt conduct and thus obeys the law and acts in conformity to his ideal, he acquires what is termed as dravya-punya or objective merit. In every meritorious act, in an (knowledge), sraddha (reverence), anuraga (self confidence, faith and love of truth), and virya (energy of will) are indissolubly blended together. It can be said that meritorious act is the moral strength consisting in willfully practicing the good with love and intelligence.

The way to nirvana lies through the three jewels triratna) of faith in the Jina and the Jaina teachings, knowledge of his doctrine and perfect conduct, in in accordance with (such knowledge and faith). In the very opening words of Tattvarthadhigama Sutra of Um-Swami, it is said “Belief in the real existence or tattvas is right faith, knowledge of the real nature without doubt or error is right knowledge, an attitude of neutrality without desire or aversion towards the objects of the external world is right conduct. To attain liberation, all the three, must be pursued simultaneously. The path has been compared to a ladder with its two side poles and the central steps. The side poles are right belief and right knowledge and the steps are the gradual stages of right conduct. Right belief is the basis on which right knowledge rests, similarly right knowledge leads to right conduct. This reminds us of the Socretic teaching that “Virtue is knowledge’ so that without knowing the good, the practice of virtue is not possible. Jaina ethics has for its end the realization of kaivalya jnana or moksa. The necessary condition for reaching this end is the possession of right faith, right knowledge and right conduct. These three perfections are metaphorically called the three jewels, they are not produced but only revealed or made manifest on the removal of the layers of karma. To effect this removal of karmas, the rules of conduct must be observed and corresponding virtues must be acquired. It has been observed that in this system Five Great-Vows are of primary importance and the first four of which are also acknowledged by Brahmins and Buddhists. We may add however, that all the five are also stressed in Yoga-Sutra of Patanjali under the name of yamasor restraints. The five great vows (corresponding to the five yamas or restraints of the Yoga-Sutra) are ahimas, satva, asteva, brahmacarya and aprigraha. i.e. non-injury, truthfulness, or veracity, abstention from stealing, abstention from sex-gratification and non-acceptance of more than what is necessary. They are all of negative nature; even truth-fullness is abstention from false-hood or deceit. These five vows are to be strictly observed by the monks who take them on entering the order, or as itis commonly expressed, on taking diksa (initiation). In either case the vows are called five great vows (Mahavrata). Lay people however, should observe these vows so far as their conditions admit. The five vows of the lay people are called the small vows (Anuvrata). A monk on entering the order takes five great vows. If they are strictly followed, no new karma can accrue; but to practice them effectively, more explicit regulations are required, and these constitute the discipline of the monks. This discipline can be described in the following way.

Since, through the activity of body, speech and mind, which is technically called yoga by the Jainas, karmic matter pours into the soul (asravas) and forms new karma, it is necessary, in order to prevent the asrava (or to effect samvara): to regulate these activities by keeping body, speech and mind in strict control; there are the three guptis (kaya-gupti, vacana-gupti and manogupti).

Even, in those actions which are inseparable from the duties of monk, he may become guilty of sin by inadvertently transgressing great vows (e.g. killing of living beings) to avoid such sins he must observe the five summitis (mentioned above) i.e. he must be cautious in walking, speaking, collecting alms, taking up or pulling down things and voiding the body etc.

Passions being the cause of amalgamation of karmic matter with the soul, the monk should acquire virtues. The four cardinal vices (Kasayas)are anger (krodha), pride (mana), infatuation (moha), and greed (lobha) and all these can be subdued only when the monks or the laity follow the five vows.

The teaching of anupreksaor bhavana (twelve reflections) on the transitoriness of all things, on the helplessness of men, on the misery of the world, and similar topics are simply elaborated so that the followers may tread on the path of the five great vows with more enthusiasm and zeal, more sincerely and faithfully. They were shown the picture in order that they may put forth efforts to cast off their attachment for this apparently charming and fascinating world. Reality is nothing but a maze for the soul in which it wanders along in blind alleys, without being unable to find out the exit.

The elaborate and clear treatment of twenty-two troubles and annoyances (parisaha)which a monk must endure without flinching, such as hunger and thirst, cold and heat, illness, ill-treatment, emotions etc., is simply to give a backbone to the sincere adherents of the five vows, so that he may not feel desperate. Right conduct consists in control of the sinful activities and in the annihilation of all karmas and thus can be best achieved only when the five great vows are strictly and sincerely followed in the course of life.

It is shown that the tapas (austerity)is of two kinds (a) external and (b) internal. This classification shows that only following a teaching externally, does not serve the purpose fully and completely; for full and complete result, the follower must adhere to it from the core of his heart, i.e. must shape his mind into the proper mold in accordance with those maxims. The overt action must be guided only by the heart or internal bent of mind. The expressed action should be nothing but the exact translation of the internal feeling and adaptation and change of mind and heart.

Radha Krishnana observes that the chief feature of Jainism is Ahimsa or respect for abstinence from injury to everything that has life”. The scrupulous enforcement of this rule has led to many practices which come in for cheap sneering at the hands of unsympathetic students ’’Lest any life be destroyed,” Jaina ascetics sweep the ground on which they walk, veil their mouth and nose for fear of inhaling a living organism, drink water which has been boiled and freed from small living animalculesand reject even honey. It is true that ahimsa in the strict sense cannot be practiced. The Mahabharata says “The world is filled with creatures which cannot be seen by eyes, though inferred by logic; when we move our limbs or even eyelids, their limbs break and fall”. A morbid fear of injuring any life perchance anywhere governs the conduct of orthodox Jninas.

To take any life, seems to the Jaina, the most heinous crime that entails the most terrible punishment, “yet the central thought of Jainism is not so much on saving up life as refraining from destroying it. “ahimsa pin amo-dharmah” - destroy no living creature ! Injure mi living creature! This is the highest religion”. Popal Lal K. Shah says “The foundational principle of Jaina religion is to abstain from killing”. They even call their faith the religion of non-killing (Ahimsa.-dharma).

A monk, scrupulously and studiously, abstains from taking any life, insect, vegetable or animal that has even one sense i.e. the lowest in the scale of living beings. The Jaina makes a very interesting distinction between spiritual and actual murder bhava himsa and dravya himsa. One sins against bhava ahimsa by wishing for any one’s death or desiring harm to befall on others. Not only so, but if one does not continue and complete one’s own education or strive to improve one’s own mind, or if one fails to exercise and discipline one’s own soul, one commits bhava-himsa. for one kills by stultification what one might have been, dravya-ahimsa or the abstention from actual or physical killing is absolutely binding on all Jainas of every sect and to offend against it, is the greatest of all sins.

As we have observed above, the five mahavratas or five great vows are the vows of ahimsa (Non-injury), satya (Truthfulness), asteva (non-stealing), brahmacarya (abstention from sexual indulgence), and aparigraha (non-acceptance of more than is necessary), ahimsa is considered to be the most important of all these five vows. Though, in Jainism ahimsa is construed negatively as abstention from killing and also abstention from all ideas or impulses of killing (bhava-himsa). There is however, nothing in Jainism to show that abstention is considered resulting from or issuing out of a heart full of love and sympathy for living beings as in Buddhism. In later Jaina Writings, we come across insistence on love and sympathy as well and in modern Jainas like Mahatma Gandhi, ahimsa (non-injury) in its positive aspect as love and concern for all creatures takes precedence over ahimsa considered merely as nonkilling. These however, are later modifications of the earlier Jain-view which preached ahimsa as a means to getting rid of asravas or the flow of karmic matter into the spirit. In fact, even the other four mahavratas were construed as different forms of non-killing. Truthfulness e.g. is abstention from mendacity and deceit, that is, abstention from stealing, a march over a fellow-being as thus depriving him of his dues. This is the same as a subtle form of killing. The same holds true of other three, asteva, brahmacarva and aparigraha. When one becomes a hoarder, he is sinning against aparigraha and is depriving others of their means of subsistence and lack of restraint in sexual behaviour brings on degeneration of the race. This is also killing of a sort. What the Jainas emphasis is ahimsa (non-killing) practiced in all the five mahavratas brings on right relations with the world in the conduct of life and thus prepares the way to sarhvara i.e. stopping of the further flow of the karmic matter into the soul. When thus, the spirit becomes free from the accumulated karmic matter, it becomes like a shining mirror that reflects the image of Reality into itself without distortion and fragmentation. This is pramana-jnana or complete and total knowledge of Reality as distinguished from the nayas, each of which reveals reality in a partial aspect. With pramana-jnana or total knowledge, the spirit becomes mere on-looker of the world-drama without attachment to it. This is the penultimate stage of Moksa in which the spirit merely looks on but is not an active participator in the world history. The highest stage however, is that in which the spirit passes beyond loka to aloka i.e. the transcendental plane. This is liberation of the spirit in the true sense of the term. According to Acarya Padmanandi, the spirit once entered the transcendental plane (lokakasa) never returned, though other planets like the Sun and the Moon do come back after entering that many a time. Thus the spirit attains Kaivalya or complete freedom in which not even a dispassionate vision of the world continues. In other words, it is also said that liberation constitutes a blissful state of the spirit (atma) retiring in the transcendental plane, when all types of desires and miseries of the mundane world are removed by the revelation of the true knowledge of the spirit.

It may be pointed out here that Moksa in the highest plane of aloka is not very much different from the svarup-avasthiti or self-repose of the spirit in the asamprajriatasamadhi of Yoga-darsana.

N.B -In samprainata samadhi there is consciousness of Prakrti as the other of the spirit and as completely detached from it, but in asamprainata-samadhi this negative relation to Prakrti as the wholly other of Purusa or the spirit vanishes and the spirit rests in itself without reference to anything beyond itself.

## C. Nyaya-Vaisesika-Conception of Moksa

The Nyaya Sutra of Gautama enumerates twelve kinds of prameyas or objects of knowledge. They are atman (self), sarira (body), indriyas (sense-organs), artha (objects of sense-experience), buddhi (experience or consciousness), manah (mind), pravrtti (will or volition), dos a (conative impulses of desire and aversion), pretyabhava (beginningless succession of bodies from past through present to future bodies causing births, deaths and rebirths till the attainment of apavarga or (liberation), phala (experience of pleasure of pain Along with their instrumental conditions), duhkha (pain) and lastly, apavarga (liberation). The apavarga according to Vatsyayana bhasya is destruction of the stream of births and deaths and consequent total and absolute cessation of duhkha or suffering.

In the Nyaya-sutrait has been said that duhkha, janma, nravrtti, dosa and mithyajnana are interlinked in the form of a chain so that the destruction of mithyajnana results in the destruction of dosa and so on. Thus apavarga can be attained through the successive destruction of the preceding factor of the series enumerated above, i.e. destruction of mithyajnana results in the destruction of dosa, destruction of dosa resulting in the destruction of nravrtti and so on. This, in other words, means that with the cessation of janma, duhkha is destroyed, since duhkha is only an effect of janma, when cause is destroyed, the effect also ceases to exist.

It will be seen that this series is started by mithyajnana or erroneous cognition. In fact, every system of Indian Philosophy considers mithyajnana or avidya to be the root cause of samsaraand all the ills of the empirical existence. One might say that it is the same belief that inspires Christian Philosophy and its doctrine of original sin. Only in Christianity, it is Adam’s original sin that corrupts the whole human race and is the cause of all woes that have befallen on mankind. But in Indian Philosophy the doctrine of vicarious sin and of vicarious suffering as a consequence is repudiated. There is, according to Indian view, no akrta-bhyagamah nor any krtapranasah. We reap what we have ourselves sowed. We never suffer from the faults of the others, at the same time, our good or bad deeds resulting in embodied existence and the consequent suffering can all be traced to an original or beginningless nescience or avidya. This is the view common both to the heterodox schools of Indian Thought, such as, Buddhism and Jainism as well as to the Orthodox Schools of Indian Philosophy such as Nyaya-Vaisesika, Samkhya-yoga, Purvamimamsa and the Vedanta.

The Naiyayika, as has been shown above is no exception in this respect. He considers mithyajnana to be the root cause that lies behind the souls participation in mundane life and the sufferings which such life entails. The Vatsyayana-bhasya points out in this connection that mithyajnana or the erroneous cognition is no unitary single experience, but is of innumerable different varieties. In fact, in respect of each of the twelve prameyas or the objects of knowledge from atman apavarga, there may be mithyajnana of various kinds. II is pointed out here that the way out of mithyajnana or erroneous cognition is tattvajnana or the knowledge of the Truth, mithyajnana in fact, is knowing one thing in the character of another thing e.g., knowing an eternal reality as non-eternal is mithyajnana or erroneous cognition, so also knowing the non-self as the self or the non-eternal as the eternal are forms of erroneous cognition. When we believe duhkha to be of the nature of sukha, our belief is erroneous, tattvajnana as distinguished from mithyajnana is knowing a thing in the form that belongs to it or constitutes its nature. Thus, knowing the non-eternal as non-eternal is tattvajnana. Similarly, when the non-self is apprehended as non-self or other than the self, it is tattvajnana. Again, when duhkha is known as duhkha, it is tattvajnana and so on. Thus, knowing a thing just as it is, is tattvajnana, just as, knowing a thing as what it is not is mithyajnana and the means of cancelling of mithyajnana is tattvajnana or the knowing a thing as it is.

The Naiyayika holds that when the non-self is falsely believed to be self, there arise the dos as in the form of attraction and aversion for objects. The non-self is other than the self and cannot in any way, affect the self for good or evil. When however, one falsely considers nonself to be the self, one comes to believe that the non-self can affect the self agreeably and disagreeably. And hence arises raga or dvesa - attraction or aversion for objects. Such attractions or aversions are based on the wrong belief that the non-self can further or hinder the self or can be a source of good or evil to the atman. It is contended by the Naiyayika, that this wrong belief of non-self being the self, or at least, as capable of affecting the self agreeably or disagreeably, gives rise to attraction or aversion which, in their turn, results in right or wrong doing, which again, is the cause of embodied existence, entailing duhkha or suffering. It is contended by the Naiyayika that janma or embodied existence means entailing suffering (duhkha). It may be noted however, that the Naiyayikas do not reject the experience of sukha as a psychological reality, but they contend that the sukha or the pleasure that we enjoy in embodied existence is inseparably bound up with duhkha so that whosoever desires complete and absolute escape from all kinds of sufferings, must also be prepared to eschew sukha-kamana or the desire for pleasure of life. Pleasure brings pain in its train, therefore, even pleasure is an effect of experience, just as pain is. It is nevertheless, so indissolubly bound up with pain that the aspirant after emancipation from pain, must consider all pleasures to be nothing but pain.

The Buddhist holds that all is pain and nothing but pain. In other words, what we call sukha or pleasure, is only duhkha-vikalpa or negative escape of pain misconstrued as positive pleasure. The Buddhist thus, denies, pleasure as a psychological reality of our experience. Not so the Naiyayikas recognise the pleasures as a real positive experience, but they also hold that on account of the relativity of pleasure as well as pain, we never have pleasure which does not bring pain in some form or the other. Vatsyayana says - just as rice-meal which has been sauced by honey mixed with poison, is to be eschewed as undesirable, so also sukha or pleasure inseparably bound up as it is, with pain, is to be eschewed as undesirable. Udyotakarathe Nyayavartikakara, further elucidating this point says - “the anusanga of duhkha in sukha i.e., the necessary concomitance of duhkha with sukha points out that the co-relation of pleasure and pain consists (1) in their avinabhava-sambandha so that wherever pleasure is, pain also is; (2) in their samananimittata so that whatever causes pleasures also causes pain ; (3) In their sumana-dharata. i.e., whatever is the adhara or substratum of pleasure is also adhara or substratum of pain; (4) lastly, there is also samanopalabhyata whatever experience of pleasure is also accompanied by the experience of pain. That is, whosoever enjoys pleasure has also to suffer misery or pain. Similar views have been expressed by Vacaspati in Nyaya-Vartika- Tatparyatika.

The Naiyayikas and the Vaisesikas agree in the main about a negative view of moksa or liberation as Parama Purusartha or the supreme value of life. In fact, the two schools, Nyaya and Vaisesika are regarded as samanatantra or as having a common and almost identical world-view. According to both, there is complete disjunction of self and non-self in their intrinsic nature. Both also, hold that samsara i.e., empirical life and its attendant evils arise from a false identification of the self with what is other than the self and Moksa or liberation consists in as it does, in terminating or cancelling this false identification due to mithyajnana or erroneous cognition by tattvajnana or the right knowledge of the self as completely detached from the non-self. This ensures complete and absolute freedom from all the ills of life. In this respect, both the Naiyayikas and Vaisesikas endorse the same pessimistic view of the empirical life holding that the pleasures and the pains of life arising as they do, from the self’s attachment to non-self, have alike to be eschewed or abjured as sources of bondage. The Vaiseksikas therefore, define Moksa as the absolute destruction of the nine specific qualities of the self such as buddhi (intelligence), sukha (pleasure), duhkha (pain) iccha (desire), dvesa (aversion), prayatna (conation), dharma (righteousness), adharma (unrighteousness), and samskaras (psychical pre-disposition). The self, in other words, has certain samanyagunas or generic qualities which it has in common with other substances. These are five in number, viz. samkhya (number), parimana (magnitude), prthakatva (Separateness), samyoga (Conjunction), and vibhaga (disjunction).

The self for example, is vibhu parimana or infinite in magnitude and so also are space (dik) and ether (akasa) etc. vibhu-parimana is what the self possesses in common with other substances mentioned above. It is thus, a samanyaguna of the self, so also in regard to samkhya, prthakatva etc. But the nine specific qualities viz. buddhi, sukha, duhkha, iccha, dvesa, prayatna, dharma, adharma and samskaras, belong to the self alone and not to any other substance. It is through these specific qualities that the self comes in relation to the non-self so that with the destruction or negation of all these qualities without any possibility of their reappearance in future, arises complete severance of the self from the non-self. This, according to the Vaissikas is Moksa or true freedom of the self. In other words, it is described as a state of felicity that belongs by nature, to the self. But, how can it be described as a state of felicity in which there is absence not only of sukha as well as duhkha but also of jnana or consciousness? A felicity which is not a felt or experienced felicity can hardly be described as a state of felicity.

The Naiyayikas like the Vaisesikas endorse this negative view of Moksa as complete and absolute freedom of the spirit from the bondage of experience. But unlike the Vaisesikas they consider it to be a neutral state of the spirit in which not only pleasure as well as pain are absent but also consciousness. Hence it cannot be described as sridhara does, as a state of felicity.

The highest state, according to the Naiyayika is not freedom from experience for its own sake, but freedom from all experience for the sake of escape from all kinds of sufferings. Naiyayika describes Moksastate as absolute cessation of all kinds of suffering. This, according to the Naiyayika, implies renunciation of the pleasures of life, because, on account of the relativity of pleasure and pain, there is no escape from pain without renunciation of pleasure. For the Naiyayika, therefore, the conscious empirical life must end, if freedom from the sufferings of life is to be achieved. The Naiyayika is more consistent than the Vaisesika in his repudiation of a transcendental felicity in the self in the state of freedom or Moksa, which is unlike empirical pleasure. The Naiyayika holds that if the state of Moksa is to be described as a feliticious state, it can be so described only in a negative sense, i.e., as a state of escape from the unrest of life and experience. In this connection the Naiyayika refutes the view of those who consider Moksa in be a positive state of bliss. Thus, against them the Naiyayika points out that if anybody is to be prompted by I be desire for imperishable bliss, he will not be free in the true sense. He will be the slave of his desire for bliss and this desire as a motive force will be the cause of his bondage.

It is true that aversion or dvesa for suffering will also be equally a source of bondage. But this is why Moksa as absolute freedom from both the pains and pleasures of life can be attained only by transcending ill attractions and aversions in and through the practice of vairagya or dispassion. Thus, the mumuksu or the aspirant for Moksa is not actuated by any unhealthy desire or aversion. Freedom from pain as an ideal is ananukula or not favourable, or is not positively favourable to the self and therefore is riot an object of pathological desire. For the same reason, aversion which itself is a form of pain cannot act as a motive to the attainment of Moksa or atyantikaduhkhanivrtti. Hence the aspirant for Moksa is prompted neither by attraction nor by aversion and pursues his end in a spirit of detachment and dispassion.

As S.K. Maitra says - “The highest ideal conceived as the total and absolute cessation of pain is independent of all pathological motives, while the highest ideal conceived as a positive happiness necessarily implies impure motives and thus cannot lead to true freedom. The seeker of true freedom, therefore, seeks only cessation of pain from a pure feeling of dispassion without any pathological aversion as the motive”.

In this connection, the Naiyayika enters into a refutation of Mimamsa-view of Moksa as abhivyakti or manifestation of innate eternal bliss in the self. The Naiyayika points out in the criticism of Mimamsa-view that if there be eternal bliss in the self lying buried below the surface of conscious empirical life and which comes to the surface and becomes manifest in the Moksa-state, why should he not believe in innate eternal duhkha or misery also, lying buried beneath the surface of our conscious life?

In fact, the Mimamsa view, according to the Naiyayika, will land us into a strange under-world of sub-conscious states in which contradictory experiences of joy and sorrow, pleasure and pain, live in a subliminal life in peace and amity without mutual repugnance. Further, if the innate eternal happiness is to be conceived as coming to manifestation in the Moksa-state, the question is if this manifestation is to be regarded as eternal or as non-eternal? If, it be, regarded as eternal, then, there will be no distinction between the liberated and the non-liberated (samsarastha). Further, there would be no diversity in the emotional life, there being only one unbroken continuum of blissfulness. Moreover, blissfulness being incompatible with misery or suffering, there will be no experience of suffering and therefore, neither any desire for liberation from suffering (duhkhajihasa) nor any toiling for such liberation (Moksa-prayasa). Nor can it be said that the body is an obstacle to the realization of happiness or bliss and therefore there is need of toiling for the attainment of the bliss in question. The body is an instrument of fruition (upabhoga) and therefore cannot act as an obstacle to the attainment of satisfaction of any kind. Further, with eternal happiness of the liberated, one can also conceive an imperishable body as its instrument (nimitta-sadhana) thereof. But if an imperishable body be a contradiction in terms, so also, must be the imperishable happiness of which it is conceived as the instrument. If, on the other hand, the manifestation of the eternal bliss be regarded as non-eternal, then, there must be some cause for its non-eternal or occasional manifestation. If, the cause be atmamanosamyoga or the connection of the soul-substance with the mind which is its instrument of experience, this connection of soul and mind has to be assumed as a condition of the manifestation, besides, the existence of eternal bliss or happiness in the self. Therefore, in liberation, there will be the only condition for the experience or manifestation of happiness in the self and no external objects will be required. But the question may be raised whether the same condition of atmamanosamyoga or self-mind-connection will suffice for the experience of colour, taste, smell and other qualities of the external objects? If this be granted, we shall have a strange kind of liberation in which the self will, not only experience the eternal joy in the self, but, also, will have experience of all the objects of empirical life, (colour, taste smell etc.). Such liberation will certainly not be detachment because there (sarvarthopalabdhi).

It is pointed out by the exponents of nityasukhabhivyakti (i.e., Mimamsakas) that there is such a thing as istadhigamartha-pravrtti, i.e., conative activity for the attainment of the satisfaction to be derived from the attainment of objects of desire. Therefore, as such, conation can attain its fruition only if there be an eternal or everlasting bliss. It follows that there must be some eternal bliss without which all conative efforts become meaningless.

The Naiyayika, in reply to this argument, points out, however, that conation is both positive seeking of what is beneficial (hitaprapti) and negative avoiding of what is believed to be injurious or harmful (anistahana). Hence conation may have a negative as well as a positive end, and Moksa conceived as negative freedom may as well, be an object of conation as positive bliss or happiness.

The Naiyayika points out that there is no happiness which does not entail suffering but Moksa conceived as freedom both from happiness and suffering may very well be the object of our conative activity. It is, therefore, contended that conation finds its true fulfilment only in a state which transcends both happiness and suffering, since happiness always brings suffering in some form or the other in its train.

The Naiyayika in this connection refutes some other views of Moksa such as the views of the Buddhist and Patanjali, the yogasutrakara. According to the both, moksa consists in stopping or arresting the unceasing flux of mental experiences (Yogascacitta vrttinirodhah). Naiyayikas point out that if, this were so, then, cessation of experiences in death would amount to liberation or Moksa (ayane moksah siddhih) The Naiyayika points out that it is the self that is the seat of the impulses through the mind which is its instrument of experience. What is necessary is, therefore, the realisation of the self’s freedom from its accumulated pathological predispositions (samskaras) by purification. So long as, these dispositions continue in the self, there is no true freedom. Death may bring on a temporary cessation through separation from the body; but as long as, the dispositions continue in the self, there is rebirth through union with a fresh body and resurgence of tumultuous passions and impulses entailing new sufferings and miseries. The way out of this cycle is complete eradication of these accumulated predispositions in the self without possibility of future resurgence. This is possible, according to Naiyayika, through self knowledge and the due practice of the unconditional duties. Self-knowledge which destroys the false identification of self and non-self is only penultimate state of Moksa. When Moksa is attained through self-knowledge and other preparatory means, self-knowledge and in fact, all knowledge and experiences of sukha, duhkha, raga-dvesa etc. vanish, and the atman becomes suddhadrayva or pure soul substance. It is, in fact, a state of complete somnolence of the spirit, a sleep which is indistinguishable from death as it is without waking. It hardly differs thus, from the Buddhist Nirvana. The only difference is that there is a permanent self that sleeps this eternal sleep, (Naiyayika view) ; while according to the Buddhist, Nirvana is the extinction of the flux of experience without a remainder admitting of being described in a positive or a negative term.

The principal objection to the Nyaya-view of Moksa is its denial or rejection even of tattvajnana-knowledge of the truth in the mukta or liberated state. The Naiyayika subscribes to a conception of Paramatman or Supreme self that is known as God of religion and this Paramatman or Isvara as creator, maintainer, and destroyer of the world is endowed with nityajnana i.e., eternal knowledge of immediate experience of the whole of reality in its own intrinsic nature. If tattvajnana or knowledge or direct vision of the truth is no cause of bondage in God who is nityamukta i.e., eternally free, why should tattva-jnana - the knowledge of truth or the knowledge of reality as it is, be a cause of bondage in the finite individual? The Naiyayika does not give any reason why self knowledge or the knowledge of the truth about the self as well as non-self, should not continue in the state of Moksa or liberation.

## D. Samkhya-Yoga Conception of Moksa

In spite of an apparent family likeness between the Nyaya and Samkhya views of Moksa, there are important differences that distinguish Samkhya from Nyaya in this respect. According to Nyaya, there is a real bondage of the atman or self caused by mithyajnana - a bondage to what is non-self whence arise the ills of empirical life or samsara. And just as, bondage of self is real bondage, so also Moksa or liberation is real liberation of the self, following on the knowledge of truth or tattvajnana which cancels mithyajnana. For Samkhya also, bondage as well as liberation are real, but both appertain to prakrti - the objective background and its transformation into a world of experience. As regards Purusa or the subject, the question of bondage or freedom from bondage does not arise. Purusa is nitya suddha-buddha-mukta, i.e., eternally pure, eternally accomplished intelligence and eternally free. It remains forever untouched by the mundane appearances due to the transformations of prakrti.

The point to note about Samkhya is that it considers the self or Purusa to be of the very essence of self luminous Intelligence. It is not an intelligent subject as the Ramanujists hold, nor any intelligent substance as the Nyaya-Vaisesikas consider it to be; but is Intelligence itself and nothing but Intelligence, sva-prakasa or self illumination constituting its very being. Hence for Samkhya, the self has being for itself and object has being for the subject, that is, aware of the object, It has thus, being for another i.e., for the subject which is other than the object. But Purusa has being for itself. This is its nature as pure self-luminous intelligence. It is thus, real, that has being because of its own self and hence, is eternally accomplished in itself. Not so, however, the world of objects which has being only for and through a subject that is aware of it. Hence Prakrti, the avyakta or indeterminate background of the world of objects, becomes vyakta or manifest world of objects only through the mediation of Purusa. In other words, by reflecting the intelligence of purusa in itself, thus, the indeterminate non-intelligent Prakrti accomplishes itself as an intelligible objective world. It is prakrti that accomplishes itself in what is called samsara or mundane life, not Purusa - the transcendental subject that, qua self-luminous intelligence is eternally accomplished in itself. In fact, it would not accomplish the non-sentient Prakrti into an intelligible world unless it were eternally accomplished in-itself. Experience, thus, and all the ills that come with it, belong to prakrti and are processes within prakrti in so far as prakrti is motivated by cicchava or the reflection of Purusa’s intelligence into itself. Prakrti in fact, in its avyakta or met-empirical on- manifest state is a blind manifold of unrelated reals called the gunas in their samyavastha or state of uniform diffusion. It is the state in which the three categories of reals-sattva, rajas and tamas - are equally distributed throughout so that no where we have a preponderance of sattva over rajas and tamas or of rajas over sattva and tamas, or the tamas over sattva and rajas. This informally diffused state or samyavastha is thus one in which we have the same form everywhere and therefore no distinct form anywhere. It is, thus, a state of formlessness or one of complete absence of the distinguishable forms. It is thus, the avyakta or nonmanifest or met-empirical state of prakrti, met-empirical in the sense that no experience is possible as long as this uniformity or formlessness continues. This therefore, does not explain experience which is the consciousness of objects as unities in-difference. Since, experience however, is a fact, we must posit an original nescience in Prakrti and anadi-aviyeka or beginningless lack of discrimination which makes the indeterminate non-intelligent background try to appropriate Purusa’s intelligence into itself and thus becomes a significant intelligible world. This is cic-chaya-patti in prakrti, prakrti reflecting Purus a’s intelligence into itself in order to become an intelligible world of experience. Out of this non-discrimination (i.e., aviveka) of prakrti from Purusa, results therefore, determination of the pre-empirical (samyavastha) uniform diffusion so that vaisamya - differentiation and integration - now succeed the original state of uniformity or formlessness. Thus, is started the evolution i.e., parinama of prakrti which first, transforming itself into buddhi (i.e., intelligence) neutral matter of experience neither subjective nor objective, and therefore, through buddhi into ahmkara or apperceiving unity on the subjective side and the five tanmatras or five forms of potential energy on the objective side, through ahmkara again, into eleven organs of sense on the subjective side, and the five different elements with actual (kinetic) physical and chemical characters . This is the involution of prakrti into samsara and desuetude of experience. Through it prakrti reflecting Purus a’s intelligence into itself, becomes an empirical self encountering an empirical world and being a jnata-bhokta and karta of the latter, but the bhoga of experience does not satisfy the empirical ego (antah karana) shining by the borrowed light of Purusa’s intelligence. The objective world arising as it does, out of non-intelligent prakrti cannot be resolved into the transparent unity of the purusa’s intelligence, as a result, there is unending movement of experience from one form to another without rest in any form as completely satisfying. This restless movement from one dissatisfaction to another brings on the realisation at least, that peace and ultimate rest can come only through reversing the entire downward process from the indeterminate prakrti into a determinate world of experience. Just as involution and transformation of prakrti has its root in an original aviveka or nondiscrimination or non-detachment in prakrti which causes the non-intelligent prakrti to try to appear as an intelligible world by reflecting Purus a’s intelligence into itself and thereby bringing on a world of miseries and dissatisfaction, so the remedy lies in cancelling aviveka by viveka-khyati i.e., by the intuition of essential detachment of Prakrti from Purusa. As soon as viveka or intuition of difference dawns in the empirical self, the indriyas become dissolved in ahmkara or ego and ahamkara in buddhi on the subjective side. Just as the pancabhutas become dissolved in tanmatras and tanmatras into buddhi on the objective side and with lava or dissolution of buddhi in prakrti both the individual and his world of experience disappear into the indeterminate prakrti. Thus bondage, i.e., the empirical life and its ills and liberation alike belong to prakrti. Purusa as nitya-suddha-buddha-mukta remaining forever untouched by the pyrotechnics of prakrti as a downward movement into experience or as an upward process into the original indeterminateness (avyaktavastha).

K.C. Bhattacarya says “Mukti is regarded as desired, not as it is to the Vedantist as something eternal, the achievement of which is itself illusory. The unfree state of the self (bandha) is real to Nyaya, beginninglessly real though, terminable. To Samkhya it is unreal as a state of the self, the self being essentially incapable of modification, but it is real as a state of the mind as object, as the beginningless but terminable non-distinction of the mind from the self. The mind is in time and its termination (which is its potentialisation into prakrti) is as much an event in time as the termination of any particular pain. The only difference between secular and spiritual desire for freedom is the difference between freedom from actual pain and freedom from the mind that is the potentiality of all pain.

Freedom from actual pain is still a bhoga or affective experience of the mind to Samkhya freedom from the mind itself being apavarga or absolute freedom”.

We have spoken above of Purusa in the singular which stands for the class of Purusas or Transcendental subjects. In other words, Samkhya subscribes to the doctrine of Purusa-bahutva or of a plurality of subjects, all of the nature of pure intelligence and each absolutely detached from and independent of prakrti - the objective background of the world of experience. According to Samkhya, prakrti has a unique relation to each of these subjects, a relation described as svatva-svamitva-sambandha through which prakrti becomes a particular world of experience in relation to each of these Purus as or ultimate subjects of experience. Hence we have as many empirical selves and worlds of experience as there are Purus as prakrti reflects each of these Purusas in itself and thereby becomes a unique world of experience different from other worlds generated in prakrti through similar unique relations to other purusas. This, according to Samkhya, explains sukha-duhkha-vyavastha and bandha-moksa-vyavastha. One man’s pleasure is not every man’s pleasure, when one man suffers, every other man does not suffer as a consequence, so also the bondage of one individual empirical self does not involve the bondage of every other empirical self. Just as, the liberation of one empirical self does not imply that all other empirical selves are simultaneously liberated with it. This one-and-one ordering of experience implies a pluralistic-illumination of prakrti by Purus a, i.e., prakrti, reflecting the intelligence of the plurality of purusas and becoming thereby many different worlds of many different empirical selves. Each world that appears in prakrti is thus uniquely related to a particular empirical self, so that there is no such thing as a common shared, inter-subjective world for all empirical selves that prakrti evolves out of itself. ‘Myself and my world’, ‘Yourself and your 'world’, ‘himself and his world’, this is what describes correctly the relation of an empirical self to its own unique world of experience, prakrti thus, evolves into innumerable different worlds reflecting the intelligence of innumerable different purusas. The main difficulty in the Samkhya view however, is to account for the difference that distinguishes one Purusa from another. If Purusa is nothing but pure self-luminous intelligence, then its numerical plurality involving a distinction between one Purusa and another implies an irrational surd which is other than pure self-luminous intelligence.

In other words, Purusa is nothing but intelligence, and then one Purusa does not differ from another. If however, Purus as are many, then there is something that distinguishes one Purusa from another, i.e., something other than its essence as self-luminous intelligence.

If there is this other in every purusa, then, no Purusa is pure Intelligence. In fact, an irrational surd is inconsistent with the very nature of Purusa as self shining intelligence. Hence, consistency will require giving up the idea of Purusa-bahutva or a plurality of Purusas. This is why the Advaita rejects the Samkhya doctrine of Plurality of Purusas substituting in its place the concept of a monistic-Brahman in which plurality appears as a consequence of the action of nescience, avidya or maya. avidya is the irrational sure that makes the one self-luminous Intelligence which is Brahman appear as a plurality of individuals as we find in our phenomenal empirical life or Samsara. Hence Samkhya stripped of its inconsistencies must pass over into the non-dualism of the Advaita.

The same observations apply also mutatis-mutandis to the Samkhya-conception of prakrti as real objective background. If Prakrti in its avyakta or met-empirical state is absolutely indeterminate, then, it can not be talked of even as an existent background of our phenomenal world of experience. If it is non-manifest (avyakta), then, it does not reveal itself as a principle of reality, and therefore, all talk of it as an unknown and unknowable existent background is inconsistent with the affirmation of its nature as avyakta or absolutely indeterminate. Hence, the background which the Samkhya speaks of as source of the world of experience can not be consistently described either as real or as unreal. It is thus nothing but the maya of the Advaitins which is sadasadanirvacya. Hence a consistent Samkhya philosophy must in the last analysis be nothing but the non-dualism of the Advaita, though masquerading under a different name.

## E. The Purva-Mimamsa View of Moksa

While both Nyaya and Samkhya define liberation as a negative state of escape both from pleasures and pains of life, the Purvamimamsa view furnishes a contrast to this negative view of Moksa. According to Purva- Mimamsa the selves are prati-sariram bhinnah or different in different bodies and each though, vibhu or all-pervasive, is both spiritual and material in nature. In respect of its spiritual part, it is the seer, the witnessing subject of experience, and is the object of the recognition ‘I am he’. In respect of its non-spiritual part, it is subject to transformation in the forms of cognitions, pleasures and pains etc. This self is revealed in introspective self-consciousness.

Hence, according to Purva mimamsa, pleasures, pains etc. are modifications of the non-spiritual part of the self and are not functions of the mind. The Purvamimamsa thus differs from Nyaya in supposing that there is a non-spiritual part of the soul, the modifications whereof are the pleasures and pains of our empirical life. For the Naiyayikas and Vaisesikas, however, pleasures and pains are the vises agunas or the specific qualities of the soul though, they are non-essential separable qualities of it. Hence, according to Purvamimamsa, self is bhokta or experiencer of the pleasures and pains of life as the transformation of its non-spiritual part, and such tranformations are without any beginning in time (anadi) and in this sense eternal (nitya). But the place of fruition i.e., (bhogsthana) and the duration of the fruition (bhogakala) are not eternal (anitya). The Purva-mimamsa rejects the doctrine of world creation in time and also of its reabsorption after a lapse of time.

Hence, as the world (iagat) is eternal, the soul’s experience of the world, i.e., its bondage, is also beginningless (anadi) Moksa or liberation, according to PurvaMimamsa is not a nivrtti or cessation from activity but the manifestation or experience of innate eternal happiness in the self (nityasukhabhivyakti) through pravrtti or active discharge of the duties prescribed in the scripture. .

The PurvaMimamsa rejects the Nyaya view of Moksa as cessation of all experience in the self including consciousness. This, according to PurvaMimamsa, is a lapse of the spirit into the unconsciousness of the material object which is joyless as well as painless and is not fit to be a worthy object of attainment or purusartha. Hence, the life of activity for the attainment of ultimate true satisfaction is to be preferred to negative cessation from activity.

The above is a general statement of the PurvaMimamsa view of Moksa. The view of Kumarila Bhatta is set forth in some detail by Parthasarathimisra, in the Sastradipika. Moksa according to Kumarila, is not prapanca-vilava or the negation of the world as the Advaitin's suppose but prapanca-sambandha-vilaya or the negation of self’s attachment to the world. But according to Sankara, prapanca or the world of experience is an illusory creation of nescience or avidya, that is, as such stuff that dreams are made of and dissolves on the awakening of the knowledge of the absolute Reality of Brahman, just as waking dissipates the dream-forms.

But idealists like Sankara who ascribe only a pseudo reality to the world as well as the idealists like Yogacara Buddhists who accept the reality of consciousness only, are alike in the wrong, according to Kumarila Bhatta. The world cannot be dismissed as unreal or as a mere dream-reality. It is a real world that causes our bondage, and liberation is nothing but detachment from the world that binds us to itself. Some (e.g., the Buddhists and the Sankarites) depict Moksa as a state of pure consciousness which is reached by cancellation of all distinctions through eradication of all residual traces of the continuum of experiences of colours, tastes, smell etc..

Some others (e.g., Madhyamikas) speak of Moksa as the cessation of the stream of conscious states as like the extinguishing of a lamp.

All these views, according to Kumarila, are open to the common objection that all of them alike take the world to be unreal and can be negated like an illusion. Kumarila's view is that the world cannot be cancelled or negated, we can only cancel or negate our attachment to the world. According to him, attachment to the world - is three-fold: (i) We are attached to it through our Sarira or body which is bhogavatana or the abode or seat of experiences, (ii) we are also attached to it through our sense-organs which are the instruments of our experience; (iii) lastly, we are also attached to it, because of its furnishing all sorts of objects to be experienced. Through this threefold connection with the world, the individual-self becomes a subject to the experiences of pleasure and pain which is bhogah. bhoga being defined as sukha -duhkha-visayaparoksanubhavah or immediate experience of pleasure and pain, bhoga is a source of a self’s bondage to the world and Moksa is the absolute negation of bondage to the world arising from the three different kinds of bonds. Whether this absolute negation of bondage to the world means also cessation of all experience is a point in respect of which interpreters differ.

In answer to the question - what this atyantikavilayah or absolute negation is - some interpreters say that it is the destruction of previously generated bodies along with their sense-organs and their objects, and the absolute cessation of origination in respect of such bodies as have not yet been generated.

It is further stated that bodies with their sense organs and their objects are generated because of generation of merit and demerit in the self, and therefore the destruction of all such dharma and adharma i.e., merit and demerit-without any possibility of resurgence, will also entail the cessation of bodies which are products of such merit and demerit. Thus, relation to the world through bodies etc. is bondage and the negation of such attachment to the world is Moksa or liberation.

It may be objected that with the cessation of adharma as well as dharma, there must cease also all experience of sukha in the self; such a state, therefore, is apurusartha, i.e., not deserving to be called a desirable state. In answer to this objection, it has been pointed out that this is no defect in the Bhatt a theory of Moksa. ananda or joy that results from dharma or is the effect of dharma in the liberated, is janya-ananda i.e., an effect in time, and as such, it must also have an end in time. Hence such ananda cannot belong to the mukta-state as a nitya or permanent character of it. Such a joy in the mukta-state contradicts the Sruti that speaks of apunaravartti or recurrence of pain in the liberated self.
