But there is in the atm an besides, janva-sukha resulting from merit, and therefore also ceasing to be in course of time, a svabhavika-ananda or the intrinsic joy in the self which remains overpowered in samsara or empirical life, and becomes abhivyakta or manifest as an object of experience, when the self becomes free from samsara or empirical life. In respect of this intrinsic joy in the self such scriptural texts as Brahman - the ultimate Reality is joy itself, are the pramana or evidence.

It may be objected that there are also scriptural texts in proof of sukhabhava or absence of pleasure in the self in the mukta-state. ‘How can the disincarnate free soul be touched or affected by a consciousness of the pleasurable and the painful’? - says Sruti or scripture. This is, no doubt, true. For sukhabhava or the absence of pleasure, that is, spoken of in such Sruti text - is the absence of visayasukha or pleasure that is derived from the objects. Such texts do not mean absence of intrinsic joy in the free self. In fact, if that were the meaning, such texts would contradict the other texts - whose number is far more numerous and which speak of intrinsic joy as an essential character of the free self.

It may be argued, why not interpret that texts relating to intrinsic joy as signifying duhkhabhava or absence of duhkha? The answer is that Ananda Sruti texts are far outweigh in number, such texts as speak of sukhabhava or absence of pleasure in the mukta or the liberatred state. Therefore, according to the cannons or interpretations, anandasruti must be given precedency over sukhabhava Sruti. Further, if ananda or joy be interpreted as mere absence of pain (duhkhabhava), it will be void of all intelligible meaning. Hence the right interpretation is that sukha i.e., happiness is a generic term that applies to the different species of sukha, i.e., visaya-sukha or the joy derived from objects as well as svabhavika-sukha or the intrinsic joy in the self.

It may be objected that a joy in the self that is not a felt or experienced joy, is an absurdity. The liberated self that is, bereft of all the sense organs cannot possibly have experience of joy of any kind. The karanas or the organs of experience lacking or having disappeared in the mukta-state, it cannot be said that this intrinsic joy or ananda in self is self-manifested i.e., does not depend on the activities of the sense-organs for its manifestation. If it were self-manifested, how is it that it does not reveal itself through itself in samsaravastha or empirical life?

In reply to this objection, it may be said that even in empirical life, the innate joy in the self reveals itself as is evidenced by the fact that the self felt very dear to itself by everyone. There cannot be prema or love (of self), if the self were asukhi or unhappy, or if the happiness that exists in the self were avijnata or unfelt.

It may however, be asked whether ourselves in the state of samsara are felt as seats of joy? If the answer be in the affirmative, then, there, being experience of nityananda or eternal bliss in the self even in the state of samsara or bondage to the world of experience, there will be no difference between the samsari or the individual in bonds and the mukta or the individual as free. This will make Moksa or freedom from the bonds of experience to be of no value. Hence, the force of logic, necessitates the conclusion that even unhappy self is an object of love, because it is so perceived.

In fact, the self revealing joy in the self does not manifest itself in samsara or empirical life, because it remains overpowered in the samsara-state. It may be asked however what is this abhibhava or ‘being overpowered’ of that which is of the very nature of manifestation?

If it be said that this abhibhava or prevention of manifestation ceases in the mukta or the liberated state so that the overpowered manifestation comes to manifestation, one must indicate the reason or cause whereby the manifestation that remains subdued in the state of bondage, attains its full nature as manifest in the state of liberation? The answer is what is called the effect or phala as manifestation is vijnana or cognition (of the ananda). Such cognition depends on the activities of the sense-organs. But in the mukta or the liberated individual there are no sense organs, how, it may be asked, in the absence of sense-organs in the liberate state there may be experience of (svabhavika) joy in the self

The reply is that though, the external sense-organs cease in liberated state, yet the internal organ i.e. mind continues even in the state of liberation. This makes the experience of innate joy in the self possible even in the Iiberated state. This view has the support of anandasruti or Scriptural texts that declare the existence of pure bliss in the self and same is proved in respect of intrinsic vijnana or cognition in the self by such Sruti texts “as nahi vijnaturvijriaterviparilopovidyate”. Hence it follows that in the mukta - or liberated-state the self has perceptual experience by the mind of the supreme bliss that belongs to it by nature. Thus it has been said that the intrinsic intelligence or consciousness that belongs to the self as also the bliss that characterises self and such other attributes as self’s eternality and omnipresence, never become separated from the self.

It has been stated that the individual with the wearing a way of karma and consequent absence of the cause of bondage, becomes liberated. But this is a negative statement of Moksa and does not explain its positive character of eternality . The meaning is that Moksa is a positive state characterised by eternality, omnipresence, intrinsic consciousness and intrinsic bliss.

According to a second view, i.e., the view of some other interpreters - Moksa by its very meaning signifies a negative state, a state of release. As regards the positive character of ananda etc. by which this negative state is described, they are only ornamental additions borrowed from the views of others. In the Mukta or released state, there cannot be any experience of ananda lacking as it does, the instruments of such experience. It cannot be said that the mind continues in the state of release, for, that is against the deliverance of the scriptures which describe, the released state as mindless and speechless (amano’vak iti).

What has been said about “nahivijnaturvijnater viparilopo vidyate” i.e., consciousness as cognition never ceasing in a subject of consciousness that is meant to convey that the power of knowing or cognition never ceases in a subject that has been attained the state of release and not actual knowing. Even in the state of susupti or dreamless sleep, one must assume actual knowing - (and not merely the power of knowing) - continuing in such a state. But this is contradicted by the deliverance of experience, there being no actual knowing in the susupti state according to the evidence of introspection, Sruti also supports this view and we have such Sruti texts declaring the muktatma as ‘not seeing all this or not seeing all this, even though it is seen” - and so also other texts declaring that there is no termination of power of seeing in the seer, nor the power of hearing in the hearer etc. . In all such texts the cessation spoken of is the cessation or disappearance of their respective objects from the respective fields of the perception of the different senses and not of the power of knowing or vijnana in its generic character (vijnanasamanva) which is common to all the different kinds of perception. It follows that in the absence of the sense organs with their respective objects the various specific experiences through the different senses such as the perception of colour, perception of taste etc. must cease to function in the state of release, as they do in the state of susupti as dreamless-sleep. And just as in the latter i.e., in the state of susupti i.e. dreamless sleep, though the experiences cease, the power of or capacity for such experiences nevertheless, continues, so also, though, vijnana or consciousness as actual experience ceases in the mukta-state, the capacity for or power of such experience continues in the self even in the state of release. This is all that is meant by Sruti text, describing the mukta state as one of the vijnanaghanah etc. and the same applies to ananda-sruti. In the mukta state, what remains is not the actual experience of joy but the capacity for such experience.

As regards the simile of the sun as svaprakasa or self-luminous, that is, usually cited for in the proof of the intrinsic self-manifestation of the self, one must explain what one means by the Sun’s self-illumination. If it means timirotsaranam or the removal of darkness, that is always there (in the Sun), sun-light being by nature darkness removing. If however, the Sun’s self illumination means jnana-jankatvam or generating or making possible the apprehension of objects that is not an eternal feature of sunlight, it does not always produce the cognition of all objects in every body.

When the other necessary conditions of cognitions are fulfilled, the sunlight reveals objects to cognise. Hence, when other conditions remain unfulfilled, the sunlight possesses only the samarthya or capacity to reveal objects to cogniser. So is it in the mukta-state that the released individual in the absence of the instruments of experience possesses only a capacity for experience, but no actual experience etc. Thus, it has been said in the susupti or mukta-state, even though there is no actual experience of any kind, such as, seeing, hearing etc. yet the sakti or capacity for seeing, hearing etc., always continues to be. Such capacity in the self is indestructible. Therefore what is meant by non-cessation of jnana or consciousness in the individual is the non-cessation of the capacity for jnana or experience in the released individual.

It may be said that in the mukta-state though, there is no visaya-jnana there may yet, the self knowledge (atma-jnana) because the self, which is visay a or object of atma-jnana is still present, and there is also the mind, the organ of self-knowledge, and there besides, jnanasamarthya or capacity for knowledge in the self.

In reply to it, it is pointed out that though there is no mind even in the released state and therefore the jnana-sadhana or the instruments of self knowledge being absent, the question of manasa-pratyaksa or internal perception of the self by the mind in the mukta state does not arise. Therefore the mukta-state is an unconscious joyless state in which only the sakti or capacity for consciousness and for enjoyment of bliss remains.

The Prabhakara view may also be briefly referred to here. Though, the Prabhakaras agrees with Kumarila Bhatt a in considering the released state to be one of cessation of all connection with physical body, yet, while according to Kumarila, this cessation means nitya svabhavika-sukhabhavyakti in atman, according to the Prabhakaras it is a state of svarupavasthanam or self repose of the spiritin which through complete destruction of all merit and demerit in the self, there is neither jnana, nor any pleasure or pain in the self. The Prabhakara view is thus indistinguishable from the view of the Nyaya-vaisesikas.

## F. The Advaita Conception of Moksa

(The views of Sankaracarya and his followers) We have so far considered three orthodox views of moksa, viz. that of Nyaya, Samkhya and Mimamsa. Both Nyaya and Purva-mimamsa, consider bandha or bondage to be paramarthika or real and due to a real relation between the self and the non-self, though the relation is ascribed to some kind of nescience or avidya. Moksa contrary wise, according to both, is the termination of this unholy union by tattva-jnana or the knowledge of the true nature of self and non-self. Thus, according to both, liberation is a real self freeing from a real bondage of the self. Samkhya however, holds that the self is eternally free by its very nature and is never in bondage at any time. And yet, according to Samkhya, both bondage and liberation are real in the sense that both appertain to the empirical self generated in prakrti through cic-chaya-patti in prakrti i.e., a reflection of Purusa in prakrti. It is this reflection of Purusa in prakrti that gets identified with the different volutes of Prakrti and thereby becomes an empirical self engaged in the activities of mundane life and suffering of all the ills of embodied earthly existence. Moksa is the dissolution of this empirical self back into the Avyakta Prakrti as the non-manifest, background of an objective world of experience. Bandha and Moksa thus, according to Samkhya, though real, do not concern the Purusa as the transcendental subject, which, as asanga or eternally unattached and nitya-mukta-eternally free, is never either in bondage or liberated from bondage in the strict sense of the term. In Advaita Vedanta, however, we have a complete repudiation of the Reality both of bondage and liberation. The Advaita like Samkhya, rests its own case on the authority of such Upanisadic texts as “asango hyayam purusah, advaya evayamatma sanmatro nityah suddho buddho satyo mukto etc.Since according to these texts as “tattvamasi” , “aham brahmasmi” , “ayamatma brahma etc. the jiva or the individual is declared to be identical with Brahman, the individual must be eternally free as the Brahman is, and his so called bondage as an embodied being must be regarded as an illusory appearance.

Madhvacarya in his Sarva Darsana Samgraha, while explaining the Sankara view of bandha as illusory, says that the case of jiva in bondage is exactly on the parallel with that of a prince who is brought up by uncivilised savages and at least, realises his own status when told of his royal parentage or that of the prince who is brought up among low people. In other words, the self, in itself pure and free, by falsely identifying itself with mahat, buddhi and ahamkara etc. appears as an embodied being suffering the ills of samsara, though in reality, the afflictions of the empirical life cannot touch or contaminate its intrinsic essence .

Madhvacarya also raises the same issue in the “Vivarna Prameva Samgraha” and proves the aparamarthikatva or unreality of bondage by refuting the views of those who hold that bondage is paramarthika or real. The very fact that bandha or bondage is terminated by knowledge (jnananivartyatvad) presupposes that bandha or bondage is a mode of nescience-stuff (avidyatmakatvam). In fact, about bondage, we have to consider whether it is paramarthika (real) or aparamarthika (merely illusory appearance). If bondage be metaphysically real, it could not be terminated by knowledge. If however, some persist in saying that bondage is paramarthika (real), though terminable by knowledge as in Nyaya and Purva Mimamsa, the question they have to answer is, whether jnana or knowledge that terminates the real bondage produces this additional character (atisayam) in the form of bandhanivrtti or cessation of bondage in the jnanavisaya or the object of the knowledge or in the jnanasraya or in the subject knowing i.e., the knower?

As regards the first alternative, i.e., the atisaya viz. cessation of bondage being generated in the object known, what is this nivrtti or cessation? Is it the cessation of the self itself that is in bondage or the cessation of the attributes of the self? Or, the cessation of such attributes of the self as being an agent of action (kartrtva) being an experiencer (bhoktrtva) etc. which are incompatible with pure knowledge as homogenous continuum of consciousness or lastly cessation of ignorance or absence of knowledge in respect of the object known? It cannot be any of the first three of the four above mentioned alternatives. In a multi-coloured half-ripe mango, the cognition of the green portion of it does not entail the negation or sublation of the portion that is yellow. Nor does the cognition of the green portion as green entails the negation of the green or the negation of its taste.

As regards the fourth alternative viz. the nivrtti or cessation of the ignorance in respect of the object, this is our own view (Advaita), and therefore cannot be urged as an objection to our position. As regards the other alternatives that the atisaya viz. nivrtti or cessation is generated in the asraya or the knower, the subject knowing, the question has to be answered whether this ‘atisaya’ in the form of nivrtti that is generated in the knower means nivrtti or cessation of the asraya or knower itself or cessation of the attributes of knower (Nyaya- Prabhakara) or again, cessation of such attributes as belong to both i.e., the knower and the known? The first alternative is not tenable for the obvious reason that it will mean the cessation of the knower itself every moment that such knowledge arises. The second alternative does not square with the facts of experience when one cognises an object such as a jar (ghata), one’s properties or qualities as knower do not thereby cease. Nor can the third alternative be right. Even when there is knowledge of the sarira i.e., body etc. there is no cessation thereby of the self’s (knower’s) relation to the body etc.

It may be urged however, that in view of the express scriptural declaration , He that knows thee passes beyond death (i.e., attains immortality), it follows by implication that bondage even if real, can be annulled by knowledge, Sruti or scripture itself being the pramana or proof of it. The answer to this objection is that nowhere in scriptures we have any express declaration that bondage is real or unreal. Therefore, in order to account for the cancellation of the bondage by knowledge as declared in the Sruti, we have to presume that bondage is of nescience-stuff. This is a legitimate presumption. The Purva Mimamsakas also assume a non-natural energy or power generated by a sacrifice performed at a certain time in order to explain the effect of heavenly bliss which the performance of the sacrifice brings on at a distant date. This non-natural energy or apurva released by the temporal act of sacrifice, they consider to be a legitimate assumption as otherwise the effect of heavenly bliss which the temporal sacrificial act bring on at a distant future date will be inexplicable. The Purva mimamsakas consider in other words, the apurva or the non-natural energy released by the sacrifice to be the link between the present act of sacrifice and its effect of heavenly bliss in the distant future. In the same way, though, there is an express declaration in the scriptures about the reality and the non-reality of the bondage, yet, they consider the unreality of bondage as being a nescience stuff as legitimate assumption as otherwise such scriptural texts as declare that bondage is cancelled by knowledge would be inexplicable. Knowledge can cancel only the illusory or false, i.e., only that which is a creation of nescience. Thus, it is established (aparmarthikatya) i.e., lack of ultimate reality in respect of bondage.

It follows from the above that our limitations i.e., our bondage as an embodied being, limited by a material world is of the nature of an illusion, that is, it has the same metaphysical status as that of a snake in the snake rope illusion (sarpa-rajju) or that of the piece of silver in the illusion of silver in a mother of pearl on the sea beach and mistake it for a piece of silver. The silver that we mistake it to be, is not the silver that is desantariya or kdlantariya silver, i.e., the silver that we saw some time ago in the silver-smith’s shop. We see a piece of silver here and now, lying before us in the locus of the mother of pearl, we do not see our seeing, we see an extra mental objective silver lying before us here, at this very moment. It cannot be said that it is a silver seen elsewhere, wrongly transported from its original location and super-imposed on the locus of the mother of pearl before us. For, if it were so, it would appear in the character of silver seen elsewhere at a different time, but it appears as silver here and now, and not with any other spatio-temporal location elsewhere. Nor can it be said that the silver seen elsewhere and at a different time is simply forgotten (smrti-pramosa) in respect of its original spatial-temporal location. A silver bereft of its spatio-temporal location is not a particular piece of silver but the universal of silveriness (rajatatva). i.e., common to all particular silver and rajatatva or silveriness as a universal cannot be transported bodily to the locus of the mother of pearl, lying before us - (nirvisesasamanyasyasiddhatvat, i.e., universal without spatio-temporal particularisation being impossible). Hence we have to conceive that the silver we see before us here and now is an objective silver projected by nescience. In fact, though we saw a mother of pearl, yet on account of the presence of dos a in us such as lobha (greed) etc., we saw the mother of pearl only in respect of its resemblance in lusture to a piece of silver losing sight of the other characteristic that distinguish it from the silver of our empirical life . It is thus ajnana or non-knowledge of the adhisthana or substratum i.e., the mother of pearl that creates the illusory silver that we see. When however, we pick it up and discover a characteristic which marks it out as a mother of pearl quite distinct from the silver we know in our ordinary life, the illusory silver vanishes and the illusion ceases with it. In the same way, the illusion of deha or sarira as the atman is a creation of nescience and with the awakening of true knowledge of our-selves as Brahman, the Absolute Reality, not only the illusion but also the illusory object viz. the sarira and the material world of which sarira is perceived as an integral part, all vanish as figments of nescience.

The points are to be noted in the above account, viz. (i) that, since, bondage is a false appearance due to false identification of self with a physical body which is a part and parcel of material world, is not merely the identification that is false but also the body itself and the material world of which the body is a part is also false creation of nescience, (ii) that, this falsity is other than reality as well as unreality, and in this sense, unspeakable (sadasadanirvacya). By false, the Sankarite does not mean the as at or the absolutely unreal, nothing can be false which is not pratita or pratiti-yogya anywhere. A vandhyaputra or child of a barren mother, is a logical impossibility and is nowhere seen, it thus, cannot be false. The false in other words, is what is pratita or actually experienced, and yet, does not exist for all the three periods of time, past, present and future, in the very locus in which it is pratita or experienced as existing. Consider e.g., silver, that is misperceived in the locus of the mother of pearl. As soon as, we realised that it is a mother of pearl, we also take it to have never been, not capable of being at any time a piece of silver. Thus, the silver that was seen is negated for all the three periods of time, past, present and future, in the locus in which it was seen. We say that it was not silver even when we perceived it a silver lying before us. The Naiyayikas say that the illusion concerns not the object that is wrongly perceived, but the subjective cognition of the object. The object remains just what it is, the illusion attaches to our cognition or miscognitions of it, the objection to this, is that we never know an illusion to be an illusion, except, through a subsequent experience of correction, and correction is never a rejection of the illusory experience as experience. Correction does not say that there was no experience of the snake. What correction rejects is not the illusory experience qua experience but only the object that appeared in the experience. It rejects, in other words, not the experience of silver, it does not say that silver was not experienced before correction ; it only says that silver that was experienced was an illusory silver, i.e., a silver though experienced as existing in the mother of pearl, yet, in fact, non-existent for all the three periods of time, past, present and future. That is why, mithyatva or falsity is defined in the Advaita siddhiof Madhusudana Sarasvati as ‘prati-pannopadhautraikalika- nisedhapratiyogityam’ i.e., that which stands negated for all the three periods of time in the very locus (upadhi) in which it is experienced as existing is what we mean by the false (anrta). In this sense, the sarira and the jagat which comprises the sarira as a part of itself stands eternally negated in the very self with which it appears falsely identified.

It may be said however, that an illusory object is not at par with the objects of our normal waking experience. An illusory object such as illusory silver that is wrongly perceived in the locus of a mother of pearl lasts only as long as the illusion lasts. When the discovery of the real character of the object-the mother of pearl - in the present instance, cancels the illusion, the illusory object, that is the silver, vanishes. But, in respect of objects of our normal waking experience nothing like this happens. For example, I see the table on which I am writing as an object of my vision. If I close my eyes, the table is no longer seen, but this does not mean that along with my not seeing the table, the table also vanishes. Further, an illusory object is private, i.e., a subjective individual appearance. The illusory silver appears only to a person who is in the grip of the illusion. It does not appear to other people. To other people, the object appears as a mother of pearl and not a piece of silver. But it is quite different as regards the objects of our normal waking experience. A table appears to me as a table and also as such to other people. Objects of our normal experience are public objects, they appear the same to all individuals. Hence, the Advaitin in classing ordinary objects of experience with illusory objects is, in fact, obliterating the distinction between the real and the illusory. The Advaitin however, in reply, points out that none of the points raised by the critics bears strict examination. The mere fact that an object qua - object continues to be the same even after our experience ceases, is no proof of its reality. Nor is the public character of the object a guarantee of its paramarthika being, i.e., Ultimate Being. Consider the Sun, for example. It is seen to be of the size of a yellow disc rising from the horizon just behind the tree at a distance that appears to be less than a furlong and in midday, it appears over head as a glowing orb, at a distance of less than half a mile. This appearance is not a private property of the Sun, but is the same to everybody and yet, Science will say that this appearance is an illusion and that the sun is very much larger than the earth and is millions of miles away from us. Therefore, if this persistent appearance of the Sun as a yellow disc is not proof of its real size, even though it appears as such to everybody. The same may be true of the world of objects. In fact, the drsyatva or objectivity is a property that is common to empirically real objects and objectivity is other than the subject or the self which is drasta or seer. Since the reality of self as seer is established both by evidence of scripture and the impossibility of denying it without thereby concealing its reality. It follows that objectivity as other than the self as the drasta or seer is other than reality. It is however, not unreality or nothing, but it appears as object to self. Hence, it is other than reality which is self, and other than unreality which is pure void (sunya) and in this sense unspeakable (anirvacya)

The Vedantaparibhasa describes the rejection of the world as a case of parihrta-parihara. i.e., as cancellation of negation of what is not. In the correction of an illusion, for example, such as, that of a snake in a rope, the correction rejects what already is not. The snake is not really, there in the locus of the rope and the correction is nothing but reaffirmation of its non-existence in the locus for all the three periods of time. And the same holds as regards moksa as brahmatva ikatva vijnana or realization of the self’s oneness with Brahman or the Absolute Reality. The self is eternally one with Brahman, and Moksa. as the realization of this eternal identity, is a case of prapta-prapti or rediscovery of what was never lost. Just as in the case of a missed golden ornament, that is, on one’s own person, there is a sense of rediscovery of what was never really lost, when one discovers it on one’s own person at the instance of somebody else (who points it out to him). So in the case of Brahman realization or awakening to one’s own identity with Brahman from the evidence of scriptural declaration.

Vidyaranya Swami says in this connection that Moksa or liberation being without beginning and without end is not an object to be achieved by such action as upasana or worship.

The beginninglessness of liberation is established by such Sruti texts which declare that ‘the free becomes freed (as it were), though Brahman itself, He becomes Brahman. And the indestructibility i.e., avinasitva is proved by such Sruti texts as declare that by knowledge he enjoys immortality i.e., being merged in Brahman (brahma samstha), he reaches immortality. It is also clear from above that the arguments that follow as corollaries from the evidence of such scriptural declaration may be used in proof of the thesis that liberation being without beginning and without end cannot be an effect of actions such as worship etc. If Moksa were to have a beginning in time, it would also have an end in time which means that the liberated would return again to the bondage of the mundane life. This will take away all real sense from the word Moksa, and liberation would have only a figurative meaning in that case. Thus, if Moksa be kriyasadhya or attainable by action, then like the fruit of svarga-sukha or heavenly bliss etc. how is it possible to do away with its augmentation and diminution in the form of connection with a body, the sense-organs etc.

Nor can it be said that the fruits of works are being characterised by vaicitrya or diversity (both qualitative and quantitative). Liberation also in respect of the disembodied spirit must be similarly characterised by differences (qualitative and quantitative). Absence of relation to a body being the svabhava or the very nature of the self, the question of the self coming into a relation to body does not arise. In fact, the self is indivisible and devoid of parts. There cannot be a relation of samavaya or inherence between the self and the body. For, the self does not stand in relation of a samavayikarana or inherent cause to the body. Nor is the self a universal that inheres in the body as its particular instance and the same reasons disprove a relation of tadatmya or unity in- difference in the sense of bheda-sahisnu-abheda or identity that also admits of some kind of difference (as Kumarila Bhatta and his followers hold). Therefore, it follows that in the absence of a vastava sambandha or real relation between self and body, the disincarnate nature of the self is considered to be its svabhava or intrinsic nature, and its embodied being to be a creation of nescience.

Says Madhvacarya in his Sarva darsana Samgrah that the self being eternally free, Moksa or liberation is an eternal possession of the self. How is it possible then for the self to attain a freedom which is already in its possession? The answer is that in spite of the self being eternally free (and therefore devoid of all real relation to a body and physical universe with which the body is connected) yet on account of the influence of maya or nescience, it has no realisation of its intrinsic purity (and freedom from all relation to a body) as in the case of milk mixed with water. Just as, in the latter case there is no taste of the milk without the water, so also with the influence of maya or nescience, there is no realization of the self in its purity without relation to the body.

## Chapter III - Moksa In Theistic Schools Of Vedanta

## A. The Ramanujist Conception of Moksa

According to Sankara and his followers, Brahman is the only reality and the world of multiplicity is a false appearance in Brahman, i.e., the creation of nescience, while according to Ramanujist Brahman or the Absolute is a Savisesa reality charactersised by cit and acit (i.e., animate souls and the world of inanimate objects) as its visesanas or attributes. The Ramanujist also subscribes to the principle of Maya. It is, however, according to the Ramanuja’s view, no source of illusory appearances as Sankara holds. Maya, according to the Ramanuja’s view, is the power of the absolute to conjure up reals constituting the universe out of nothing as it were. It is in the same way as the magician by his magical power conjures up various objects to the amazement of his spectators, Ramanuja holds that Brahman is characterised by innumerable auspicious qualitiesand is an Omnipersonality comprising both finite persons and the physical universe as part and parcel of itself. In fact, according to Ramanuja, Brahman is no impersonal identity of being, consciousness and bliss as Sankarites hold. Brahman, in fact, is the Supreme Person characterised by every possible perfection and free only from undesirable qualities.

Scriptural texts that speak of Brahman as unqualified or nirguna mean, according to Ramanuja, the absence of heyagunas or undesirable qualities in Brahman. They do not mean the absence of kalyanagunas or beneficent qualities. In fact, according to Ramanuja, Brahman is nothing but a personal God who is the object of worship by religious people. The Absolute is absolute according to Ramanuja, only as an Omnipersonality mediated by finite personalities. None of the kalyanagunas of the Absolute can be explained without conceiving Brahman as the supreme spirit in necessary relation to finite spirits ; consider, e.g., the kalyanagunas of sarvajnatva-Omni-science, sarvasaktimatva-Omnipotence, mercy etc.What for example, is knowing (jnana) in the All-knowing for, unless it be for the enlightenment of the ignorant finite beings who do not know their real good ? What again, is ‘might’ (sakti) in the Almighty for, unless it be for helping weak, helpless creatures who unaided, are incapable of attaining their goal? Similarly, straight forwardness (ariavam) is inexplicable in God unless it be for overcoming the crookedness of creatures and putting them on the straight path. Nor is mercy in the All-merciful God intelligible unless there are sinful creatures who can be saved only by God’s forgiveness and exercise of mercy.

Thus, each and every one of these beneficent qualities in the Absolute or Brahman as the supreme spirit, presupposes necessary relation to finite spirits. It follows therefore, that Brahman or the Absolute is no impersonal identity of being, consciousness and bliss, but is an Omnipersonality characterised by every possible perfection and beneficent qualities and in necessary relation to finite spirits. Ramanuja thus, calls himself a visistadvaitin i.e., a believer in Brahman as cidacidvisista i.e., a supreme spirit qualified by cit i.e., sentient being and acit i.e., inanimate, non-sentient objects. Since the Absolute spirit as an omnipersonality presupposes necessary relation to finite spirits, and is, what it is in and through such relations, it follows that finite spirits, though visesanas or adjectives of Brahman as the substantive reality, must yet, have eternal reality the visesanas or attributes involved in the very nature of Brahman as visesya or substance. In fact, according to Ramanuja and his followers, it is not merely Brahman that is a unity-in-diversity but every individual self as part and parcel of the All-inclusive Brahman is also a unity-in-diversity (bhedabheda) though, a subordinate and subservient unity within the all-inclusive unity of the whole which is Brahman.

Ramanuja thus rejects the Sankarite view of the finite selves as non-different from Brahman, and being nothing but the impersonal identity of being, consciousness and bliss which is Brahman ; according to Sankara. The Ramanujists contend that the self is not consciousness itself, and nothing but consciousness and bliss as Sankara holds. It is on the contrary that “I” or thinking subject that has consciousness as its quality. According to Ramanuja, in other words, the self is not intelligence itself as the Sankarite holds, but only an intelligent substance possessing intelligence as one of its auspicious qualities. Intelligence however, is no accidental quality of the self as Nyaya-Vaisesikas hold, but is an essential quality thereof which the self can never be without. Consciousness or (Intelligence) is, therefore, an inseparable attribute of the self.

Bondage, according to Ramanuja, is a state of self in which it forgets its true nature as an adjective of the all-inclusive supreme spirit which is Brahman and sets itself up as an independent being. This brings on the miseries of mundane life. Moksa or liberation is self awakening into the realization of one’s true status as an eternally subservient factor of the whole. Consciousness, according to Ramanuja “is svaprakasa in the sense that it reveals itself by its own activity to its own locus, viz., the self. But the self is svavamprakasa or self-revealing in the sense that it reveals itself to itself. In other words, consciousness does not reveal itself to itself but reveals itself to its own locus (i.e., the self). While discussing Ramanuja’s conception of self, S.K. Maitra observes in the Ethics of the Hindus,“The self therefore is the “I” known as the subject of knowledge and it is this self which reveals itself in the state of Moksa. Since it is manifest to itself by itself it is essentially an “I” or a thinking subject, and it is as this revealing “I” or subject that it manifests itself in the Moksa state. It is an unwarranted dogmatism to suppose that; because, the self reveals itself as an “I” therefore it must be implicated in error or ignorance. The self, in its true essence is an “I” and therefore, there can be neither error nor ignorance in the apprehension of it in its essential nature as an “I” (Atohamarthasyaiva jnatrtaya sidhayatah pratyagatmatvam. sa ca pratyagatma muktavapi ahamityeva prakasate, sa sarvatah “aham” ityeva prakasate. na ca “aham” iti prakasamanatvena tasyajnatva-samsaritvadiprasangah........ ajnanam nama svarupa-jnanamanyatha-jnanam viparita-jnanam va. aham ityevatmanah svarupam iti svarupajnanarupo' hampratyayo najnatvamapadayati kutah samsaritvam “Sribhasya”.

It is svavyatirikta prakasakam- that is, it reveals an object other than itself and reveals itself as revealing the external objects not to itself but to its own locus. The self, however, reveals itself to itself and is svaprakasa in this sense. A stone does not reveal itself to itself but only to a conscious subject that has experience of it. A past mental state reveals itself to consciousness not by its own activity but by the activity of the present act of recollection. But consciousness reveals itself by its own activity to its substratum-the self. It however, does not reveal itself to itself. Consciousness however, though svavyatiriktaprakasaka and is also the svaprakasa in the sense of revealing itself by its own activity to its own asrava or substratum viz., the self, and is yet, subject to contraction and expansion in the state of samsara. When however, the self recalls its true status as eternally subservient to the life of the Absolute spirit and becomes free by renouncing self will and pursuing the purpose of the Absolute in creation, the limitations of contraction and expansion disappear and the scales fall off from its vision as it were, so that there is an intuition of Reality as a whole. This is Moksa.It is not very much unlike that of the Ajiva of Jainas when it has attained to the total view point by purgation of the soul, and has not yet passed beyond Loka to Aloka. In common with all schools of Vaisnavism, the Ramanujist subscribes to the monadic nature of the self, though, at the same time, holding that its attribute of consciousness in the liberated state is without spatiotemporal limitations.

It may be noted however, that the Yatindramatadipika speaks of two ways of attaining Moksa viz. (1) Bhakti—the way prescribed for the caste Hindus and (2) Prapatti—the way suitable for those who have no right to read or hear the Vedic teachings i.e., the Sudras. For them, the way laid down is that of Prapatti or complete self surrender. It is pointed out that for the caste-Hindus there must be Karmayoga leading to Jnanayoga and culminating in Bhaktiyoga which alone brings liberation. Thus, in the first place, there must be practice of nitya-naimittika-karmas i.e., disinterested performance of unconditional duties and kamya and nisiddha varjana i.e., eschewing of works for attainment of empirical ends and also of acts that are forbidden as immoral. Karmayoga brings on Jnanayoga or the realization of one’s essential dependence on God as the Hariri or Soul of which the finite spirits are as bodies. And Jnanayoga matures into Bhaktiyoga which consists in the practice of devotion through the astahgavogaas its auxiliary conditions consisting of the practice of yama or restraints and niyama or rules, asana or posture, pranayama or control of the motions of expiration and inspiration, pratyahara or the withdrawal of the sense organs from their objects, dharana- or focusing the mind to some particular object, dhyana or meditation and samadhi full concentration in the inner nature of the self, is a continuous stream of remembrance which is compared to the uninterrupted flow of oil.” And also, such Bhakti is practiced through the sadhana saptakanamely (1) viveka consists in the purification of the body by food that has not yet become impure either on account of species or abode, or adventitious cause, (2) vimoka (mental detachment) consists in eschewing of desires or non-attachment to desires, (3) abhyasa is the repeated practice of mediating upon the perfect object, (4) kriya (or actions) consists in due discharge of five great sacrifices (5) kalyanas or practicing truthfulness, straightforwardness, compassion, liberality, non-violence or harmless-ness and non-covetousness (6) anavasada or non-depression at misfortune, consists also in freedom from dejection, (7) anuddharsa or non-elation at good fortune. Devotion so practiced according to the above seven conditions, brings on realization at the end and is essentially of the nature of an intellectual intuition in which the representation of the ultimate truth becomes a presentation or intuition of the reality, as it were.

We shall go into a detailed account of the Ramanuja’s view of Moksa as delineated in the Yatindramatadipika. According to this text, there are two categories of mumuksu or aspirants after liberation viz. (1) kaivalyapara i.e., those who desire Kaivalya or self autonomy, and (2) moksapara or those who desire liberation from samsara or ills of life. According to this work, the kaivalyapara or aspirants after self-autonomy desire freedom from the grip of prakrti or physical universe and it comes through the way of knowledge in the form of consciousness or intuition of one’s own self without any attachment to prakrti. This self-intuition takes the individual through the path of light (arciradi) into some corner of the domain of the spirit. Such self autonomy is compared to that of forsaken wife that finds shelter in her own self. It is a state of self-realization devoid of God-consciousness. Some say however, that since autonomous-selves are not spoken of as returning to the world in the scriptures ; the self-centered beings have therefore, to be conceived as participating also in the world’s affairs and therefore, both self-conscious and world-conscious. As regards the second category, i.e., moksa-paras or aspirants after liberation, they are again of two kinds , viz. (1) Bhaktas or devotees who seek liberation through Bhaktiyoga and (2) Prapannas or those who seek liberation through complete self-surrender or Prapatti. (A) Bhaktas or devotees are those who are thoroughly conversant with the Vedas, the Vedangas and the Upanisads as also the (Purva & Uttara Mimamsa, and have thereby attained to the realization of the Absolute Reality as other than both finite spirits and inanimate objects and as constituted by bliss, unsurpassable and infinite, and free from all that is undesirable and evil in nature and as the very embodiment of all beneficent qualities, actual and possible. So far as, the way of Bhakti is concerned, even the gods themselves are qualified to choose it for the realisation of Brahman. But, so far as, Sudras or low castes are concerned, they being precluded from the study of Vedas etc. the way suited for them is Prapatti or complete self-surrender to God.

Devotees again are either (1) those who follow the way of sadhana-bhakti or bhakti as means, such as Vyasa etc. or (2) those who follow Sadhyabhakti or bhakti as end such as Sri Pararikusa and others (3) The Prapannas amongst the Moksaparas or aspirants after liberation are those who, feeling completely helpless and finding no otherway, take shelter in God in complete self-surrender. The prapannas are again, of two kinds, viz. (1) those who take shelter in God for the attainment of three relative ends of Dharma, Artha and Kama (traivargikapara) and (2) those who take shelter in God for the attainment of liberation from all the ills of life (Moksapara).

The Moksaparas desire freedom from the ills of transmigratory existence because of realization through the company of saintly people, the utter hollowness of earthly interests and values. Hence the prapanna is one who considers the feet of the Lord Narayana as the only resort, all other means being beyond his capacity to resort to. Self-surrender is open to all without exception while, the way of Bhaktiyoga is open only to the high class and the gods.

Moksapara prapannas are again of two kinds, viz.

(1) Ekanti (2) Parama-ekanti. An Ekanti-Moksaparaprapanna is one who takes shelter in God as the only recourse not merely for attaining liberation from samsara but also for attaining such relative ends as dharma, artha and kama. The meaning is that to him other gods are simply non-existent. And the Parama- Ekanti-Parapanna is one who takes shelter in God as the only recourse for the attainment of no other purpose than that of Jnana (knowledge) and Bhakti (devotion). Such Parama-Ekanti-Prapannas are again of two kinds viz. (1) drptas are those who, believe that the fruits of their works that are already in the course of fruition must be patiently borne till the end of their bodily existence, and (2) artas are those who, impatient of their works wearing away through fruition in course of time, are for immediate liberation through the mercy of God in whom they have taken shelter.

Muktior liberation is described as consisting in entering the domain of the Lord after issuing out in subtle body through the aperture in the crown of the head (brahmarandhra), and crossing the Viraja river-that separates the physical universe from the realm of the spirit. Through the crossing of the Viraja, the subtle body falls off and one assumes an immaterial spiritual body with four arms and is adorned with Brahmic decorations, and is led before Lord Narayana in Vaikuntha where he is taken up by the Lord Himself in His arms and blessed by Him with powers of Lord Narayana Himself excepting the cosmic function of creation, and reabsorption etc. (iagadvayaparavarianasya pratipadanat).

Bhakti is defined in the Sribhasya as dhruvanusmrtireva bhakti sabdenabhidhivate - It is thus, according to Ramanuja, a kind of intellectual intuition or intellection transformed into an intuition by means of continued concentrated meditation. Ramanujist’s conception of Bhakti is thus, a kind of intellectual love of God and must therefore, be distinguished from the Gaudiya conception of Premabhakti in the sense of disinterested pleasure conducive to pleasure or delight of the Lord. In Ramanuja, Bhakti is more a matter of knowledge than a feeling, while, according to the Gaudiya Vaisnavism, Bhakti of the highest order is essentially a feeling of joy, that is, of the nature of an aesthetic sentiment that delights in being a source of delight to the Lord for His own sake.

As regards the Prapannas - those who take to the way of self-surrender and consider the shelter of God to be the only way out of life’s ills. It is to be noted that the desired end i.e., liberation or any of the relative ends does not come to the Prapannas except through God’s krpa or grace. The question is raised in this connection; is God’s grace bestowed on all without discrimination or is it bestowed only on those who have qualified themselves for receiving the grace ?

There is the difference between the two schools of Lokacarya and Venkatesa, as regards the answer to this question. While Venkatesa holds to what is known as markata-kisoranyaya or the way of the young-monkey clasping the mother with its own arms and thus being taken by the mother to a safe retreat on the branches of the tree, Lokacarya subscribes to what is known as mariara-kisoranyaya or the way of the mother-cat towards its kitten. According to Venkatesa, God’s grace comes only to those who have qualified themselves by their own efforts to receive grace just as the young monkey must clasp the mother-monkey in order to be taken to a safe place. According to Lokacarya, however, God’s grace is bestowed indiscriminately on all alike, irrespective of the question of their fitness or otherwise to receive grace, just as, the mother-cat takes her kitten in her own mouth and removes it to a safe place. So, it is with God bestowing His grace. The kitten does not put forth efforts, all is done by the mother-cat for the benefit of the kitten. So, it is with God bestowing His grace for the benefit of His creatures - The Jiva or the individual self does not require to qualify himself to receive His Grace. God’s grace comes to all without exception and without asking.

## B. The Madhvite Conception of Moksa

As we have shown that Sankara, being a pure monist (Advaita) does not admit the reality of anything else apart from Brahman. Ramanuja who is also a monist of a different type (visist advaita) does not dismiss the world as wholly unreal. In fact, Ramanuja seeks to show that there is essential non-difference between Isvara (cit) and the world (acit); but according to Madhvacarya and his school, there is real and eternal difference between Isvara and jiva. This difference is of five kinds, (1) there is difference between Isvara and jiva. (2) there is difference between Is'vara and matter, (3) there is difference between jiva, and matter, (4) there is difference between one jiva and the other, (5) there is difference between one material thing and another.

Is'vara, according to Madhva, is bimba- the original (proto type) while iiva is pratibimba or unconditioned reflection or imitation. But, while Isvara is complete and full in respect of Being, Consciousness and Bliss, the jiva has only limited being, consciousness and blissfulness.

Jiva, according to Madhvas is amsa of Is'vara but while he is a vibhinnamsa or a part which is existentially separate from Brahman as Isvara, the avataras or various lesser manifestations of Isvara are the svarupamsa or consubstantial partial manifestation of Isvarajivas, according to Madhvas, are anupadhika or nirupadhika pratibimba or unconditional imitations of Isvara or God. A pratibimba or imitation is conditioned when it is a reflection in some other adjunct or upadhi. e.g., Sun’s rays reflected in the sky appear as the rainbow. Here the sky is the upadhi or conditioning factor, jivas, the finite individual, however, is nirupadhika or unconditioned reflection or imitation of Isvara (God) and existentially separate from Him. It is however, a partial imitation, not possessing God’s unlimited Being, Consciousness and Bliss completely and fully. There is taratamva or difference of degrees between iivas as partial imitations of God’s unlimited being, consciousness and bliss. Finite beings, thus, can be graded into different classes as imitations of God. This gradationor difference is caused due to the difference in the accumulated fruit of their karmas which varies from one individual to another.

The jivas can be classified into three categories of (1) nitya or eternally free (2) Mukta or liberated jivas or souls like Rsis, gods, Pitrs and men. (3) Baddha or bound the finite individuals who experience the ills of mundane life. These baddha jivas are further divided into three classes of (1) mukti yogya (eligible for liberation) (2) nitya Samsari - i.e., ever bound to empirical life, (3) tamoyogya those finite jivas who deserve hell, because of their ill deeds and darkness of mind, e.g., demons etc..

God, according to Madhva, is efficient cause and not the material cause of this physical universe. He is independent (svatantra)while jivas are dependent (partantra) on God though, existentially separate from Him, yet as partial imitation thereof. As remarked by K. Narain in his ‘outlines of Madhva Philosophy’, “his only intention in calling jiva as His part was to illustrate the point that Isvara is the cause of the existence and the essential nature of jiva just as, a father is the cause of the existence of his son (Putro’ham paramatmanah).

The bondage of the jivas arises from God-forgetfulness and their essential dependence on God. Liberation consists in a change of heart and awakening to one’s essential subservience to God. It comes through worship and Bhakti to Lord Vjsnu who alone is capable of liberating the iivas who are in bondage through His own Benediction (moksasca Visnu prasadamatarena eva labhyate). Same is illustrated by Kathopanisad ‘atman reveals Himself to His devotees. Jayatirtha explicitly mentions in his Yukti-mallika that one who is the cause of bondage, He alone can liberate and the liberation is possible only when He so desires and whom He wishes to liberate and obtain the benediction or grace of God.

K. Narain described the nature of moksa according to Madhva and his school as follows, “unlike the Nyaya Vais'esika system, the nature of emancipation is not only cessation of pain but also consists in the acquisition of positive bliss which constitutes the ultimate nature of iivatman. As elaborately discussed by Jayatirtha in his Tattvaprakasika, the nature of jivatman is sat (truth), cit (consciousness), ananda (bliss) which on account of the psychological and personal principle of avidya remains unmanifested. When the Jiva, after having acquired the requisite bhakti and inana, becomes blessed with the benediction of Is'vara (Is'varaprasad), the obstructing sheeth i.e., avidya is destroyed and the intrinsic blissful quality of the jiva becomes manifest. This manifestation of the real nature of jiva is really the state of moksa, a positive condition overbrimming with bliss”. While however, according to Sankarites, liberation is self-merging in the impersonal Brahman as the identity of being, consciousness and bliss, according to the Madhvas, it is not self-merging in an impersonal absolute, but self-subservience to the Absolute as a personal God and the realisation of the supreme spirit. In fact, the Madhvas interpret the Upanisadic text - ‘tattavamasiti’ as literally meaning ‘that art thou’ as being a misreading, the correct reading being ‘atattvamasiti with akara as lupta or dropped. So the text really means, “thou art not that’, i.e., ‘thou art different from that, and eternally dependent on that. It means, according to them, tvamtat sadrsositi ‘Thou are like unto that’, having Being, consciousness and Bliss, though in limited quantity in common with the ‘that’ which has unlimited being consciousness and bliss. In liberation or Moksa, therefore, the self does not become one with God but realises itself as an eternal servant of God.

While stating the Madhva-stand point in his ‘Bhagavata Sampradaya, B. Upadhyaya mentions four successive stages of Moksa *viz.,* (1) karma ksaya - destruction of all types of actions (good or bad) or Utakranti laya-destruction of birth, arciradimarga - proceeding by the path of arciradi (regions of fire-gods) to reach the supreme abode of Brahman from where there is no return. Bhog-enioyment this enjoyment of liberated soul is of four kinds: Salokya, Samipya, Sarupya and Savujva (Sarsti being counted as a species of Sayujva and not a fifth kind of liberation). Of all the four varieties of Moksa mentioned above, Sayujva is considered to be the highest of all others, according to this system. Though, all souls that enter the abode of the Lord acquire freedom from the bonds of this empirical life, yet Madhva points out the theory of difference in the nature-and status of their Moksa. As remarked by Dasgupta, ‘the acceptance of difference amongst the liberated souls in the states of enjoyment and other privileges forms one of the cardinal doctrine of Madhva’s system; for, if it is not acknowledged, then, the cardinal dualistic doctrine that all individual souls are different from one another would fall.

Dr. K. Narain explains the four types of mukti (mentioned above) on the basis of the conception of gradation as follows: “In the lowest of these four types of mukti, *viz.* Salokva-the soul enters into the abode of the Lord called Vaikuntha. Among those who have, through the blessings of Isvara received entrance in Vaikuntha there are some of higher calibre in the sense that they possess greater knowledge and become more approximate to the Lord. This particular condition in which the mukta jiva obtains proximity to Isvara is termed as Samipya mukti. Again there are certain more blessed souls who while living in Vaikuntha and having proximity with the Lord acquire His very form and appearance which shows that their enjoyment and nature has become similar to that of the Lord, this state of liberation is called sarupyamukti. There is still a higher state of Moksa in which the souls who have acquired the very form of the Lord, become united with Him. This state, according to Madhva, is the highest and the supreme state of emancipation in which the enjoyment and the bliss of the liberated soul becomes absolutely similar to that of Isvara and is known as sayujva mukti.

It may be noted here that the state of Sayujva mukti does not mean the mergining of the liberated souls in the Paramatman as the Sarikarite would hold. Against such a misgiving Vyasa tirtha issues a note of warning in his Nyayamrta and urges that Sayujva mukti should never be interpreted so as to mean total identity.It has been already stated above that the emancipation can be attained through the grace of God alone. Though knowledge has its own importance as means to the attainment of the grace of the Lord, but it does not occupy the ultimate position in the Madhva philosophy. Here, knowledge along with Bhakti is an effective means to bestow the Divine Benediction on the devotee only when the Lord Himself so wishes. According to Jayatirtha in his Tattvaprakasika, the grace of God can be granted only to those who acquire the right knowledge of God. Now the question is how to obtain such knowledge? In reply to this query, we quote from K.Narain’s ‘Outlines of Madhva Philosophy’, ‘the right knowledge can be obtained only by those who have heard from the Srutis the true nature of God or Isvara and have fully meditated over its contents (sravana, manana and nididhya sana). But this hearing, etc. would be impossible without the worship of the Lord which in turn is caused by hearing the greatness of Isvara (mahatmya) alongwith the renunciation of the desires for worldly objects (vairagya) as enjoined in the Sruti.

It may be noted however, that in common with other schools of Vaisnavism the Madhvas equate God with Visnu, but unlike the school of Sri chaitanya they consider Visnu to be fully manifest in Vaikuntha as Narayana with Laxmi as His consort. They consider ‘Laksami Narayana’ form of Visnu to be the highest and reject the Chaitanyite view that Lord Visnu has His highest manifestation in the form of the Gopal Sri Krsna making love to the milk maids of Sri Vrindavana. A Madhva considers this kind of unlawful love-making to be inconsistent with the divinity of Visnu.

In common with the Ramanujist, the Madhvas consider the Lord in the (ais'varya) aspect of transcendental majesty and glory and ignore altogether the madhurya aspectof the Lord that reveals itself (according to Vallabha, Nimbarka and Caitanya,) in the sweet relations of master and servant, of friend and friend, of parent and child and of lover and beloved. In other words, if the Aisvarya or transcendental glory and majesty of the Lord is best expressed by the epithet, ‘Sublime’, it is the sublimity of God *i.e.,* his incomprehensible transcendental perfection that constitutes, according to the Madhvas and the Ramanujists, the essence of divinity. To forget the distance that separates man from God, is according to Madhvites and Ramanujists, a denial of the godliness of God. The devotee can attain liberation only by a complete subservience to God as the supreme spirit of unsurpassable and incomprehensible sublimity and glory. Thus, alone can jiva attain liberation in the sense of the enjoyment of the blissfulness that comes with the worship of Lord Visnu in the form of Laksami Narayana ruling over Vaikuntha-the paradise of the Vaisnavas.

## The Vallabhite Conception of Moksa

According to the Madhvite, jiva is Vibhinnansa of Brahman, or a part of Brahman *i.e.,* existentially separated from Brahman, while according to Vallabhacarya, jiva is svarupari da or svams'a of Brahman , *i.e.,* part and parcel of Brahman as Bhagavan or God - the Supreme spirit. But while Brahman as God, according to the Vallabhite is saccidananda in the sense that all these are the dharmas or attributes that characterise Brahman as God, the jiva is at the will of God tirohitanandarupa. In other words, while Brahman is characterised by infinite bliss and has besides both intelligence and being without limits, the jivas is an amsa or part of God in which through divine will, the aspect of blissfulness is tirohit or in abeyance.

Sarvajnatva or the Omniscience and other qualities of Brahman are, at the will of God in abeyance in the jiva (sarvajnatvadi dharmanam bhagavadicchayaiva tirobhutatvat). Because of the disappearance of the perfections of God in the jiva, there is bhogeccha or the desire for the experience of objects in the jiva.

If, the perfections of God were present in the jiva, there would be no vaicitrva or diversity and contrast in the jiva’s life, and therefore, also no bhoga or experience would be possible. That is why, though the jiva, the part and parcel of God Himself, yet, appears to be in a striking contrast with the perfect personality of God. That is, what we mean by bandha or bondage of the Jiva. In other words, the jiva, is a part and parcel of God Himself, yet, at the will of God, the Sarvajnatva and anandamayatva etc. of God are in abeyance in him and this makes possible in him the desire for bhoga or experience of objects. The jiva is ‘tirohitanandarupa’ and craves for the joy or ananda that is in suspension in him through the experience of objects. The jiva is however, unlike a jadapadartha or material object. A jadapadartha has the property of being only and lacks both the cit (intelligence) and the ananda or blissfulness that characterise God, of which it is a part or amsa. The jiva however, does not lack the intelligence of God, though it lacks God’s sarvainatva *i.e.,* Omniscience, jiva is ‘alpajna’ and not sarvajna’. The bondage of jiva arises from its lack of Omniscience and of blissfulness of God whose part and parcel every jiva is. It is this lack of Omniscience and of the joy that makes jiva a seeker of the experience of objects. This is samsara or bondage of the empirical life. Bala Krsna Bhatta in his Premeyaratnarnava describes the essence of the jiva as follows: The finite individual (jiva) is monadic (anu), is an infinitesimal fraction of Brahman and is non-different from Brahman, its material cause. Monadic jivas shoot forth out of Brahman like sparks from a big fire, jivas or the finite individuals are thus emanations from Brahman (their Immutable and Inexhaustible Source). Like their source or material cause, *viz.,* Brahman, the jivas are also saccidananda svarupa that is of the essence of Being, Consciousness and Bliss ; But when the jivas emante from Brahman, the inherent property of suddha sattva’ or pure sattva and the sattva without raias and tarn as in Brahman becomes divided into minute parts and beocmes attached to the anuijvas *i.e*., in infinitesimal quantities. As a result, the jiva becomes tirohitanandarupa *i.e.,* it has the Brahman’s aspect of ananda or blissfulness in a state of suspension or abeyance. Hence, the jiva is to be defined as a monadic conscious being which, though, a fraction of Brahman yet, has the aspect of blissfulness in Brahman in a state of suspension or non-manifestation. That is why in Sruti the name Jiva is said to stand for bhagavaccidamsa or a fraction of the intelligence of God.

Jivas again are said to be of three different conditions - (1) suddha. (2) samsari. (3) mukta. A jiva is spoken of to be in the condition of being suddha or pure when after shooting forth out of Brahman as a monadic intelligence with the aspect of blissfulness in suspension, but, is yet, without any relation to avidya. Hence the suddha state of the jiva means that state in which all relation to avidya is absent. After this suddha sattya or pure sattya state of the jiva, there arises in it at the will of God, the disappearance of the six godly perfections of yasa. sri etc. Thus arises in the jiva the predicament of bandha or bondage or its sarhsaritva or empirical life.

From amongst the samsaris or unfree jivas, Bhagavan or the Lord endows some with a godly nature. This reveals itself in them as subtle vasanas or predispositions towards a higher spiritual life and qualifies them for mukti or liberation. (Others, however, God leaves to stew in their wicked and demoniac nature). In other words, some amongst the baddha jivas entangled in the toils of mundane life, God chooses for liberation by endowing them with predispositions for a higher life, others however, He leaves to remain for ever in bondage. This is a doctrine of predestination and closely resembles the doctrine of predestination which St. Augustine - the church father - preached as implied in the Christian Conception of the original sin of the first man, which must not go unpunished even though God out of His infinite mercy, may elect some, among the siners, for salvation and re-entrance into the Kingdom of God.

The Vallabhite view, however, is no parallel of the Augustinian view, as we shall show after we have completed our exposition of the Vallabhite conception of bondage and liberation. Bandhaor bondage of which the other name is the samsara of the jiva, *i.e.,* jivasamsara or the empirical life of jiva, is due to avidva or nescience and is beginningless in reference to other effects which arise out of it.

avidya or nescience functions through the five layers of (1) deha - body, (2) indriyas - external senses, (3) antahkarana - inner sense (4) adhyasa - super imposition and (5) svarupa vismrti - forgetfulness of one’s true essence, avidya in these five forms causes the bondage of the jiva and the consequent sufferings. This is why bandha or bondage is called jivasamsara or the empirical life of the jiva. Bondage however, is not a generated effect in time, being anadi or beginningless due to anadi-avidya as already explained above.

Thereafter assuming subtle and gross bodies, the jiva becomes endowed with the characteristics that distinguish empirical life and has experience of death and rebirths etc. But through bhagavatkrpa or God’s grace, he sometimes, is able to contact saintly persons and through them, acquires the five-fold knowledge that destroys the five layers of nescience and thereby attains mukti or liberation which is of the nature of paramananda or the highest bliss.

Science or knowledge (vidya) (which is contradictorily related to avidya) consists of five forms of (1) vairagya (dispassion) (2) samkhya (knowledge of the number of the ultimate objects, (3) yoga (practice of suspension of the mental activities (4) tapah - practice of austerities and intense meditation and (5) bhakti or devotion to Lord Kesava. The five forms of vidya destroy the five avidya parvani or layers of nescience. It is said that so long as these five kinds of vidya remain only ends to be accomplished and without being actually achieved, the individual remains entangled in the toils of samsara or empirical life. After the achievement of the five kinds of science or knowledge, he ceases to be a samsari jiva or a Jiva in bondage and beocmes a liberated being.

Such a liberated being is of two kinds *viz.*

(1 )jivanamukta (2) muktasca. (1) jivanamukta or liberated while continuing to live the embodied life and mukta or liberated or released. Sri Sanaka Sanadana etc. belong to the class of liberated while alive, but those who are dwellers of the realm of God other than that of the pervasive Vaikuntha or paradise, are the muktas or the liberated. Hence Paramamukti *i.e.,* mukti in the highest form is the same as oneness with Brahman and is due to a special act of grace on the part of the Lord.

Some, godly beings, however, through the association with saintly beings develop a liking for the way of bhakti and as a result of bhakti generated in them through the practice of sravana kirtan etc. in accordance with their bhaktimarga, becomes participants in the eternal sports of the Lord and such participation in the Lord’s sports is the liberation, so far as, they are concerned.

We have thus, the three categories of the liberated

(1) Jivanamukta *i.e.,* liberated while alive, (2) Mukta *i.e.,* dwellers in the domain of the Lord other than the Vaikuntha. (3) Paramamukta *i.e.,* participants in the eternal sports of the Lord.

As regards those who are other than godly creatures with aspirations for a higher life, they are all of a demoniac nature and through the domination of avidya on them, are subject to low feelings and passions and are debarred from liberation ; because of the barriers of the low passions etc. to which they are subject. They are thus called asurajivas or demoniac beings. Being subject to their demoniac desires, they become addicted to disreputable lives and thus become reborn into ever lower and lower forms of living beings. They thus remain wedded to the earthly life for ever (never caring for a higher life) Even these, however, are not altogether lost, for, when the Lord desires then, entering the earth and the earthly activities of creatures dedicated to the earthly life, He undoes the work of avidya thereby causing the release of all without exception, and without reference to the efforts of the creatures for release or a more satisfactory life. Thus, reappear even in the Asuras or demoniac beings ‘suddha-jivatva’ or pure creaturehood through destruction, at the will of God, of all the dosas or faults that were the effects of avidya and that constituted the barriers to their liberation. It is to be noted that according to the Vallabhites, Jivas, though conceived as shooting forth out of Brahman as sparks from a big fire, yet they are not existentially separated from Brahman, but remain all along as parts of the all pervasive Brahman. They are, thus, not exactly creations in time, though, we may call them creatures, yet, strictly speaking, they are emanation from the Absolute Reality. In fact, Brahman is both the material and the efficient cause of the jivas, the anujiva, being consubstantial with Brahman and never separated from Him, strictly speaking. The conception of creation of the finite individuals in time is foreign to the Indian view of the jiva. Either creation in time and jivas masquerading as independent individuals are a false appearance, as in the Advaita View, or the jivas are eternally real in their intrinsic essence. If creation in time is to be spoken of at all, as in Nyaya and some other systems, it is in reference to the physical universe that God creates for the ultimate liberation of the jivas from bondage to earth by bhoga or experience of the fruits of their good and bad deeds, jivas are not created neither, according to Ramanuja or Madhvacarya etc. According to the Advaitins again, jivas as distinct from Brahman is a false appearance, and therefore the creation of Jivas is nothing but a figment of nescience. Vallabha as we have seen, is no exception to the general rule of Indian Philosophers. Another point to be noted in this connection is that the concept of eternal damnation is foreign to the Indian religious out-look. Even the Asuri jivas, as we have seen, are not precluded from liberation at the will of God, it being quite within the power of God to undo the work of avidya that acts as inseparable barrier to their release.

In the Augustinian theory of pre-destination we have, however, something quite different. St. Augustine takes for granted that without punishment for the original sin committed by the first man, God would not be a just God, and therefore, for the cause of justice it is necessary that some creatures should be eternally damned for paying the penalty of the first act of sin. Augustine takes it for granted that evil must bring evil in its train and that punishment is the just retribution for an evil doer. In other words, Augustine is only echoing the ideal lying behind *i.e.,* (the law of an eye for an eye and tooth for a tooth). So evil must be met with evil for the sake of upholding justice. It is rather strange that St. Augustine, a devout Christian Saint, should cast into the winds Christ’s express teachings in his Sermon on the Mount. It may be worth while to note here that no Indian Philosopher believes in an eternal damnation of the soul or into a division of the mankind into the elect and the condemned. Even Vallabhacarya after speaking of God as implanting spiritual aspirations in some creatures which become the cause of their liberation and leaving others in the clutches of avidya that makes them more and more earth-minded and degraded-unsays all that he has said by saying at the end that even asuri - demoniac creatures are not beyond liberation through God’s krpa or grace.

Jivas as already stated, are of two kinds - (1) jivas of godly nature (daivah) and (2) jivas of demoniac nature (asurah) godly jivas are again of two kinds, *viz.,* (1) those who follow the way of (marvada bhakti) and (2) those who follow the way of pusti bhakti. With a view to differentiate the devotees of the Maryada way from those of the Pusti-way, God by exercising a special act of favour bestows on the latter the fruit of pusti or spiritual nourishment. As regards the followers of the maryada way, they practise karma, jnana and bhakti - yoga (any two or all of the three) and as prescribed in the Sastras and therefore, are of various classes. Both the Marvada bhaktas & the Pusti bhaktas become liberated in the end*.*

## The Nimbarkist Conception of Moksa

While the Advaitins consider the world to be one with the Supreme Reality, Brahman, having no separate existence apart from it, the Dvaitavadins like Madhva lay equal emphasis on the world and the Highest Reality, Brahman, both having independent existence. The followers of Nimbarka School, however, follow a middle path in as much as they admit the identity of the world with Brahman who is at the same time said to be different from the world in essence. They thus propound the theory of Unity-in-difference or bheda- bheda-vada according to which the Highest Reality Brahman is identical with the world of matter and spirits and yet different from it.

Nimbarka admits three kinds of categories *viz.,* Brahman, the Absolute (to whom he calls God or Vasudeva), cit or finite spirits and acit or non-intelligent matter. These three categories are real and eternal, having independent existence.

Brahman or God is the Highest Reality who is Omniscient, Omnipotent, Inconceivable (by His very nature), cause of the manifestation etc. of the world, capable of being known only through the scriptures. He is one with the universe and yet at the same time different from it. He is also the material and efficient cause of the world in as much as He is the fountain, from which the world of objects and selves spring, though, in essence He remains unchanged and unaffected by the manifestation of the world. Thus the jivas (finite selves) and the world of objects are merely expressions of His infinite power, hence non-different from Him in essence. He is controller of all things and beings and therefore above all and independent of all beings and things. As such, he abides in all, yet, at the same time, is not affected or tainted by individual imperfections, such as birth, deaths etc. He is described as possessing infinite and inconceivable powers. He is both intelligence (cit-svarupa) and knowledge personified. He has distinct form or figure as the Highest Person and as the sweet object has an attraction for his near and dear ones. In fact, it is the sweetness of beloved in Brahman that outweighs the grandeur and glory of his transcendence. It has therefore, been said that He is the repository of infinite bliss ; all joy or bliss, that an individual experiences when he comes in close contact with Him, is the result of outflow from Him.

Cit or jiva, like Brahman, is also of the nature of intelligence. It is subtle and monadic in nature. Though atomic in nature (anu parimana) it pervades the entire body and its pervasion has been explained on the analogy of sandalwoodpaste which remaining in a part of the body soothes the entire body and emits frangrance all over. It has the power of illumination (prakasa) and like a lamp, remaining in one corner of the room illumins the entire room, it illumins the entire body. It has been conceived as amsa or fraction of Brahman.. Though, it is a fraction of Brahman and is also a knower, doer as well as enjoyer, yet, it is under the complete control of Brahman and as such, is devoid of His power of creation, maintenance and dissolution. It is eternaland enduring and even in the state of liberation, it has its individual being. Being finite and monadic, it is innumerable in number, different in different bodies.

Acit or non-intelligent world is however, altogether different from Brahman and jiva by its very nature. Both, Brahman and jiva are eternal categories and spiritual by nature, acit or the world is ever-changing material category that constitutes the entire physical universe. This is, despite the fact that it is Brahman who is not only the support of the world of pluralities; it is He who transforms Himself as the world in exercise of the super natural powers. In fact, the non-intelligent world of matter has sprung forth from Him.
