Nimbarka quotes from scriptures to support his view that Brahman, though formless in His Essence, assumes forms and manifests Himself as the world (acit). He gives the analogy of a serpent which, when coiled, appears as different but, is infact, non-different from its elongated form likewise, Brahman when assumes form, appears as world but in essence, He remains formless as ever. The acit is said to be of three kinds, *viz.*(1) Prakrta *i.e.,* what comes out of prakrti as material world of objects, (2) Aprakrta or what is non-intelligent but not prakrtaja or evolved out of prakrti and kala or time. The Prakrta- acit is conceived as the material cause of all objects that constitutes the physical universe, while Aprakrta-acit or immaterial acit constitutes the realm of God, out of which temples and other objects connected with divinity are made. Kala or time has been conceived as a kind of acit tattva which controls the evolution of the universe. It is one and eternal; but due to upadhi. it appears as non-eternal and many.

So far we have discussed the nature of the three categories viz., Brahman, jiva (cit) and jagat (acit) in the absolute or exclusive manner. Now, let us show the relative difference and non-difference between Brahman and cit (jiva) between Brahman and (acit) i.e., (jagat).

Let us consider, for example, the relation between Brahman and cit. (jiva) Brahman is the cause while cit or finite individual is an effect of Brahman. There is always difference of some kind or other, between cause and its effect. Similarly, Brahman is the whole (amsi), while cit or jiva is arhsa or a part. Brahman is the upasya or object of worship, while jiva is the upasaka or worshipper who worships. Brahman is the jneya or object of knowledge while jiva is the seeker or knower (jnata) further, Brahman dwells in the heart of every jiva as its inward controller, thus, the jiva is as it were, the dwelling place of Brahman; and between the resident and the residence, there is always difference. Moreover, jiva is subject to the control of Brahman in all its functions and activities; between the agent that controls, there is always a difference. All these examples clearly, establish a relation of difference between Brahman and cit (jiva).

Let us now consider a relation between Brahman which is the cause and acit (jagat) i.e., non-intelligent world; which is the effect. There is the difference that characterises the relation of cause and effect. Further, the world is only an amsa or part of Brahman, while Brahman is amsi or whole that comprises the part which is the world and also much else. Again Brahman is jnanasvarupa or of the nature of consciousness, or knowledge and is immaterial, ‘nitya, suddha. buddha’ *ie.,* eternally pure and intelligent, while jagat or the world is unconscious, material, impure and non­intelligent. All this establishes a difference between Brahman and jagat.

The difference that is shown above to hold between jiva, jagat and Brahman is according to Nimbarka, both eternal and a part of their respective nature (svabhavika). But just as, the difference is part and parcel of their respective nature, so also is an intrinsic non-difference between them. Let us consider, for example, Brahman and jiva (cit). There is a relation of cause and effect between Brahman and cit (or jiva); and between cause and effect there cannot be absolute difference. While the effect is in a way distinct from the cause, it is also non-different from it as being the manifest condition of what remains non-manifest as the cause, Let us take the case of different ornaments that are made out of a precious metal such as gold. Though the ornaments as effects are different from one another in their shapes and uses, they yet, are all made of the same material and have no separate existence apart from it.

The same is true of Brahman as the cause and jivas as the effect. Though jivas may differ from one another in respect of their powers and capacities, yet they are not apart from Brahman as their cause. So far the relation of Brahman and jagat (amt) is concerned, we can deduce from above as a natural corollary of what has already been proved, jagat or the world which though acit or non-intelligent, has yet Brahman as its material cause. In fact, in so far as Brahman is the amsi or the whole of which jiva and jagat are amsa or parts, the relation between Brahman and the latter can not be either one of pure bheda or of pure abheda, but must be a relation of bheda and abheda. The relation of difference and non-difference between Brahman, jiva and jagat as lias been shown above is, according to Nimbarka due to their intrinsic or svabhavika natures and not due to upadhis or extrinsic nature as Bhaskara holds. We can cite any number of examples of difference-in-non­difference in nature, *viz.* between fire and its sparks, between the snake and its coiled form, between the sun and its rays, between the sea and its waves.

As in the present context we are concerned with liberation alone, let us confine ourselves to the examination of the nature of finite individuals as samsari. According to Nimbarka, it has been admitted that t he finite individual in samsara is covered by a veil of ajnnana as a result of which he is not conscious of his real nature. The veil of nescience, technically, called unudimaya is responsible for his bondage and consequent sufferings of earthly life. There is difference of opinion as to how this veil of ignorance can be removed. In almost, all the Vaisnavite systems, grace of God has been considered essential for removal of this veil of ignorance.

Though, the grace of Lord is granted to everyone, without exception; but it is not so, as we find in actual experience. It may be noted that the benediction or grace ul (iod comes to one and all but, being a reciprocal in its nature, it can be realised only by those finite individuals who aspire to be liberated and toil hard to get rid of this voil of avidya .

Nimbarka being no exception to the rule, admits the same fact in his Vedant Kamadhenu (popularly known as Dasa-Sloki) and thus speaks of two categories of finite individual *viz.* (1) baddha or those who are bound to the ills of this empirical life and (2) muktas or those who are liberated from bonds of this world. Purusottama - an exponent of this school, explicitly explains it, in his Vedant Ratna Manjusa and admits the grace of God as the only tenable remedy for the removal of the veil of ignorance and quotes from scriptures that grace of God is beneficient to whom He elects to be liberated.

Further, he defines the categories of baddha and mukta iivas or finite individuals, baddhas. he says, are those finite individuals who are completely attached to the pleasures and pains of the empirical life and forgot their true nature of the self or rather wrongly consider the non-self to be their real self. Even such baddha or bound finite individuals are also of two types as (1) mumuksu and bubhuksu mumuksavah or the finite individuals are those who aspire after liberation by abnegating the sufferings of this mundane life and try hard to get rid of this cycles of births and deaths. These mumuksus are also of two kinds *viz.*

(2) bhagavad-bhava patti laksanamuktikama and

(2) niasvarupapattikama. That is to say, those who aspire to identify with bliss and consciousness of God, or aspire to share the powers of God and thereby enjoying in the very essence of God are called as bhagavad- bhavapattilaksana mukti kama mumuksavah, secondly, those who only aim at regaining the true nature of the self are called niiasvarupapattikama mumuksavah . Further, the bubhuksavah are those finite individuals who are desirous of having sensual enjoyments of this empirical life only. They are also of two kinds (1) bhavisrevaskah and (2) nitvasamsarinah. In other words, those finite individuals who lead a life in such a way as to enjoy a better one in future are known as bhavisrevaskara-bubhuksavah,on the other hand, those who have no future object of enjoyment here or hereafter, are completely attached to the enjoyments of earthly life and remain satisfied in being eternally in bondage are called nitya samsarinah. So far we have discussed the baddha jivas. Now let us explain muktas-liberated individuals. They are classified under two heads *viz.*

(1) nitya-muktas or eternally free souls (2) muktas- liberated or released from the empirical life. In other words, muktas or liberated finite individuals of the first variety are those who are free from cycles of births and deaths and are eternally immortal and always partake in the bliss of the Lord by His Sweet association and kind darsana etc., such aspirants constantly engage themselves in worshipping Him. They are further divided into two categories *viz.* (1) anantarvah such as kirita, kataka, kundala etc. which are also conceived as nitva-mukta jiva or eternally free souls, and (2) parsadah - eternal attendants of the Lord such as Visvaksena and Guruda etc.

The second variety of mukta jivas are those finite individuals who are free from the bonds of the empirical life and its consequent sufferings and as such they enjoy liberated life. These muktas or liberated individuals are also of two types *viz.* (1) those who having attained union with God, share in the powers and bliss of the Lord exclusively, are called niratis'avanandarupa bhagavadbhavapattilaksana muktivantah and (2) those who remain contended only by their restoration to their original state of the true self are called svasvarupa nandapattimatrena santustah.

The jivas who have achieved liberation by the grace of God reach a stage when they feel themselves one (sadharmya) with God. The feeling of their independent existence is replaced by one of union with Him.

As attained onenesswith God or (the Lord), he has no action, no desire, no motive, independent of Him, as he feels himself as a constituent part of the Lord. He abides in Him, partakes of his Bliss and divine powers. In Vedanta Parijata Saurabha, Brahma-saksatkara and its fruits are described as following, with the intuition of the real nature of Brahman as the Absolute Reality, the worshipper (upasaka) attains samya or similarity of nature with Brahman. This is described as a condition in which the individual with the vision (darsana) of Brahman as a source of all that is, including even Brahman the world creator, becomes free from sin and merit as well as from fear and as sinless attains samya or similarity of nature with Brahman.

This is the ultimate goal of life, the Journey-End. The way to attain this end of life, according to Nimbarka is the way of Prapatti *i.e.,* complete self surrender of the aspirant to Lord Krsna (Nanyagatih krsnapadaravindat). This prapatti develops only in those sadhakas or aspirants who forgetting every thing else, are engaged in continuous rememberance of Lord Vasudeva. It is illustrated by the analogy of uninterrupted flow of the river Ganges. Further, it may be added that those who have taken refuge in the Lord by admitting their inability of following any way of sadhana such as jnanavoga etc. are the real prapannas or the followers of the way of prapatti.

Even, those who have surrendered themselves completely to the will of the spiritual teacher (Gurudeva) like an innocent child, and is incapable of practising the ways or means even of prapatti may also be considered as true aspirants in the way of prapatti.

## The Gaudiya Conception of Mukti

Historically speaking, Gaudiya Vaisnavism propounded by Sri Caitanya is an off-shoot of Madhva School, Caitanya being initiated to Vaisnava faith by Is'vara Puri and Kes'ava Bharati,but looking from doctrinal point of view Gaudiya Vaisnavism differs fundamentally from its parent stream namely, Madhva School of Vaisnavism in as much as the former postulates a doctrine of inconceivable dualism-cum-non-dualism and rejects the latter’s dualism. In the foregoing pages while reviewing the philosophical treatments of Ramanuja, Madhva, Vallabha and Nimbarka Schools of Vaisnavism, we have pointed out how, each school repudiates the view of the other rival schools in order to establish his own view point. In that context we referred to the criticism by Nimbarka of Madhva’s dualism, Ramanuja’s qualified non-dualism, Vallabha’s pure-non-dualism to pave way for his doctrine of dualism-cum-non-dualism (Bheda-bheda). Incidentally, it may be mentioned that Nimbarka’s philosophical views are very much similar to those of Gaudiya Vaisnavism. In fact, the doctrinal affinity between these two systems has led. N.K. Brahma to remark, ‘Their (Gaudiyas) doctrine of inexplicable difference and identity is held by Nimbarka also.

The Gaudiyas advocate the existence of Ultimate Reality being one without a second (advaya jnana). They reject the theory of two ultimate realities, of which one is independent, and the other is dependent as admitted by Nimbarka and Ramanuja. Both of them hold two dependent entities such as cit (jiva) and acit (i.e., non-intelligent world) in addition to one independent existence of Supreme Reality.

The Gaudiyas on the one hand postulate three powers of Bhagavan (the Supreme Reality), namely

(1) antarariga-sakti or cit-sakti or svarupa-sakti.

(2) tatastha sakti or iiva sakti. (3) bahirariga sakti or mavasakti. The antaranga sakti or Cit sakti has been described as that power by which the Lord reveals Himself in His spiritual reality as immaterial being, consciousness and bliss. The tatastha sakti or iiva sakti is the power responsible for shooting forth innumerable monadic jivas or finite spirits from Him. The finite spirits come immediately under the influence of the material world and its attractions because of their karmas. The bahirariga sakti or mava sakti is that power by operating which the Lord presents Himself as the endless series of non-sentient material objects, all limited in space and time.

Before, we take up the question of relationship of Brahman (Absolute) with jiva and jagat which in Gaudiya System has special significance, it would be worthwhile to examine their nature one by one in isolation. Brahman, according to the Gaudiyas, is of the nature of pure Bliss. He is the locus of all divine powers which are only attributes of Brahman. In fact Brahman associated with all divine powers as attributes is technically designated as Bhagavan. Brahman, Paramatman and Bhagavan, all these three terms signify one and the same, Absolute Reality. He is both nirguna *ie.,* above prakrta gunas and at the same time, is associated with infinite gunas or attributes *(e.g.,* omniscient, omnipotent etc.).

He is eternal and immaterial Reality (aprakrta). He has Vaikuntha as His abode - He is above all kinds of differences such as intrinsic (svagata) homogeneous (saiatiya) and hetrogenous (vijatiya). He is responsible for creation, sustenance and dissolution of the world. He is the material and efficient cause of the world . He has infinite powers of which three are the principal ones. They have been variously described as Para. Ksetraina and avidyaor Visnu, jiva and mayaor cit sakti.jiva sakti and mava sakti as referred to above.

Brahman is said to manifest Himself in three formsnamely, Mahavisnu or Sankarsana, Pradyumna and Paramatman. Brahman as the controller of finite beings and things, is called Mahavisnu or Sankarsanawhile Pradyumna represents Brahman in the form of the controller of finite beings only. Paramatmanis the inner controller of the whole creation and as such He keeps Himself aloof from the function of manifesting the world order. Brahman has two aspects *viz.* aisvarya (majesty) and madhurya (sweetness) which constitute His true nature. This Brahman has been technically called Krsna by the author of Srimad Bhagavata. This has been supported by Sri Jiva, Gosvami in SadsandarbhaKrsna sometimes descends in the world as avatara (incarnation) when he does not appear as different from other jivas. Baladeva calls Bhagavana as Hari in the aspect of creation, Narayana in the aspect of glory and existence. Krsna represents love and bliss aspect, and hence it is regarded as the highest form of manifestation of Bhagavan.

jivas on the other hand, are produced by the Lord out of Himself, impelled by the ‘desire to be manyby operating His jiva sakti. Since jivas are only ‘amsas’ or parts of the Supreme Being (Bhagavan), and therefore essentially of the same nature as the Lord is, they are non-different from Him. At the same time, jivas are monadic by nature, hence countless in number and also bereft of divine majesty and powers. They are, therefore, different from the Lord. The jivas are ruled over by the Lord. In fact, the Gaudiyas regard jivas to be eternal servant of the Lord (nityadasa)whose true destiny lies in the love and service of the Lord. Jiva Gosvami has compared the jivas to the rays of the Sun, and Brahman being the Sun itself. Just as the rays of the Sun are different yet non-different from the Sun, in the same way jivas are different from Brahman in certain respects and yet are non-different from Him in essence. The difference between jivas and Brahman is due to maya which is again another sakti of Brahman. Under its influence jivas are oblivious of their true essence and consider themselves as different from the Lord. In this connection, it may be pointed out that jiva can remain earth-minded for ever, and a time comes in the life of every jiva who after a prolonged wandering in t he wilderness of earthly pursuits, awakes at last, to the consciousness of his true nature and turns, at last, t o the service of God as the true end of his being.

Like Jivas, material world *i.e.* jagat is also a product of Lord’s Divine Power called avidva or mava. Since maya sakti also called bahirariga sakti. is one of the Lord’s infinite powers, the material world manifested as a result of the operation of mava sakti is not false as has been held by Sankara Vedanta. The material world is true in the sense that it exists eternally in the Lord. Even at the time of cosmic dissolution (pralava). the world exists in Brahman in an unmanifest form, and therefore, it is not perceived, just as the birds, though existing at night in a jungle, are not perceived - (van linavihangavat). This material world, like the jivas is also held to be different yet non-different from the Lord in as much as it is offspring of Lord’s Bahirariga Sakti at the same time it has Brahman as the locus.

From what has been said above, it is clear that the entire metaphysics of the Gaudiyas rests on their unique concept of sakti. We have already briefly enumerated the saktis *i.e.,* different varieties of divine powers. Let us now examine their nature and scope of functioning in the following paragraphs, svarupa sakti also called antarariga Sakti or cit-sakti is that power of Bhagavan which constitues the perfect and supreme nature of the Lord, whereby He manifests Himself as the Supreme Reality, the Absolute. The functioning of this Sakti is responsible for the manifestation of His svarupa (essence) as the Lord who is of the nature of sat (pure being), tit (pure consciousness), and ananda (Bliss). It is through this power or sakti that the perfect selfhood of the Lord is manifested to His devotees. The Svarupa Sakti, as̮ it functions, is said to have three modes *viz.* samdhini, samvit and hladini.

The samdhini cit-sakti is that mode of svarupa sakti which goes on to constitute the immaterial stuff of the Lord’s Spiritual realm with its immaterial places, temples etc. with their immaterial paraphernalia of attendants of immaterial figures. In the aspect of samvit the Lord’s svarupa sakti reveals itself as the consciousness of all this immaterial splendour and magnificence. Again, it is by virtue of this power, Bhagavan manifests Himself as both, the knowledge and the knower and also makes others possessed of knowledge*.* Lastly, as Hladini, the self same svarupa sakti reveals itself as the Lord’s selfless delight in the love and service of His immaterial associates in the eternal drama which He enacts on the highest plane. By virtue of this sakti, Lord also, possesses infinite Bliss. By the functioning of this sakti the Lord becomes Bliss incarnate and also causes in the devotee the experience of pure Bliss. All these three modes of svarupa sakti (mentioned above) exist inseparably and eternally in the Lord (Bhagavan) and constitute His very self in totality. But they are so graded that samvit is supposed to include and supersede sandhini and hladini is said to include and transcend both sandhini and samvit hladini sakti is regarded as superior to the other two powers (sandhini and samvit) because bliss, necessarily, includes and supersedes both existence and consciousness.

The tatastha or jiva Sakti is that form of divine power which being distinct from both (antarariga and bahirariga saktis) does not fall under either of these categories (Saktis), yet is related to both of them, jiva or the individual self is a product of this sakti which the Lord displays in His role of Paramatman. Since, jiva is the product of this (tatastha) sakti, he cannot get himself related or connected with the svarupa sakti without first being released from the afflictions of this material world due to maya sakti. The tatastha sakti, therefore, occupies an intermediate position, as it were, among saktis having its connection with both *i.e.,* svarupa sakti and mayasakti.

Lastly, the bahirariga sakti or mava sakti is opposed to antarariga sakti which is svarupa sakti or tit sakti of the Lord in the sense that it causes creation, sustenance and dissolution of the phenomenal world. It thus, constitutes only an external aspect of the Lord in the form of the material prakrti or pradhana. Though, it is not connected directly with the antaranga sakti yet it is as real as the other two saktis are. By virtue of this (mava) sakti Bhagavan limits Himself into His partial manifestation of Paramatman, as world.

It may be noted here that both tatastha and mava sakti function through Paramatman who as a matter of fact, is (stated above), the controller of creation. All saktis however, reside in Bhagavan, the Supreme. It is through the operation of tatastha and maya Sakti in the medium of Paramatman that Bhagavan—the transcendent Absolute, becomes immanent Reality as well.

We have examined so far Brahman as the absolute Reality with its different forms and nature of His divine powers in all respects. Now, let us consider the relationship of Bhagavan and His divine powers. That saktis are different and yet non-different from Bhagavan who is conceived as saktiman *(i.e.,* possessor of sakti) has been beautifully explained by Sri Jiva Gosvami on the analogy of solar disc (mandala), its rays (rasmi) and its reflection or halo (praticchavi). Bhagavan or saktiman is represented by Sun, while svarupa-sakti of Bhagavan is represented by luminous solar disc (Surya-mandals). The rays of sun represent the tatastha sakti while the halo of the Sun represents maya sakti (bahiranga). As Sun is the substratum of luminous solar disc, in the same way Bhagavan is the locus of svarupa sakti which is responsible for the divine splendour and magnificence of the Lord. The rays of the Sun are countless in number as they emanate from the Sun, in the same way, the innumerable jivas emanate from the Lord. The halo of Sun does not form the intergral part of the Sun. Similarly maya sakti which though real, exists outside, not forming the integral part of the supreme Being.

In the foregoing paragraphs we confined ourselves to the examination of the nature of ontological categories admitted by the Gaudiyas. Let us now focus our attention to another aspect of their philosophy, *viz.,* the relation of the different categories with one-another, because, it is this field, in which the Gaudiyas have made notable advance.

If we closely examine the different theories of non­dualism advocated by different schools of Vedanta, we come across at least, five varieties of non-dualism postulated by non-theistic system like Sankara Vedanta, Theistic or Vaisnava systems like Ramanuja, Vallabha, Bhaskara and Nimbarka. Gaudiyas, too, postulate the theory of non-dualism but of a different type.

Sankara accepts the ultimate existence of one but denies the realities of many, while Ramanuja admits the existence of one Supreme Reality on which Cit. the principle of individual consciousness and acit or the principle of matter are dependent. As he too, is a believer in monism, postulates a relation of quality and qualified between dt and acit on one hand and Isvara on the other. Vnllabha on the other hand advocates pure non-dualism. As .such, he postulates the existence of Brahman which possesses the power to create world of finite beings and things by mere force of His will. The finite souls and material objects in his view, are one and identical with Brahman, since they are His own creation out of Himself. Hence the relation between finite souls and material objects on one hand and Brahman on the other is one of identity as between limited and unlimited. Bhaskara, too, believes in dualism-cum-non-dualism. According to him, finite souls and the world is one and identical with Brahman in essence, but at the same time, they are also different from Brahman as the former has limitations due to its adjuncts. Nimbarka does not accept the Bhaskara’s view that non-difference is the reality while difference is due to upadhis. For, to accept this view would mean, conditioning Brahman who is essentially unconditioned. Nimbarka, therefore, advocates a relation of difference cum non-difference between finite selves and Brahman on the same pattern as between parts and the whole, effect and cause, worshipper and worshipped.

The Gaudiyas find defects of one kind or the other in the above theories and hence they reject all of them severally criticising them on one ground or the other. Thus, by postulating power in the Lord, through which He becomes related to the world of finite beings and things. Gaudiyas avoid pitfalls of other varieties of Advaitavada as well as Bheda-bheda vada especially, the one advocated by Bhaskara and Nimbarka, technically, called Aupadhika bhedabheda and Svabhavika bhedabheda respectively.

Without entering into detailed arguments adduced by them, let us pass on our exposition of their theory on this subject which they call Acintyabhedabheda. Sri Jiva Gosvami explains this through the analogy of sakti and saktiman, as we come across in this world. Between sakti or power and saktiman or the possessor of power, there exists a relation of difference and non-difference that does not admit of any logical explanation. Let us, consider for example, fire and its burning power. The capacity of generating heat is part of the very nature of fire so that there is no fire that lacks the power or capacity of generating heat. It cannot be concluded, however, that because, the power of generating heat is the nature or property of fire, so its power of generating heat and fire are absolutely identical. In fact, if fire were nothing but this power of generating heat, the fire that is visible from a distance (not as heat producing but as mere light) would not be recognised as fire, since it does not look heat-producing to the eye. In other words, between a thing and anyone of its intrinsic powers, neither there is any absolute non-difference nor any absolute difference. Just as, we say that fire and its burning power are not absolutely identical, we cannot also say that they are absolutely different. Between a thing and its natural capacities or powers there exists a relation which, according to Jiva Gosvami, cannot be construed either as pure bheda or pure abheda since pure difference being inconceivable between the power and the possessor of power, so also is non-difference. We have therefore to concede a relation of difference and non-difference on the ground that none by itself is a thinkable relation between the two so that the complex conception of bhedabheda has to be conceived because of the inconceivability of all other alternatives.

The principal objection from the Gaudiya standpoint to the Nimbarkist variety of bhedabheda which we propose to examine closely on account of its affinity to the Gaudiyas is its inability to give an intelligible account of the relation between Brahman and its cit and acit principles. If Brahman, as Nimbarkists say, is intrinsically of the nature of cit or spirit, and if it is also sarvagata (omnipresent) and vibhu (All pervasive), how can it split itself into monadic jivas on the one side and acit (jagat) on the other. How can we talk of amsas or parts separating themselves from a reality which is infinite transcending all measurement in quantitative terms? Where can the separated parts find a home except in Brahman itself outside which there is nothing? Besides, how can an acit or non-intelligent world come out of Brahman which by definition, is of the nature of spirit or cit? Nimbarkists cannot have it both ways, that is, an absolute all-inclusive Reality which is cit or spiritual by nature and the self same spiritual principle as both the material and efficient cause of an acit or non­intelligent world.

The Gaudiyas thus reject both Aupadhika and Svabhavika bhedabheda on the ground that they, not only contradict the teachings of Sruti but also fail to give an intelligent account of the relation between Brahman and the world of finite beings and things. The problem is to explain the relation between unity and plurality, between the all inclusive eternal reality and the world of plurality as it appears in our experience. One way is to explain away the apparent contradition by denying the reality either of all inclusive unity (as Bhedavadins like Madhvites, Naiyayika or Samkhya philosphers do), or by denying the reality of the world of plurality (like Sankara and his followers). In either case the consistency is attained only by the sacrifice of obviously undeniable aspects of a total experience of situation.

The Gaudiyas point out that even though bhedabheda be a distinct advance on that of pure bheda or pure abheda as representing more comprehensive approach to the problem that does not ignore any of the essentials as yet, in either of its two varieties considered above, fails to give a satisfactory solution of the problem of the one and the many. The failure of both is a consequence according to the Gaudiyas of their initial assumption of that problem which admits of a purely logical solution. There are obvious facts of experience which cannot really be challenged even by hard-boiled sceptic, and yet their reality, when closely examined, does not admit of any logical explanation. That, sugar is sweet and quinine has bitter taste are experienced facts whose reality cannot be denied and yet, if any reason for the sweetness in the one case or the bitterness in the other be demanded, there can be no other answer than that they are just so. In other words, whenever any question is raised about the why of the ultimate constitution of reality, there is no other way than to accept it as an ultimate inexplicability which does not admit of comprehension in logical terms. This is the idea that underlies the Gaudiyas conception of the relation between Brahman and the world as one of acintyabheda- abheda *i.e.,* inconceivable dualism cum-non-dualism.

Jiva Gosvami rejects a direct relation between Brahman as the All inclusive spiritual reality and the world of the finite beings and things on the ground of obvious contradiction of their views involve. There cannot be any direct relation, causal or any other between a reality which is spiritual and a world which is material, nor between the infinite (all pervasive) and the finite and the limited. He therefore, invokes the concept of sakti or power as that through which the spiritual appears as material, and the one becomes many.

It may be noted that the view of acintvabheda bheda provides the basis for the Gaudiyas’ concept of Bhakti as Rasa which flows down from the Lord’s numinous quality into the hearts of mortals thereby generating in the devotees a sense of the transcendental and unearthly joy which has no parallel in the material world of finite things and beings.

As our present study centres round the concept of mukti as admitted by the Gaudiyas, let us turn our attention to the examination of Gaudiyas theory of mukti in the background of their philosophy of Reality. According to the Gaudiyas, the ultimate goal of one’s life consists in his attaining the supreme bliss which is also of the nature of knowledge. It follows from his getting on intuitive glimpse of the highest Reality. It has been said that when an individual aspirant has intuitive vision of the Highest Reality, the ignorance about the self and the mundane miseries immediately fall off as a consquence, and all the fatters of the heart are rent asunder, all doubts are removed and all impressions of previous actions are completely effaced. The realization of God is thus the only way of attaining highest happiness or bliss. He then, feels no fear as he is filled to the brim with bliss of the highest type (anandam brahmano vidvan na bibheti kutascaneti) It thus implies the desctruction of all kinds of bonds of empirical life (samsara bandhoccheda purvakatvat.) and also of maya which creats gulf between him and the Supreme Self. This state is known as ‘atyantika samplava: then the individual begins to feel himself ns identified with Him. This, in other words, implies the restoration of the individual self to his original state of existence following the destruction of false identification of self with non-self. This incidentally has been the view of all Vaisnva systems. To this the Gaudiyas only add that the attainment of real nature of self implies the realisation of his self *(i.e.,* as a spark of the divine spirit & eternally subservient to its will) ever m servitude of Lord krsna (krsnera nityadasa).

Before we enter into discussions of the various ideals on Mukti, let us briefly examine the conception of saksatkara or revelation of the Supreme Being according to the Gaudiyas, which is a unique conception in Indian Philosophy. The Gaudiyas admit two different kinds of saksatkara, either (1) inward relevation (antah saksatkara) or (2) outward revelation (bahihsaksatkara) of the Lord to the devotee. Some sadhakas have revelation of their ista (deity) within themselves by means of mental process. This kind of revelation of the deity within produces a feeling of satisfaction in the mind as it is only a kind of knowledge or consciousness; but there are other sadhakas who experience outward revelation of the deity through their sense-organs. In this type of revelation, there is actual darsana (vision) of the Person of the Lord and is thus considered as higher than the previous one. The kind of revelation as a particular individual aspirant would get, depends on his desire as to whether he wishes for God’s personal revelation, that is, outward revelation, or he feels contented only with the consciousness of inward revelation through his mind.

The author of Satsandarbha, however, speaks of two ideals in mukti that an individual aspirant can aspire to achieve in consonance with his inclination and capacity. In his language, the attainment of amsi (whole *i.e.,* Godhead) by amsa (or parts of the Godhead) can be of two kinds namely; (1) Brahma prapti and (2) Bhagavat prapti. Brahma prapti means attainment of Brahma following the destruction of ignorance due to Maya. When one attains this state, he has intuitive knowledge of Impersonal Brahman (which is regarded as lesser in manifestation than the Supreme God *i.e.,* Bhagavan). This knowledge is not of mundane objects but it is an outcome of the aspect of consciousness of Lord’s intrinsic Power. But (2) Bhagavat-prapti is a higher ideal in mukti which implies the attainment of Bhagavan through inconceivable power of the Lord *i.e.,* acintya sakti. In this ideal, the aspirant achieves the nearness to the holy feet of the Lord (svacaranaravinda sannidhya ca) in Vaikuntha (the Supreme Abode). It is true that the Lord is omnipresent and even manifest in creation but He is not available to the sadhakas owing to the presence of defilements in the sadhakas as well as differences.

It may be pointed out that the attainment of Brahman must not be understood to imply that the individual attains sameness with Brahman or identity, but it only means that the individual merely experiences its own intrinsic similarity to the divine reality. The similarity consists in the inherent possession, even in infinitesimal proportion of the divine svarupasakti of Bliss which for the time being was supressed by the Mayasakti. This mukti is possible both in lifetime of t he individual (jivad-dasyam) as well as after he is disembodied (utkrantadasayam).

jivan-mukti or emancipation during life-time of an individual consists in his realization of the falsity (mithyatva) of his association with the world experience due to avidya or maya. He also learns to identify the world experience as the part of God. He leads a detached life and remains absolutely unaffected by the joys and sorrows of the world, being merged in his contemplation and meditation. This state of mukti is preferred to even Brahma-saksatkara or by revelation of ultimate entity *i.e.,* Brahman. In this state, according to Jiva Gosvami, he has to experience the fruits of his actions which are on the verge of yielding fruits (i.e., prarabdhakarmas). Since the aspirant or the sadhaka remains disinterested in such experience, so he is no longer bound by them. In the end, when his mind is purged of all blemishes and when he has the revelation of Bhagavan even the effect of maya sakti vanishes.

According to Baladeva Vidyabhusana, a iivanamukta holds his body only through the will of God and all his merits and demerits (punya-papa) are destroyed with the rise of true knowledge. But so far as the fruits of obligatory duties are concerned (which he continues to perform after attaining (jivana mukti-state). they are not totally destroyed. The effects of such deeds rather help him in the wake of his knowledge. Baladeva referring to a passage in Kausitaki Upanisad in connection with a discussion on obligatory karmas remarks that the merits of a wiseman go to his friends and demerits to his enemies; so in the case of the devotees anxious of entering the communion with God, the effects of their meritorious deeds are shared by their friends and the near and dear ones, but those of his sinful deeds are shared by his enemiesso the devotee is not necessarily bound with the body so as to suffer the fruits of his prarabdha karmas. He is released from them as has been stated above. It is only due to the will of God that he is having a body only for worshipping the Lord. Even in the state of ultimate emancipation, iivas retain their separate individuality from God.

utkranti mukti i.e., emancipation after death has been said to be of two kinds, namely (1) sadvomukti i.e., instantaneous emancipation and (2) krama-mukti i.e., emancipation in gradual steps. This classification is based on individual sadhaka’s inclination and capacity for such achievement. Instantaneous emancipation is achieved by those yoginswho wish immediate release from the bonds of empirical life by destroying their gross and subtle body at their own will. These vogins do not have any consideration for a particular place (desa e.g. holy places etc.) or for a particular time (i.e., kala e.g., uttarayana etc.) when they decide to forsake their life. Such yogins give up their gross physical body as well as subtle body by sitting in an asana befitting the occasion meditating on Lord Hari all the time. This is described in detail in the 2nd skandha of Bhagavata Purana.

There is however, another class of yogins or Sadhakas who wish to enjoy pleasures of higher realms. Such yogins are said to be eligible for krama-mukti i.e., emancipation in gradual steps. It is also described in detail in the Bhagavata Purana. These yogins are required to give up their gross physical body retaining their subtle body so that it may serve as a vehicle for pure enjoyments (bhoga) in higher regions. It is obvious that such sadhakas have pure bhoga vasana in themselves while forsaking their life too. These yogins ascend gradually to higher and higher regions by performing worship (upasana) and meditation and enjoying the fruits thereof. Both these types of sadhakas once having attained emancipation, do not come back to earthly life. It may be noted here that those sadhakas who attain krama mukti are quite different from those sadhakas who practise the way of action i.e., Karmavoga. Since the followers of Karma Yoga enjoy the pleasures of the Heaven and after fructification of their good karmas they ought to return to earthly life (ksine punye martyalokam visanti).

The exponents of Gaudiya Vaisnavism while accepting the above two kinds of utkranti mukti (sadyo and krama) as described in the Bhagavata Purana, do not attach much importance to them. They seem to be inclined more towards service and love of Bhagavan, Sri diva Gosvami further describes the other five varieties of Mukti in utkranti dasa (i.e., after death) which has been accepted by other vaisnavite schools also. They are as follows, viz. (1) salokya (2) sarsti (3) sarupya (4)samipya (5) sayujya.

Salokya mukti i.e., dwelling in the divine habitation (i.e., Vaikuntha loka) with the Lord. The aspirant who achieves this type of mukti becomes a part and parcel of the Divine Light of the Lord that illumines Vaikuntha. Such Sadhakas seem to add the beauty of the highest region. They engage themselves in constant worship of the Lord without aspiring for any fruit thereof.

Sarsti Mukti means, enjoying similar and not identical, glories and powers of the Lord. Only that aspirant is eligible for such mukti who devotes himself fully for the sake of God, after renouncing all kinds of actions. The sadhakas who attain this mukti partake in all the functions of the Lord such as eating, playing etc., but they are not allowed to share in the Lord’s power of creation, sustenance and dissolution of the world. In other words, such sadhakas enjoy the glories and magnificence of the Lord only in a partial way and not in its entirety (ata eva animadipraptirapi amsena jneya).

Sarupyamukti means, having similar divine form that of the Lord Himself. The sadhakas who attain sarupya mukti seem to assume divine four-armed forms with yellow garments on their divine body. It has been stated in the Bhagavata that Gajendra attained Sarupya mukti and thus assumed similar divine form, having four arms and yellow garments and other accessories. This indicates only similarity with Lord and not identity as held under the Sarsti type of Mukti.

Samipya mukti means, attainment of nearness to the divine Self. The sadhakas achieving this kind of mukti enjoy the constant company of the Lord-inPerson. Of all these varieties of Mukti, Samipya mukti is considered to be the best one by the Gaudiya. Herein the sadhakas achieve an outward revelation (bahih saksatkara) of the deity in themselves, whereas in all the rest, the sadhakas have an experience of inward revelation (antah saksatkara) of the Deity only.

Further, Samipya mukti is preferred by the Gaudiyas on account of sadhakas getting greater opportunity for the service and love of the Lord, consequent on having a direct vision of the Person of the Lord.

Sayuiya mukti means, entering into or merging in the divine form of the Supreme Self. In this state the sadhakas enjoy the Bliss of the Lord in an indistinct form as it were. The sadhakas who attain Sayuiya mukti have no separate existence of their own whatsoever; hence, there is no question of service of the Lord in any manner. It is therefore, regarded as the lowest of all other varieties of mukti by the Gaudiyas. The Bhagavata Purana gives the instances of demons like Aghasura attaining sayuiya mukti by entering the effulgent body of the Lord.

The sadhakas who had attained Sayujya mukti are sometimes pulled by the Lord out of Himself for the sake of His lila (sport) exercising His free will. The Bhagavata illustrates this fact by citing the examples of Sisupala, Dantavaktra who having attained merger in the Lord were manifested by Him as the Parsadas (eternal attendants) of the Lord. In this connection it may be observed that the author of the Bhagavata Pur ana is rather partial in briefly describing Sayuiya mukti which is mentioned only incidentally while other varieties of mukti have been described in detail.

Sri Jiva Gosvami further, describing the characteristic feature of Sayuiya mukti says that the enjoyment experienced by the sadhakas in this state is a unique kind of bliss arising out of their merger in the divine bliss of the Lord as it were. This bliss is enjoyed by the sadhakas as flowing from intrinsic power of the Lord out of His grace. Such sadhakas however remain outside Him. That is to say, they don't loose their identity in Him, hence they enjoy the remnants as it were, of the divine bliss as bestowed on them by the Lord.

Sri Jiva Gosvami quoting from Chandogyo'panisad says that the emancipated souls have the capacity to assume more than one form and see their own self everywhere. These sadhakas can experience the bliss of any kind of mukti whatsoever out of their own will.

As Vaikuntha is a region beyond Maya, so the enjoyments experienced there, are also supramundane i.e., beyond the sphere of this prakrta world. It is therefore, stated by Sri Jiva Gosvami that all these five types of mukti mentioned above are gunatita that is to say, of the nature of Supra mundane. Hence, the sadhakas who achieve any kind of mukti, depart from this world once for all. There being no question of return from the highest Abode (anavrtti sabdat).

The Gaudiyas however, while estimating the real worth of all kinds of mukti state that the sadhakas going in for mukti aim largely for self-gratification or enjoyment by having the absolute cessation of sufferings and attaining the divine bliss as a consequent thereof. Such sadhakas do not at all aim at the service and love of Lord for its own sake which is the supreme ideal of man, according to the Gaudiyas. And thus, the Gaudiyas attach no importance to any kind of mukti whatsoever if there being no opportunity to serve the lotus like feet of the Lord with love and devotion. Love of God for its own sake is far superior to the enjoyments and bliss of emancipation. This love of God or Priti Bhakti can be attained only through the grace of the Lord by practising sravana, kirtana etc. and associating with the saintly persons and following the advice of the preceptor who has the experience of Love of God.

It may not be out of place to remark here that the Gaudiyas attach so much importance to Bhakti that they prefer to use the term Kaivalya to mukti, which according to them is not synonymous with Kaivalya or moksa implied in Samkhya or other systems of Indian Philosophy or where merger of oneself implies. Sri Jiva Gosvami has pointed out that Kaivalya implies bhakti (love of God), hence it is far superior to mukti (which does not involve Prema-bhakti) and hence is not the ideal to be sought by man. The Gaudiyas consider the terms apavarga and kaivalya to be synonymous in as much as both of them imply bhakti of the Lordy.

## Chapter IV - Premabhakti As Pancama Purusartha

In the foregoing pageswe have discussed Purusarthas first as in reference to the triad of mundane ends otherwise called the trivarga, viz. Dharma. Artha and Kama to which supra-mundane end viz. Moksa was added later on to constitute what is known as the caturvarga. In that connection it has been pointed out that dharma, artha and kama have bearing on our earthly life while Moksa belongs to supra mundane sphere and thus it transcends all the three, and is the final and the absolute end. Besides this, the Vaisnavas especially, the Gaudiyas accept another value of life, viz. Bhakti or Premabhakti which they describe as Pancam a Purusartha or the fifth and the highest end of life. According to Gaudiya vaisnavas, Premabhakti or devotion as love of God for His own sake stands head and shoulder above Moksa or liberation. As a matter of fact, the Gaudiyas give very little importance to Moksa in comparison to Premabhakti. In support of this let us, quote from Rupa Gosvami’s Bhaktirasamrta Sindhu which says, “the four ends of human life are of no more value than that of a mere blade of grass to those in whose hearts the love of God has sprouted forth only”. Further, it has also been pointed out in the same work that “not only the pleasure derived from the caturvarga but even the eternal bliss constituting the essence of Brahman stands no where in comparison to the bliss or joy arising from the love of God which is the ocean of all blissThe superiority of Bhakti over mukti is evident from what has been said by Lord Krsna in the Bhagavad Gita, viz. “Having become one with the eternal and cheerful in mind, he neither grieves nor desires, the same to all beings, he obtains supreme devotion to me”.

Moreover, mukti is within easy reach of every body and even the Lord Himself offers it unhesitatingly to all without exception, even to those who cherish feelings of ill-will and animosity towards Him, but the same is not true of Bhakti.

The joy that comes from the attainment of Mukti is no doubt, of the highest kind, very different from the pleasures of the flesh; but the joy or bliss arising out of Bhakti transcends by far the joy or delight arising out of Mukti. That is why, even the aspirants of liberation (mumuksavah)and the liberatedsouls who have tasted the joy of mukti are found worshipping the Lord Hari with earnest love and devotion.

Now it may be asked however, that what makes the joy or bliss of Priti Bhakti greater than that of mukti with its four varieties of Salokya. Samipya, Sarupya and Sayuiya as admitted by the Vaisnava systemsand which is not accepted, rather rejected by the aspirants of Bhagavat-Priti who are found engaged in loving service of the Lord without any motive or desire thereof even when offerred by the Lord Himself.

It may be noted in the first place that Mukti here has been conceived in relation to a personal God, but in some Indian systems e.g., in Samkhya mukti is conceived independently of any reference to a personal God. The Gaudiya view of mukti is narrower than that which has been conceived in other Indian systems of Philosophy. In Vaisnava systems, other than the Gaudiya system, mukti is conceived as liberation from the ills of earthly life realised in relation to a personal God while in non-vaisnava systems it is independent of reference to supreme Personality i.e., God. Secondly, the Gaudiyas reject sayuiya mukti as being quite unacceptable to a true devotee who loves the servitude of God; since in Sayujya type of mukti there is complete self-merging into the transcendental bliss of God and it bears no separate existence of its own. Thirdly, the other four varieties such as Salokya, Sarsti, Sarupya and Samipya are admitted by the Gaudiyas in so far as, they are subservient to and not obstacles in the way of Bhakti.

In the Priti-sandarbha, it has been pointed out that the desire for enjoying the bliss or delight in the svarupananda of God or having a similar joy which the Lord feels Himself in His own perfections and which is enjoyed by the liberated souls or the muktas is not purely unmotivated and hence it is not desirable or acceptable to those who practise or aspire for Prema-Bhakti.

The joy of Bhakti, however, is something quite different. It is not the svarupananda of the Lord, but it is svarupasaktyanandai.e., the ananda which arises in the individual through the Lord’s exercise of His svarupasakti or Intrinsic Potency. The exercise of this svarupasakti causes a transference of the element of hladini in that sakti to the individual and arouses in him the incorporable joy of devotion to and service of the Lord, and this joy aroused in the individual in its turn, arouses a corresponding joy or delight in the Lord Himself which transcends by far that joy of svarupananda or the joy that results from the consciousness of His own perfection. In other words, by causing delight in the bhakta or the devotee in the contemplation of the Lord’s perfections, the Lord Himself experiences a delightfar greater than the joy of svarupananda. This shows that the joy of Bhakti is greater than the joy of Mukti. In other words, the Lord is not fully satisfied with the joy that results from the consciousness of His own perfection, it is by sharing this svarupasakti that He feels a far greater joy. That is to say, the Lord is completely satisfied not in His self-centred joy in His Self but in a joy shared with others as the results of the consciousness of his incomparable perfection. Such a joy, that is, a joy of give and take may be a joy that results from the consciousness of His aisvarya i.e., His transcedent glory, splendour, might and valour etc. or it may be a joy in which the madhurva or sweetness that marks the consciousness of a near and dear one be mixed up with the joy of Aisvarya i.e., a consciousness of his might, majesty and splendour etc.

Or lastly, it may be the joy of the consciousness of a near and dear one without any sense of the distance that separates one from the other characterised by aisvarya or transcendence, glory etc. It is this last variety of svarupasaktyananda that characterises the damsels of Vrindavan who love Krsna as a dearest of the dear and a nearest of the near and they have no consciousness of His transcendence, glory or might, that is, a bar to a face-to-face, close personal relation with the Lord. This is the highest type of Bhakti and the joy that characterises transcendence by far, not only the joy of svarupananda but also, the joy of the consciousness of aisvarya and that of aisvarya midra (mixed) with madhurya. All this proves that the joy of Bhakti not only surpasses the joy of Mukti, but also that the madhurya or the joy of the consciousness of the Lord in His Madhurya aspect as a near and dear one, such as that characterises the Love of the Vrindavana Gopis for their well beloved Krsna, outshines by far, all other forms of bliss. The joy of svarupananda of Lord’s own perfection is such that “he does not care for His own self so much as that of his devotees, neither does He care for his own splendour or glory as personified in His consort Laxmi , His chief and main concern is those who have taken their last refuge in Him. Furthermore, the Lord openly, admits the superiority of his devotee to all others as e.g. .atmavoni viz, Brahma - Sankara - the personification of His own gun as, Samkarasana - the elder brother and Sri i.e., Laxmi - and the last of all in the series, His own self i.e., atma which is constituted by pure bliss.

Now, it may be asked, however, what is that Prema-bhakti which supercedes the joy or delight even of svarupananda of the Lord, and which makes Him delighted and intoxicated, as it were, and also about which Sruti says, ‘It leads to Him, it reveals Him ; He is controlled by it and it is the highest of all” .

In reply to the above question Jiva Gosvami in his Pritisandarbha describes the nature of devotion as follows:

The bliss caused by Bhagavat-priti is to be comprehended as a special expression of the divine manasananda. This divine bliss cannot be compared or likened to the bliss of the Samkhya arising from the sattvaguna, Bhagavan is eternally untouched by the gunas brought into existence by the maya-sakti, nor is it like the brahmananda of the nirvisesa-vadins, for, it would then, be nothing more than svarupananda, nor is it like the ananda of the Jiva which is only atomic ; but it is the peculiar bliss of the highest power i.e., hladini (svarupasakti) by which Bhagavan Himself enjoys and makes others enjoyed. This divine bliss, which surpasses every other kind of bliss, being placed in the devotee becomes Bhagavat-priti. The experience of which makes both the deity and the devotee completely engrossed in each other (paraspardvesatva). Thus a direct channel of mystic contact is established between them, but there is never complete identity, but the relationship continues for ever. The process is illustrated by the analogy of the heating of iron by the fire in which the iron is possessed by the attributes of the fire and becomes fiery, but its character as iron remains unchanged. After stating the intrinsic nature of Prema Bhakti or Love of God for God’s sake, the extrinsic nature of Bhakti has been stated as follows:

Bhakti is self revealing. It reveals itself through itself. When the name of Lord Hari is meditated on in rememberance, as it were, or is recounted in a company of devotees; as a consequence of this, the hairs on the devotee’s body stand on their ends and he feels indescribable joy, his heart melts and tears of joy begin to roll down on his cheeks.

As a matter of fact, cold logic hardens the heart while the emotion of Bhakti warms and melts it. In other words, horripilation and tearful eyes and melting of the heart are the psychophysical concomitant of Bhakti as Priti or Love. They are the anubhavas or the outer expression of the inner exhuberance of the emotions of joy or delight which is Bhakti or Bhagavat-priti. Also for the full development of Bhakti, the purification of heart is an essential condition and which is caused by the above mentioned feelings of the heart.

Briefly speaking, Premabhakti may be defined as that natural and spontaneous flow of divine love which is absolutely uninterrupted or distracted by any other thought or desire, and is nothing but the function of the essence of the Bliss-Potency (ananda sakti) of Bhagavan or the Lord Himself. It consists of nothing but thought and desire congenial to the service of the Lord for His own sake. It brushes aside and throws into the background all the other objects of human pursuit. In fact, it is the nidhana or the storehouse of all qualities that are noble and holy and it reaches its consummation or culmination in eternal consort of the Lord Himself (Bhagavan), and is the only thing that can win over even the Lord who is the supreme spirit and is, as it were, the central joy or delight that radiates into the joys and delights of the entire universe and even as the moon is the centre of the soothing beams that is, a source of delight to all earthly beings. This is the highest type of bliss or love of God. It is very difficult to attain on the part of earthly mortals unless there is complete absence of all cravings in them for earthly pleasures and even the desire for liberation from the ills of life, there cannot be any awakening of true bhakti or devotion in their heart. In other words, one must conquer not merely, the lusts of the flesh, but also the desire for liberation from earthly bondage before one can be qualified for the Bhaktimarga or the path of devotion. No body is real adhikari for the path of devotion who has not renounced all earthly interests, as also the desire for liberation from mundane life and its engrossing attractions.

In the very opening chapter of Srimadbhagavata Purna the highest authoritative scripture of the Vaisnava religion, the parama dharma or the highest conservations of the spirit is described as that where the aspiration or desire for moksa or kaivalya vanishes. Commenting on the above, Sri Jiva Gosvami in his Krama Sandarbha has emphasised on the fact that the absence of desire for moksa or Kaivalya i.e., losing one’s individuality into the Supreme Entity which is called as Sayujya Mukti is not sufficient enough for the awakening of bhakti in oneself but also the desire for the rest of the four types of mukti viz. salokya, sarupya, sarsti and samipya must also be absent.

Sri Rupagosvami points out that, strictly speaking moksa laghutakrta - does not constitute a definition of bhava Bhakti, but is an invariable effect or consequent of bhavabhakti. In other words, with the awakening of bhavabhakti or bhakti as inward feeling as distinguished from bhakti as outward service, there is an indifference even to moksa-usually regarded as parama purusartha. That is to say, for a bhava Bhakta even moksa as an ultimate value has no importance.

We can account for the reason why the Prema bhaktas disregard all the five forms of Mukti. So far as Savuiva Mukti meaning either entering the persons of the Lord (Isvara sayuiyam) or entering the Impersonal Brahman, is concerned, the devotee has to lose his individuality in the Absolute. This implies that there can be no service or devotion in the absence of relation between the devotee and the object of devotion. Hence, the true devotee rejects Mukti or liberation in the sense of union with God. The same holds good about the other four types of Mukti also, but with a little difference. In these four types of Mukti viz. salokya, sarupya, sarsti and samipya the devotee cares more for his self enjoyment than the enjoyment or pleasure of the Lord. Here, the devotion is more for the sake of the Lord’s splendour, glory, fame and powers than for the sake of the Lord Himself. But, according to Rupa Gosvami, these four types of Mukti are not to be rejected outright, only they are considered as undesirable on some ground or

the other. That is to say, so far as the part of the selfish-motives of the devotee, or the desire for the self enjoyment of the Lord’s glory and perfections are concerned, these Muktis are not desired by the devotee i.e., a (Prema bhakta) of the Lord. And these four kinds of liberation, when come to the devotee unasked, he may accept them as gifts of God Himself, and thus if he makes use of them in serving the Lord with Love and devotion for the sake of the Lord’s delight and comforts and the desire for self enjoyment is in abeyance, then these muktis are not to be neglected. But, those worshiping the Lord Hari with exclusive love and service, wish to relish the taste of the love-sentiment of the Lord in some social relationship of parent and child, of master and servant, of friend and friend and of lover and beloved, such devotees, of course, can neglect Mukti with all its varieties, whatsoever by giving primacy to the delight of service through these muktis over the pure love and devotion in and through the above four kinds of social relationships. In other words, even service through the four kinds of mukti appears to them as insignificant when compared to prema seva and the delight which it generates both in the Lord and the devotee. Also there is not enough scope in these four forms of mukti for the fulfilment of seva-vasana or the desire to serve the Lord in various ways.

The sole object of a prema-bhakta or love-intoxicated devotee is service of the Lord, so much that even paramananda or super-excellent joy which follows as a matter of necessity is considered as impediment as it does, sometimes, by violent physiological disturbances in the capacity to serve the Lord. In short, for a prema-bhakta, the question of self-enjoyment does not arise at all. What he is concerned with is to cause the pleasure and delight of the Lord in utter disregard of his own personal pleasure and gratification. Such is the greatness of a prema-bhakta, that "of crores of liberated and self-realised souls, one, rarely, meets even with a single premabhakta in the true sense of the term".

In short, a premabhakta is also superior to a mukta in the sense that the former establishes an intimate social relationship with his beloved deity or the object of devotion viz. Sri Krsna and loves him as the nearest and dearest to his heart. He, thus, feels a sort of madiyata or mineness about Sri Krsna. He feels that the Lord Krsna is his own in a way that none else is. On the contrary, the mukta, has no such feeling of ownness and no sense of intimate relationship. His feeling is one of tvadiyata i.e., a feeling that expresses itself in the form ‘I am thine’ entailing depersonalisation of all personal feelings and in an impersonal being where no personal relationship is possible. Obviously, bhakti or devotion with love and affection in this respect, is superior to mukti. Hence Love that is pure and self-validating and self-luminous and which is disinterested without prejudice to self-conscious personal relationship is certainly to be preferred to a liberation or emancipation which is self-obliterating and there is complete destruction of the self as a person.

The superiority of bhakti over mukti has been dealt with in detail by the author of Brhad Bhagavatamrtam through the story of a Gopa Kumar who makes a trip to upper lokas i.e. higher regions in search of his dear friend Sri Krsna. Details of his journey have been dealt with in the concluding chapter.

Now we conclude the chapter concept of Bhakti as pancama purusatha' by examining its superiority over mukti or moksa being the fourth and final purusatha considered by orthodox and other system of Indian philosophy except the Gaudiya school. We shall examine in detail, the concept of Bhakti, its origin and development right from Vedas to the Bhagavata tradition and the theistic schools of vaisnavism in the next chapter that follows.

## Chapter V - The Concept Of Bhakti According To Theist1c Schools Of Vedanta

In the foregoing pages we have already discussed the superiority of bhakti over mukti or emancipation. According to all the Vaisnavite systems, mukti is regarded as the supreme goal of life to which bhakti stands as means to realise the highest end. According to the Gaudiyas, bhakti is regarded as the culmination of all human endeavour, the supreme goal of life to which bhakti itself is means for its achievement. It is therefore, according to the Gaudiya Vaisnavas, named as Pancama Purusartha or the fifth and final end of human life.

The cult of bhakti though, a distinguishing feature of the Vaisnavas, is not an exclusive one of the Vaisnava religion, but it is also found in other branches of religion such as Saivism, Sikhism and Christianity.

Before we take up Bhakti as such for discussion, it would be worthwhile to trace the origin and development of the concept of Bhakti. The cult of Bhakti is as ancient as the Vedas. We find the germs of this cult in the Vedic hymns and the Upanisads. The hymns to Varuna, Savitr and Usas are replete with sentiments of piety and devotion.

Certain passages from the main Upanisads are said to refer to bhakti indirectly in connection with deep meditation in which the bhakta sees God face to face as it were. According to the Taittiriya Upanisad, the nature of Brahman is described as bliss itself (raso vaisah) so the devotee also becomes bliss when he realises the blissful nature of Him. After the achievement of such a blissful state, the devotee knows no fear of any sort. Such a state of bliss is indescribable in language and is beyond thought. So, the mind and speech are helpless to get at such a state. The Chandogya-Upanisad in support of the above fact states that such an aspirant sees Him everywhere in all beings. Knowing thus he thinks, plays and enjoys in himself and feels himself to be the highest of all other creatures. Thus, having the realization of blissful nature of Brahman, he is able to move about in all the regions, of his own will. Brahadarnyaka Upanisad characterising the features of a devotee speaks that the aspirant should have an innocence of a baby inspite of his knowledge and wisdom. He should be above mundane desires. The only desire for an aspirant is the longing for ultimate Reality.

It is said in the Kathopanisad that the self cannot be realised by the study of the Vedas, nor by the intelligence, nor by deep learning. It can be realized by him alone whom it chooses or favours; to him the self reveals its own nature.

In the earlier Upanisads, we find only stray references about the concept of bhakti, but in the later Upanisads such as Gopala Tapani and Nrsimha Tapani etc., the concept of bhakti has been discussed in clear terms, for instance, bhakti, in Gopala Tapani Upanisad, has been defined as adoring service of the Lord. This implies centering of the mind on Him expecting no other gain here and hereafter.

The word ‘bhajana’ is derived from the same root as Bhakti is and both have the same meaning, one connoting the feeling while the other the action. The elements of love i.e., bhakti differentiates bhajana from sadhana. Since sadhana or the practice of certain disciplinary courses may or may not include the factor of love such as in the field of jnana and yoga. But it is not true in the case of bhajana which is mainly based upon love. Its very soul is the offering of loving homage to the object of worship. Here the sadhana or the disciplinary action is only as being subservient to bhajana. Thus, Bhakti is essentially a love cult.

A detailed discussion of bhakti is however found in such texts as the Narada Pancaratra, the Sandilya Sutra, the Narada Bhakti Sutra and the Srimad Bhagavatam. The Narada Pancaratra describes bhakti in this manner, bhakti is a kind of feeling in the heart of a devotee that the Lord is his own. He has inculcated this feeling of love in his heart so intensively that he thinks of Lord as an exclusive object of his love and adoration.

Again, in the same cited work, bhakti is defined as nothing but an unconditional service of the Lord, the master of senses, by means of senses themselves purified by intentness on Him. It is further discussed as that by which, the Lord is subjugated by the devotee. It is in the form of constant flow of mind brimming with love towards the Lord without involving therein any kind of selfish desire whatsoever.

It can be concluded here that bhakti according to Narada Pancarata is of a supreme type, it is such devotion which is not marred by any external desires or motives.

According to Sandilya Sutras, bhakti has been defined as supreme attachment to God for its own sake. Svapnesvaraji Maharaja, commenting upon the above sutra says that here anurakti implies the same meaning as raga so the sutra denotes as the highest attachment to the object of devotion or worship (aradhya visayaka ragatvam). Another commentary on the same sutra named as Bhakti-Sudha explains that bhakti implies a feeling of exclusive love and attachment to God and which is compared to the strong desire of a hungry person for food, that of a thirsty for water and that of a miserly person for wealth etc.. This analogy has a parallel in Visnu Purana where Prahlada prays to Lord for granting him bhakti as such and says - that he should love his object of worship so much as the worldly people love for sense objects etc. It is therefore, pointed out that the natural and spontaneous attachment to God is called Bhakti in its true sense.

Narayana Tirtha in his commentary on Sandilya sutras called Bhakti-candrika, is of opinion that Bhakti and Priti denote the same thing. Love of God in its highest stage is designated by Bhakti even if there is no notion of worship, as in the case of Gopis’ love for Sri Krsna. While differntiating bhakti from jnana or knowledge, he further points out that bhkati is totally different from jnana in as much as it is of the nature of raga or attachment, while jnana or knowledge is opposed to it. In fact, where knowledge ends, bhakti begins.

Svapnesvaraji Maharaja while agreeing upon the above fact denies that bhakti to be a sort of knowledge or of Sraddha or of action or worship. It is defined in the Bhakti Martanda by him as that form of love in which there is a fear of separating the two when they are together, while they have a painful longing for being together when the two are separated from each other.

In Sandilya Bhakti Sutra, bhakti as such has been classified into two heads, viz. one is mukhya and the other is itara which is also called gauni bhakti. This gauni bhakti or bhakti of secondary type enables the aspirant to achieve samadhi sidhi. It also includes devabhaktii.e., devotion to gods if it is practised without having any fruits thereof. Thus, an aspirant gains the capacity of attaining parabhakti or supreme love of devotion by way of following the secondary type of devotion without any other motive except the love of God.

In another sutra, it has been pointed out that there is a feeling of raga or attachment in gauni bhakti but this raga is not to be discarded as is generally advised. Here, raga or attachment is as welcomed as being a part and parcel of the supreme love of God. Attachment to earthly objects is not bhakti, it is bhakti if it has for its object as the highest and the best.

Sandilya in his Bhakti Sutras also mentions a few characteristics of bhakti. An aspirant should have the knowledge of the divine qualities of the Lord, and singand hear the praises and stories about Him. He should meditate upon Lord alone, completely resort to Him, have purity in mind, body, speech and action. He should have sympathy for everyone, and serve the Lord and His devotees in every possible way.

In Narada Bhakti Sutra, bhakti is defined as intense love for God and which is also of the nature of immortality. It is further described as that condition of the soul in which all its activities are consecrated to God and if an aspirant is temporarily oblivious of God, it causes extreme anguish and pain in him. Thus, Bhakti, in Narada’s view is not merely a constant flow of mind towards Lord, but it involves supreme longing for Him also. The nature of which cannot be described adequately in wordson account of its being devoid of qualifications, free from desires and too subtle a feeling for expressing in language.

Thus, bhakti being the highest of all human experiences, a man, on attaining it, desires nothing, laments nothing, but becomes peaceful, quiet and full of joy. It is, however, supported by Narada Bhakti Sutra that such an aspirant sees and hears only that (God), talks and thinks only that (God), after attaining such devotion.

Bhakti has been classified under two main heads, viz., (1) Para bhakti i.e., bhakti of the highest type, and (2)Gauni bhakti or bhakti of secondary type.

para-bhakti or bhakti of the highest type, is unequal and unparallel. The nature of the supreme devotion is the same as mentioned above. It has been said to be far superior to karma (action), jnana (Knowledge) and yoga (Concentration of mind) in as much as it is not merely a means to the achievement of ultimate goal but also as an end-in-itself.

gauni bhakti is devotion belonging to mundane region and as such it has certain characteristics of this region. It is therefore, classified in two ways. One, from the point of view of the three gunas of prakrti which characterises the mind of a person in whom it manifests itself viz. sattvika, rajasika. tamasika. secondly, it has been considered in accordance with the nature of the aspirant such as arta jijnasu and artharthinas has been mentioned in the Bhagavad-Gita also.

There is a kind of gradation in the above classification in so far as the ultimate goal of life is concerned. To illustrate, the tamasika-bhakti lies at the lowest run in this gradation, it develops and leads the aspirant to the rajasika-bhakti which is the next higher step. This, in turn, develops and leads the aspirant to sattivika bhakti which is the highest among the three, and which in turn, develops and leads the aspirant to Para-bhakti. The same is the case in the second classifications (of arta, jijnasu and artharthin).

It has also been said in another sutra that the gauni bhakti is easier to be followed and hence, most of the people have the inclination towards this kind of devotion.

In Narada Bhakti Sutra, there are eleven phases of this Para-bhakti. They are more or less similar to nine modes of devotion described in the Bhagavatam also. They are enumerated as viz.

- (i) Guna mahatmyasakti i.e., love for glorifying the qualities of the Lord,
- (ii) Rupasakti i.e., love of divine form or beauty.
- (iii) Pujasakti - i.e.. love of worship.
- (iv) Smaranasakti - i.e., love of remembereance,
- (v) Dasyasakti - i.e., love of service to Him as a master,
- (vi) Sakhyasakti - i.e., love of Him as a friend,
- (vii) Vatsalyasakti -i.e., love of Him as a child,
- (viii) Kantasakti - i.e., love for Him as that of a wife for her husband,
- (ix) Atmanivedanasakti - i.e., love of self-surrender to Him,
- (x) Tanmavatasakti - i.e., love of complete absorption in Him, and
- (xi) Paramavirahasakti - i.e., love of the pain of separation from Him

All these eleven phases of bhakti are not different as they appear to be, but they are all manifestations of love which in itself is only one. By this specification, it is meant to say only that each one is characterised by a predominant attitude. The different attitudes may be found in the same person at one time or the other as in the case of King Ambarisa - a great bhagavata. He followed almost nine different modes of bhakti while there are some who attained the ultimate goal of life by following mainly only one attitude or the other. It is also to be noted that this specification is based on the difference in tastes and preferences of the individuals. The last attitude i.e., parama-virahasakti is the common characteristic of all bhaktas.

Before we take up the concept of bhakti according to Srimadbhagavata it may be pointed out that according to ancient Indian tradition other than Bhagavata Tradition, there were three ways of God-realisation such as Karma (action), Jnana (knowledge) and Bhakti (devotion). All of them stand by one another in a complementary form and not in isolation. To explain, it is said that jnana helps the aspirant to know the ultimate goal of life, karma joins hands in the achievement of the same and bhakti enables him to dive deep into the ocean of love which is God-itself. In other words, knowledge gives impetus to action and devotion, devotion arouses knowledge and action, while action assists in the cultivation of both, knowledge and devotion. All the three ways of God-realization are essential for the achievement of ultimate goal acting in harmony with one another.

In the Bhagavad Gita also, we find all these three ways of God-realization, but they are not essentially worked out as complimentary to one another. They have been treated as three, independent ways. The aspirant can achieve the supreme goal by following any of them exclusively or all the three simultaneously in harmony with one another. But a careful study of the BhagavadGita shows that the way of bhakti or devotion has been considered as higher than the rest of the two ways.

According to Srimad Bhagavatam, bhakti or devotion occupies the highest place among all the other ways of God-realization such as Samkhya Yoga, svadhyaya and tapas etc.. One pointed devotion to God is the only effective and efficient means of attaining the ultimate goal of life. Even the low-caste people can also achieve the highest end of life by way of devotion to Lord alone.

The rise of one-pointed and supreme devotion to the Lord, as it has been referred to, makes the heart of the aspirant melt and the speech to be faultering. He sometimes behaves like a mad man as it were. He begins to weep at one time and begins to smile after sometime. Such is the effect caused by supreme devotion as is not untarnished by any extraneous motives and is unconditional and unalloyed.
