According to Srimad Bhagavata, there are two kinds of bhakti (devotion) viz. (1) nirguna-bhakti and (2) saguna-bhakti.

The saguna bhakti as the name implies to itself appertains to the gun as of prakrti and its roots lie in the mundane sphere. Since it is not unconditional and unmotivated, it is regarded as secondary devotion and not of the highest type. Generally, it has been conceived by an aspirant to achieve some kind of fruit or the other by way of following the devotion of this type. Or in other words, it may be noted that an aspirant who follows the way of saguna devotion aspires for some motive at the root of it. The motive or desire to be aimed at may be of mundane or supra-mundane in nature. It is also said that saguna bhakti is merely a means to achieve the highest end of life, viz. mukti. This variety of bhakti has been classifed into three categories in accordance with the nature of the desire of an aspirant. They are viz. (1) sattviki. (2) rajasi and (3) tamasi. (1) sattviki bhaktiis that which is done for the sake of duty only and the fruit of the actions performed is consecrated to God alone, rajasi-bhakti stands for that which is cultivated by way of obtaining mundane glories and pleasures, while the last one i.e., tamasi-bhakti consists in having the motives of its cultivation to do harm and tease others. The last variety of saguna-bhakti is regarded as the lowest among the three, as it appertains to injurious feelings, the second variety though, has a march over the third one, is considered as of middle one, where as the first variety has an advance over the rest of the two and is regarded as the best of all of them. But since, it is also cultivated with some motive such as moksa, it has to be regarded as subsidiary to another variety of bhakti, viz. nirguna bhakti.

nirguna-bhakti, according to the Bhagavata, consists in having supra-mundane qualities as its base. It is the bhakti which is said to be an end-in-itself. It culminates in the attainment of supreme love of God. nirguna-bhakti being unconditioned and unmotivated in its essential nature is described in the Bhagavata as an uninterruped flow of mind towards the Lord for its own sake. This is the highest form of bhakti and is also called para according to the Narada-Bhakti-Sutra. It is unique and without any parallel.

The nine phases of bhakti for its manifestation are prescribed in the Srimadbhagavata. They are enumerated as follows: i.e., (i) sravanam or hearing of name, qualities and stories of the Lord, (ii) kirtanam or chanting and singing the praises of the deity,(iii) smaranam or remembering of divine name and qualities etc. (iv) padasevanam or service of the Lord, (v) arcanam or worshipping of the Lord in every possible way by offering flowers, sweets etc. (vi) vandanam or offering salutations to the Lord, (vii) dasyam or Service of the Lord in a manner as that of a servant towards his master, (vii) sakhyam or having a feeling of love for the Lord as a friend, and (ix) atmaniyedanam or self-surrender, that is to say, the aspirant resorts himself fully to Lord’s will.

It may be pointed out that all these nine phases for the manifestation of bhakti are described in consonance with different attitudes and inclinations of individuals. Each of them is so efficacious and that, if it so followed with full mind and devotion, it must culminate in the achievement of the highest goal. It has been said that bhakti was obtained by Pariksita through listening to the Bhagavata, by Sukadeva through remembering of the Lord and so on. The Bhagavata, however, cites an example of Ambrisa who is said to have followed all the nine modes or phases of bhakti as described above.

In the foregoing paragraphs we have traced the origin and development of Bhakti from the Vedas to the Bhagavata. Now, let us consider the conception of bhakti as described in the theistic Vaisnava schools of Vedanta such as the schools of Ramanuja, Madhva, Vallabha and Nimbarka.

Bhakti has been defined in Sribhasya of Ramanuja, as a constant rememberance of God. While in the commentary on Bhagavadgita, Ramanuja describes bhakti as contemplation on Brahman (ultimate reality)with love. Since Brahman according to him, is described to be of the nature of pleasure (sukha), so the act of contemplation upon Him also produces pleasure in the sadhaka. It may therefore, be concluded that bhakti is continuous loving meditation on God. The very act of meditation having been illustrated by the analogy of unbroken stream of oilRamanuja while admitting the necessity of bhakti for Ultimate God-realisation does not discard knowledge altogether. He infact, is of opinion that bhakti in the highest stage involves and includes knowledge (jnana) but he does not consider knowledge to be sufficiently efficacious for the achievement of salvation. He infact, regards a harmonious blending of devotion and knowledge to be effective means for the attainment of the highest-goal. In other words, the directrealization of Brahman consists in the highest knowledge which is devotion in its essential nature.

Ramanuja advocates three ways of God-realization viz., (1) the way of Karma, (2) the way of Jnana and

(3) the way of Bhakti: He lays almost an equal emphasis over all the three paths of God-realization. He is of opinion that by performing the disinterested action (karma) the mind of an aspirant becomes purified and thus it leads him to the path of knowledge and that of devotion, since in a purified and pious mind, the nature of true knowledge and that of devotion can be easily reflected. It is also said in Sribhasya that Brahman, the highest object of realization, can be realised by the path of jnana (knowledge) also, but it being rather difficult path to be followed by an ordinary sadhaka, the aspirants are therefore, advised to follow the path of meditation and devotion. The path of bhakti or devotion is said to be the easiest one for the achievement of the highest goal. He regards karma and jnana to be merely the auxiliaries to Bhakti.

Worship of God is fruitful only if it actuated with the knowledge of the nature of the object of worship as well as that of the aspirant himself. In other words, the aspirant should know his real nature, the nature of the object of worship and his relationship with the object of worship as is stated in the Bharadvaja Samhita that God - the supreme soul is my Master whereas I am His Servant. It appears that Ramanuja prefers Dasya bhakti i.e., devotion of a servant towards his master, but he has not elaborated what he means by Dasya bhakti.

Sri Nivasadasa speaks of two kinds of Bhakti viz. (1) sadhana bhakti which is engendered by spiritual excercises and (2) phala bhakti which is spontaneous and due to Divine Grace and is therefore beyond any effort on the part of the aspirant. Phala bhaktias found in Sri Prankusa, Natha etc. arises in an individual by the grace of the Lord, sadhana bhakti however, develops in an aspirant as a consequence of his personal effort by practising astangayoga or the eight fold path of yama, niyama, asana, pranayama, pratyahara, dharana, dhyana and samadhi and the sadhana saptaka or the seven fold discipline of viveka, vomoka, abhvasa, kriya, kaluana, anavasada and anuddharsasadhana bhakti has three successive stages of development which are technically called Parabhakti, Parainana and Paramabhakti. (1) Parabhakti - according to Ramanuja, is the primary love of God that arises in the heart of an aspirant and makes him inquisitive about Him. (2) Parainana denotes the perfect knowledge of the God-head. It arises in the heart of the aspirant when he comes near to God through bhakti and when the perfect knowledge culminates in the development of supreme devotion, it is called Paramabhakti. Thus bhakti according to Srinivasadasa developed through successive stages of Parabhakti. Parainana and Paramabhakti has Praptti (self-surrender) as an indispensible limb.

The Ramanujists lay greater emphasis on Bhakti and Prapatti that are said to be particular modes of knowledge. As it is stated in Yatindramatadipika that Is'vara alone grants moksa or release when he is propitiated by bhakti and prapatti. Hence both of them are the only means for the achievement of final release (moksa).

Here, bhakti is also denoted by the term dhyana or meditation while prapatti or self-surrender is called as nyasavidya. The former is manifold, because of the difference of Vidyas which are of two kinds ; firstly, those that are the means to the achievement of worldly fruits, and secondly, those that are helpful for the attainment of liberation, nyasavidya or prapatti has been defined as that vidya which enjoins the aspirants to conceive what is inconformity with the will of Isvara to reject what is not agreeable to Him, to have firm belief in Him as the saviour, to seek Him as the only Protector, and to surrender oneself to Him in all humility. These are the five constituents of Prapatti also called Saranagati.

It may be pointed out that these two (bhakti and prapatti) are the only effective means for the attainment of release, hence the means collaborated by other schools of Indian Philosophy are rejected by the Ramanujists.

It is also stated in the Yatindramatadipika that the path of bhakti is not open to all. Only upper three classes are said to be eligible for the path of bhakti. While the way of prapatti is open to all. The path of bhakti is purely based on Vedas and thus is beyond the reach of the low-castes’ minds and hence they are debarred from it, while the path of prapatti or (self-surrender) is of the nature of puranas where there is no distinction is made for caste and creed, and thus is open for all; but it is specially meant for those who are unable and helpless in having any other way except the way of complete resignation to the Lord’s will.

Lokacarya in his Artha-prapancaka speaks of bhakti and prapatti to be different from each other, while Sri Nivasadasa holds prapatti to be a phase or limb of bhakti. Sathakopa also agrees with Lokacarya on this point. According to Lokacarya and Sathakopa, prapatti is said to be of two kinds, viz. (1) arta and drpta. In the former case, the aspirant is anxious to get rid of this body as soon as possible while in the latter, the aspirant awaits for the prescribed period of time for getting rid of this body. This analysis of Prapatti is also accepted by Sri Nivasadasa.

Bhakti has been defined by Madhva as firm love for God more than for anything else; with full consciousness of His glory and magnificence. Jaya-tirtha in his Tattva-prakasika defines bhakti to be a feeling of love towards God produced by the knowledge of the greatness of Isvara and by vairagya or detachment to the objects of the world. He, in his Nyaya-Sudha describes bhakti as uninterrupted flow of love for God which is beyond any obstruction and which is much greater than love for one’s self. It involves knowledge of the Lord’s infinite number of benign and auspicious qualities.

This means that there can be no bhakti in Madhva philosophy without the knowledge of glory and magnificence of God (Isvara). Bhakti thus, occupies some what lower place in comparison to knowledge in this system. Vadiraja, the author of Yuktimallika however, tries to assign a higher place to bhakti than knowledge. He observes that usefulness of knowledge lies in the rise of devotion only. It appears that he makes an unsuccessful attempt to raise the status of bhakti which therein occupies inferior position to knowledge.

Though, Madhva, lays an emphasis on bhakti and jnana, it should not be taken to mean that bhakti has been accepted as an auxiliary to jnana, in his system. What the Madhvites have in mind while emphasising on knowledge of the glory of the Lord is the fact that without such knowledge for the study of scriptures and the like, there is no possiblity for the awakening of bhakti in an aspirant. In fact, Madhva points out that the achievement of liberation is not possible without the grace of the Lord and the grace of the Lord is not possible without bhakti and bhakti in turn is not possible without knowledge of Him. Hence bhakti has been accepted as an inalienable part of knowledge. It has been so conceived because knowledge in this system is not merely an abstract intellectual apprehension of Reality it is vivid perception of the Supreme Reality as the foundation of one’s own existence with utmost warmth of love.

Madhva regards that the rituals when performed with knowledge are useful. He also admits the necessity of moral and intellectual discipline. The former is helpful in purifying the soul while the latter helps in awakening its faith in God. He cannot be gained by meritorious deeds but the faith of an aspirant is definitely responded to when He is pleased with devotion. When an aspirant surrenders himself in all meekness before the object of worship and has full faith in him, Lord’s benediction is conferred upon him.

According to Padmanabha, the author of Madhva Siddhanta Sara, an exponent of Madhva School, worship or upasana is classified under two heads (1) continuous worship in the form of study of sastras and (2) uninterrupted worship in the form of meditation (dhyana). dhyana or meditation is further defined as a continual thinking of Lord leaving all other thingsaside.

sadhkas can embark on the path of such a meditation on God as the spirit, the existent and having pure consciousness and bliss only when they have developed within themselves a firm conviction by thorough study of scriptural texts and having rational thinking which results on the removal of their doubts and false ideas.

Bhakti has been defined by Vallabha as firm and overwhelming feeling of love for God together with the full knowledge of His greatness and glory. Such bhakti is regarded as the sole means for emancipation. This definition of bhakti by Vallabha is like that of Madhva. It is therefore, obvious that Vallabha also believes in the necessity of knowledge no less than Madhva and Ramanuja. While referring to Tattvadipa-prakasa, Gopesvaraji Maharaja in his Bhakti Martanda observes that bhakti, according to Vallabha means sneha or affection, (sneho-bhaktih). Since the term bhakti has been derived from the root bhaj (service) with the suffix ktin (love) the word bhakti etymologically, connotes seva or service in addition to sneha or love. He thus tries to prove that prema and seva both are denoted by the term Bhakti. He further develops the concept of bhakti to show that the aspirant who performs this seva of Lord Krsna ultimately becomes one with Him through sadhana.

According to Vallabhites, bhakti is of two kinds, viz. (1) Marvada -bhakti which is enjoined with the performance of Vedic duties, and (2) Pustibhakti which implies unconditional descent of the grace of the Lord on the aspirant, marvadabhakti is a means to the attainment of sayujya-mukti which is the end. The aspirant who follows maryadabhakti has to perform Vedic duties. He has to acquire the knowledge of the nature of Aksara Brahman by means of following the nine modes of bhakti as sravana, kirtana etc. and becomes one with Brahman in the last resort. The aspirant who follows this kind of bhakti always desires for the fruits of his actions, so this bhakti is not considered as of the highest quality.

As regards pustibhakti, the aspirant has no other desire than the Lord Himself, as He is the only object of his desires and action,and he is so unmotivated that he is even regardless for the attainment of mukti or emancipation.

Pustibhakti is an end-in-itself. It culminates in the realization of the intrinsic nature of Lord Krsna-in-Person and enables the aspirant to partake in the Bliss of the Lord and His sportive nature. Only a few fortunate souls who are chosen by the Lord and are bestowed with His benediction, have the previlege of partaking in Lord’s Lila i.e., sportive nature.

Pusti, according to Vallabha, has been defined as the grace of the Lord. It is an attribute of Lord Krsna, the operation of which is not conditioned by such factors like time and space etc. (kaladibadhika). Pusti or divine grace, as it descends on the aspirant has different kinds of manifestation, as regards the fruits, they are mundane and supra-mandane. They are emumerated as (1) samanya pusti i.e., ordinary, (2) visista pusti or special, (3) mahapusti or par excellence.

samanya pusti or grace of the ordinary type is that by the operation of which the aspirant attains four ends of human life, viz., dharma, artha, kama and moksa, and some sadhakas are capable of performing worship etc. and repeating the divine name, visista pusti or grace of the special order is that which helps the aspirant in the realization of Lord’s intrinsic nature and the bhakti thus resulting from such special favour of the Lord is called pusti bhakti, maha pusti is the favour or grace of t he Lord Par-Excellence. It operates in those individuals who are helpless to reach the ultimate goal of life for some reason or the other. The grace of the Lord Par-Excellence is however, capable of removing all obstruction in the way of the individuals due to their nature and also caused by their past bad actions. It is illustrated in the Bhagavata by the examples of Pariksita who was saved by the grace of the Lord from the divine weapon of Asvatthama-son of Drona, and same is the case with the sons of Diti.

According to Balakrsna Bhatta, Pustibhakti has been classified into four categories of viz.(l) Pravaha Pusti hhakti, (2) Marvada Pustibhakti, (3) Pusti-Pustihhakti and (4) Suddhapusti Bhakti.

(1) Pravaha-Pusti-Bhakti in which Pravaha stands for a continuous consciousness of one’s ego and sense of ‘mineness’ etc. in the form of a stream and all this results in empirical existence. So the Pustibhakti arising from such Pravaha, where the interest of performing of actions is shifted to that suited to the nature of the Lord, is called Pravaha pustibhakti.

(2) Marvada-Pusti-bhakti is that condition of maryada in which there is attitude of detachment, attitude towards the wordly pursuits and the interest is shifted to having of Lord’s beauty, sports etc. and thus this cultivation of devotion to God deserves the name of Maryada Pusti Bhakti.

(3) Pusti-Pustibhakti is applicable to those aspirants who are already following the path of Pustibhakti by special grace of the Lord, are the recipients of another favour of the Lord. By the operation of this additional favour they are enlightened more and thus get the privilege of knowing all about the nature of the Lord and His Sports etc. Such bhaktas deserve the name of Pustibhaktas.

(4) suddha-pusti-bhakti in which the cultivation of bhakti is actuated by way of pure love and affection towards the Lord for its own sake. According to suddha-pusti-bhakti, the service of the Lord is performed without any other motive or desire. This is the highest of all and the followers of which are generally very rare. Here the feeling of love and devotion is natural and spontaneous and is not a result of one’s efforts. The bhakti of the highest order is found only in the Gopis of Vrindavana and by this Supreme devotion the Lord is subjugated. The Love of Gopis is for the sake of Lord alone and there is no sense of self-enjoyment in isolation. It may be noted that the desire for Moksa is not totally absent in the first three varieties of Pusti-bhakti, whereas the fourth variety stands so high that it attaches no importance to any kind of mukti whatsoever.

Certain features of Suddhapusti-marga are briefly stated below as described by Hariraya in the Prameya Ratnarnava of Bala Krsna Bhatta. It has been said that the aspirant following this particular path is not required to perform any sadhana or course of discipline for the achievement of supreme goal, and is dependent entirely on Lord’s Grace. The Lord selects the individuals out of His free will for Grace and looking from His point of view, there is no question of any pre-requisite for the selection. Similarly, aspirants are incapable of judging the rightness or otherwise of Lord’s action of bestowing grace on particular individuals. This is technically, called ahetuki-bhakti that is, bhakti not measurable in terms of human reason. As soon as Suddhapusti bhakti arises in the devotee as a result of divine grace, he experiences supreme felicity arising as it were, out of his union with the Lord, union both physical and mental. This experience of felicity persists even when there is a separation of the devotee from the Lord. The devotee is generally indifferent to all things as he experiences repugnance for mundane objects, but at the same time, he desires for the preservance of his body as he always hopes for re-union even at the time of separation.

Bhakti has been defined by Nimbarka as a special kind of deep love for the Lord and which can be attained through the grace of the Lord alone. Grace of the Lord causes a feeling of dainva or humility in the heart of the aspirant which in turn generates devotion. Unlike Ramanuja, Nimbark considers upasana and bhakti as two different terms, though they involve each other.

Purusottama in his Vedanta Ratna Manjusa defines bhakti to be an experience of continual remembrance of the Lord and which is ever-increasing.He compares it with the uninterrupted flow of the Ganges-water which is ever-increasing. It has been further defined by him as the service of the Lord for its own sake. It also implies to the performance of worship with body, mind, speech and action.

Nimbarka speaks of two kinds of bhakti or devotion, viz. (1) Apara or Sadhanarupika and (2) Para or Phalarupa.

(1) Sadhanarupika-bhakti which is denoted by the word itself, means that bhakti which is generated through sadhana or the performance of the meritorious deeds of an aspirant during series of his births and deaths. Or the means of practising parabhakti is named as Sadhanarupika-bhakti.

The Sadhanrupika-bhakti is said to be of two kinds, viz., (i) Vedic sadhanarupika and (ii) Pauranic-sadhanarupika. The former implies those activities which are prescribed in the Vedas such as Madhu-vidya, Sandilya vidyaetc. The performance of such vidyas is confined to the upper three classes only, as is stated in the foregoing paragraphs also, while the latter i.e., pauranic Sadhanarupika is meant for all the classes including the Sudras. Since this variety is based on injuctions of Puranas where no distinction of caste and creed is made and all are allowed to practise the way of such bhakti.

(2) Phalarupa bhakti is that devotion which involves the knowledge of God and the relation of God to the world of finite beings and things. Awakening of such knowledge however, arises only by the grace of the Lord when He is so pleased by the performance of unmotivated actions for the sake of Lord alone. It is called Para and Prema-laksana-bhakti also. It inculcates such a continuous and spontaneous inclination of mind towards the Lord, His intrinsic nature, His good and auspicious qualities. The inclination of mind is compared to the spontaneous and natural inclination of our senses towards their respective objects.

The Phalarupa bhakti is superior to sadhana-rupika and is an end-in-itself. It culminates in the supreme love of God for its own sake. This kind of bhakti can be attained only through krpa or favour of the Lord. The followers of this kind of devotion serve the Lord in all possible ways.

The conception of bhakti in Nimbarka School is very much similar to that of the Gaudiyas. We have so far discussed in brief the nature of Bhakti in the Nimbarka system, we propose to examine in detail, the Gaudiya conception of Bhakti in the next chapter.

## Chapter VI - The Concept Of Bhakti In Gaudiya Vaisnavism

In the foregoing chapter we have traced the origin and development of the concept of bhakti right from the Vedas. We have examined how Bhakti which originally formed one of the different ways of God-realisation later came to occupy a prominent position in the Vaisnava systems - so much so that Bhakti came to be regarded as only efficacious means of God-realization. Bhakti, in the beginning, was regarded as of the nature of meditation (dhyana) on God and His worship (upasana) but later on Bhakti came to denote loving service of God. Bhakti, in the early Vaisnava schools was blended with inana (knowledge), but as we shall see in course of our discussion of Gaudiya conception of Bhakti it was totally divorced from jnana: In fact, the Gaudiyas regard inana and karma to be very much inferior to Bhakti.

We begin our exposition of Gaudiya concept of Bhakti as Sri Rupa Gosvami has defined in the following manner:

*“anyabhilasitasunyamjnanakarmadyanavrtam*  
*anukulyenakrsnanusilanam bhaktiruttama”.*

That is, Uttama bhakti is the constant practice of service of Lord Krsna-i.e., service which is agreeable to Him, and which is unadulterated by any extraneous desires, and is free from such avaranas (contaminations) as inana (knowledge) karma (action) and yoga etc. If we analyse this definition of Uttama bhakti, we find that it contains two laksanas, viz. (i) tastastha-laksana (description of extrinsic character) and svarupa-laksana (description of intrinsic character). The first part of the definition gives two characteristics of bhakti which constitutes the tatastha laksana or the relational description of bhakti. These are (i) anvabhilasita sunyam and (ii) jnanakarmadyanavrtam. The term anyabhilasita sunyam i.e., cultivation of bhakti not prompted by any extraneous motives means that bhakti must be practised for the sake of bhakti. By implication, it follows that bhakti is self-justified and self established. It does not derive its value and validity from, any extraneous factors. The term ‘jnanakarma-dynavrtam implies that true bhakti is also uncontaminated by jnana karma etc. In other words, true bhakti is not possible by way of knowledge which has been established (taught) by the Advaitins of Sankara School. When one seeks to realize his identity with undifferentiated Absolute following the path of knowledge, there is no room left for the practice of bhakti in it. But the knowledge concerning the way of bhakti is not to be eliminated.

In the same way, true bhakti is not possible if one follows the path of karma (action), because the practice of varna sarmadharma and the Vedic sacrifices are opposed to bhakti by their very nature. The practice of such actions gives rise to merits, which in turn, cause the achievement of svarga or heaven only. But the actions which are in consonance with the practice of worship are welcomed.

Nor, bhakti is prompted by the way of yoga which ensures Kaivalya i.e., self’s freedom from all that appertains to non-self. Hence it has been said that one can never taste bhakti or the nectar of true love of God by following the path of knowledge, action and yoga etc..

The svarupa laksana of Uttama-bhakti constitutes the second part of the definition, i.e., ankulyena Krsnanusilanam. The adjective ‘anukulyena’ implies to the ways that are agreeable to Lord Krsna, and Krsnanusilanam means practice of the service of Lord Krsna. For example, one may have SriKrsna constantly in his mind actuated with the feeling of antagonism and hostility (pratikulya) towards Him. Such constantthinking of Lord Krsna as a deadly enemy does not deserve the name of bhakti in the true sense. It expresses itself in acts that are not agreeable to the Lord and therefore, is not bhakti at all, but only animoisity as distinguished from the constant thinking of God with real zest and relish, and restoring to various ways and means to serve and please Him, are agreeable to Lord Krsna. It therefore, constitutes ‘anukulyena Krsnanusilanam’ which is bhakti in the true sense .

Sri Visva Natha Cakravarti commenting on Rupa Gosvami’s samanya laksana of bhakti says that if Krsnanusilanam i.e., bhakti when cultivated in a way that is not distasteful to Krsna Himself, but is, on the contrary, agreeable to Him, it deserves, the name of bhakti. Further, such bhakti as service of Lord Krsna is uttama i.e., of the highest quality when it is anyabhilasitasunvam or free from all desires for any thing other than bhakti, that is, when it is free from bhogavasana (bhukti) or desire for worldly enjoyments and free from moksavasana or desire for moksa or liberation. Further, it is true bhakti when it is practised according to the way of bhakti, i.e., through sravana, kirtana etc.

When bhakti is practised as an ancillary to nirvisesa-Brahma-inana or as an element in the performance of the duties laid down in the karmakanda of the Vedas, i.e., as part and parcel of the practice of the sacrificial cults laid down in the Vedas or as part and parcel of the varnasramadharmas prescribed for the different social classes and different stages of life. Or again, when bhakti is practised as an element of or means to voga-sadhana, it is not bhakti, pure and undefiled.

The meaning is that bhakti must be practised in the prescribed way laid down for the practice of devotion. Thus alone, it is bhakti in the true sense. When the practice of bhakti gets mixed up with other forms of sadhana i.e., with the way of jnana or way of karma, it is misra-bhakti and not svarupasiddha or bhakti in its ownWe may note here that Jiva Gosvami in the Bhakti-sandarbha m akes a distinction between Aropa-Siddha, Sangasiddha and Svarupasiddha. Aropasiddha-bhakti arises when all actions are dedicated to Krsna even if there is no self-evident germinal bhakti. Sarigasiddha-bhakti also has no self-evident germinal bhakti (svato-bhaktitva); still knowledge and action have been prescribed as components of bhakti, for instance, when the Bhagavata Purana says that one must learn every thing from the god like guru, it is an injunction for knowledge. Similar is the injunction, 'divert one’s mind from all attachments and keep the company of saintly persons which prescribes action'. The result of the combined performance of such injunctions as components of bhakti is known as Sangasiddha bhakti.

The third type is generated by sravana, kirtana etc. which by their own potency induce bhakti even in a congenital idiot or a mad man. This is known as svarupa-siddha-bhakti.

All these three varieties of bhakti again may be classified into two broad divisions of (i) akaitava and (ii) sakitava. Aropasiddha-bhakti and sarigasiddha-bhakti are called as akaitava when they are begotten in persons capable of receiving it and there is no desire other than bhakti. But if they are practised to produce some result either for self or for someone else, they are called sakaitava. In the same way, if one concentrates on serving Bhagavan, mindful only of His Glory and depends only on Him, it is called svarupasiddha-akaitava or akincana-bhakti, otherwise it would be called sakaitava.

It is pointed out that even though karma (action) either vedic i.e., scriptural or laukika i.e., non-scriptural, is not a form of bhakti ,yet, by aropa or dedication to the Lord such actions acquire the character of bhakti as bhagavaddharma.

Here again, a further distinction is made between actions dedicated to God for the sake of the pleasure of the Lord, and actions which are dedicated to the Lord irrespective of any motive of conducing to the Lord’s pleasure. The latter applies especially to such nonscriptural actions that are considered morally reprehensible, even such actions dedicated to God, though not for His pleasure, acquire the character of bhakti as bhagavaddharma. One may,for example, consider oneself as in his embodied empirical being to be incapable of escaping sinful actions, and one may therefore, dedicate all his actions good or bad to God Himself . Dedication of karma in the belief that one is a mere instrument of the divine purpose and has no will strictly speaking, of his own in whatever one does, makes such actions, acquire the character of bhagavaddharma or bhakti.

Further, it is said that when there is action with phalanyasa or renounciation of the result and its dedication to God, that also deserves the name of the dedication of action to God. The possible occasions for such dedication are alternately (1) desire for fruits (kamana). (2) cessation of desire for fruits, (naiskarmya) and (3) renunciation of acts solely for the pleasure of God which is pure bhakti (bhaktimatra) Of these, the first i.e., desire for fruits of actions is not commendable, the second, which is desirelessness, though preferable to the first one, is not possible by itself, since whatever is done by man is with some motive or the other. Hence the third variety only deserves the name of that, whatever a bhakta does or acts, it is, for the pleasure of the Lord alone.

In fact, karmamisra-bhakti is found to be accompanied either (1) by some specific desire for worldly objects (sakama) or (2) by the desire for kaivalya or liberation (kaivalya-kama) or (3) by the desire for pure bhakti alone (bhakti-matrakama).

According to the first variety, the devotee has to indulge in some activities due to the instructions from above (godly beings), it is thus called sakama-bhakti mixed with karma or action. For instance, Rsi Kardama practised austerities for about thousands years with full devotion and concentration of mind for reproduction as ordered by Brahma.

The second i.e., kaivalyakama may be mixed with both karma and jnana or with jnana alone. The term jnana however, is not used in the sense of ordinary knowledge; but signifies perception of complete identity of the jiva and Brahman (jnana-caikatmadarsanam)and also the knowledge of the scriptures. In case of yoga-misra-bhakti, the term yoga implies meditation with a view to have union with Paramatman. As a mode of attaining the highest good, none of these varieties is equal to pure bhakti or suddha-bhakti which aims exclusively at the loving service of the Lord as the dearest and the nearest one, even regardless of all else including his ownself.

Uttama-bhakti has been characterised by reference to its six effects viz. (i) klesaghnatvam or which removes sufferings, (ii) subhadatvam which confers goodness and happiness, (iii) moksa-laghutakrtvam i.e., which makes the delight or joy of liberation as insignificant, (iv) sudurlabhatvam i.e., which is difficult for attainment, (v) sandranda-visesatma i.e., which constitutes a special kind of ecstatic delight, and (vi) Srikrsnakarsini i.e., which is said to be alluring Lord Krsna by sujugating Him.

According to the first viz. Klesaghnatvam which comprises of three sub-divisions viz. (i) klesas or suffering due to papa or sins, (ii) or due to the seed of sin (papa-bija) which is nothing but vasana or desire for empirical pleasures, or (ii) due to avidya or nescience.

papa or sin is of two kinds, (a) prarabdham or that which remains in the self and has not yet started its fruition.

Uttama-bhakti confers all kinds of good on the devotee such as good qualities which make him the object of attraction for others. It also bestows happiness or sukhawhich is said to be of three kinds uiz.(i) vaisayika-sukham or pleasure arising out of earthly objects (ii) brahmasukham i.e., the pleasure or bliss that constitutes the realization of Brahman or the Absolute Reality and (iii) aisvaram or joy of the love of Supreme God for its own sake.

The third effect of Uttama-bhakti makes the joys and bliss of emancipation as pale asunder and has no attraction for a real bhakta.

Uttama-bhakti is said to be difficult to attain. It is t he gift of God Himself. He showers His benediction of granting devotion to a few and rare persons only, though He seems to be very liberal in bestowing emancipation to all and many.

Uttama-bhakti as sandrananda-visesatma reveals its nature in a special kind of delight which is due to the hladini-vritti or joy giving function of the intrinsic power of the Lord that makes its appearance in the heart of the devotee as love of God for its own sake.

Lastly, it is said that bhakti which can captivate the heart of Lord Krsna is called Krsnakarsini. He being the master of mayasakti is ever free and above all kinds of allurements and charms but He is bound by bhakti i.e., loving service done by his devotee exclusively for God. Thus, being subjugated and attracted He enjoys the love of his devotee through personal appearance before him.

It is to be noted that out of these six effects of Uttama bhakti, the sadhana-bhakti contains only first I wo effects, and Bhava-bhakti as next higher stage of sadhana-bhakti has other two effects in addition to the first two. But Premabhakti is said to include next two effects in addition to the first four effects which are included in Bhavabhakti and Sadhana bhakti. This has been illustrated by Sri Jiva Gosvami by the analogy of five elements of prakrti (air, water etc.).

It may be noted here that avidya or ignorance is the source of the finite individual’s empirical life and its bondage to the earth and its interests. This is common to all Indian Schools of Philosophy-heterodox and orthodox, even Buddhism is not an exception to this. In Christianity also, there is concept of original sin or the sin of the first man that corrupts the whole human race. There is however, a difference here between the Indian and the Christian approaches to the problem of bondage and salvation. While according to the Christian view, the sin of Adam's acts vicariously corrupting the whole human race and Christ, sacrificed on the Cross, redeems the whole human race vicariously. According to Indian view, no body suffers for another’s deeds, though no deed goes without bearing fruit in the form of pleasure or pain. Hence the question of vicarious suffering or of vacarious redumption has to be ruled out in the Indian view. We reap what we sow. There is no krta-pranas’a i.e., no destruction of the fruits of ones action, nor is there akrta-abhyagama i.e., enjoyment or suffering that has not been earned by one’s own deeds. This is formulated by the Indians in their doctrine of karma which is an autonomous inexorable law that rewards or punishes according to one’s action. That, we are in the grip of the law of karma and as participators in the ups and downs of mundane life is due to avidya or a beginningless ignorance. And avidya, according to the general Indian view-both Heterodox and Orthodox-can be cancelled by vidya or intuition of the true nature of Reality. But the Gaudiyas hold that there is no escape from samsara or empirical life without the krpa or grace of Lord Krsna and in special cases the Lord’s mercy descends in the form of the pure unalloyed bhakti in the heart of the devotee. The appearance of bhakti through the Lord’s grace makes even liberation appear to be of no consequence.

In this respect,the Gaudiyas may be regarded as Leap philosophers, a term used by Karl H. Potter in his Presupositons of Indian Philosophy for those philosophers who “believe in salvation through a sudden act of grace of the Lord as distinguished from the progress philosophers who believe in liberation through sustained practice of jnana (knowledge) and karma (Action).

Uttama-Bhakti or devotion of the highest quality as defined above, is further described as being of two kinds; viz. (i) Sadhana bhakti i.e., devotion as means and (ii) Sadhya bhakti i.e., devotion as end.

Sadhana-bhakti signifies devotion attainable by special external effort such as sravana (hearing) and kirtana (chanting) etc. While Sadhya-bhakti consists of the feelings, emotions or sentiments believed to be nitva-siddha or eternally accomplished and generated, though revealed through the practice of different forms of Sadhana-bhakti. In other words, Sadhana bhakti lays an emphasis on the performance of the acts of hearing (sravana) and chanting (kirtana) the holy name etc. while in Sadhya-bhakti (or bhavabhakti) the interest shifts from the outward acts to the inward feelings that are generated by the external actions. Sadhya-bhakti or devotion as end, is again of two kinds, viz. (i) Bhavabhakti or devotion resulting from spontaneous inward fueling or bhakti in the primal stage of rudimentary feelings and emotions, (ii) Prema-bhakti or devotion matured into a sentiment of love, that is bhakti as fully developed love of God for its own sake.

Now, we shall describe the two varieties of Sadhana-bhakti viz. Vaidhi and Raganuga. Of these two varieties, the first viz. Vaidhi-bhakti is defined as follows:

Sadhana-bhakti is vaidhi-bhakti when sadhana or the various acts of hearing, chanting etc. are prompted by the fear of these being prescribed by the scriptures and therefore, non-observance thereof would entail the sin of violating sastric injunctions.

In explaining the above definition, it is pointed out that the injunctions, ‘Thou shalt always recollect Visnu in Thy mind and Thou shalt not forget Visnu on any account," are the basic injunctions of the scriptures. All other injunctions and prohibitions are secondary to the basic injunctions about Visnusmarana or mental contemplation of Visnu and avoiding every lap resulting in ‘Visnu-vismarana’ or forget-fulness of Lord Visnu. Other injunctions and prohibitions are subservient to the primary injunctions and are practised in disregard of the basic or primary injunctions, which bear no fruit. If knowing that sastras or the scriptures have laid down the Visnusmarna as the primary injunction without which the practice of any other vidhi (injunction) or nisedha (prohibition) is fruitless, one resorts to the observance of hearing about Visnu, chanting the name of Visnu and other similar acts stated in the scriptures as obligatory on devotee, one is called a Sadhana-bhakta of the Vidhi type i.e., a devotee prompted to the acts of hearing, chanting etc. because they are prescribed by the scriptures as primary duties.

In other words, Vaidhi-bhakti is the practice of sravana, kirtana etc. about Visnu arising as it does, from the consciousness that they are prescribed by the sastras as primary duties. Such practice has to be continued till feelings, emotions and sentiments appear in the mind of the devotee. After the appearance of bhava, however, Vaidhi bhakti i.e., practice of hearing and chanting etc. out of reverence for scriptures has no longer any application. In other words, the field of Vaidhi-bhakti extends only upto the appearance of bhava in the bhakta or devotee. Vaidhi Marga ceases to be obligatory; Strictly speaking, when bhava makes its appearance, one may have to resort to the practice of hearing, chanting etc. either from raga or from real liking according to one’s taste or preference.

According to Sri Rupa Gosvami, there are sixty four angas or means of Vaidhi bhakti. of which the following twenty-five are very prominent:

- 1\. Gurupadasraya - submission to the feet of the Guru or the guide.
- 2\. Krsnadiksadisiksanam - receiving training from him in spiritual initiation and instruction regarding Sri Krsna.
- 3\. Visrambhena-guru-sevanam - serving the spiritual teacher with real zest and delight.
- 4\. Sadhu-vartmanuvartanam - following the path of the saintly persons.
- 5\. Saddharma Prccha - query about true or real dharma.
- 6\. Bhogaditvagah Krsnasvahetave - Giving up all worldly enjoyments for the sake of Krsna.
- 7\. Tirthavasa - residing in places of pilgrimage (like Dvaraka, Mathura etc.)
- 8\. Bahu-grantha-kala-bhyasa-vyakhva-vada-viyar-janam - giving up the studies of various books with the exception of scriptures and abstaining from the practice of various arts and all sorts of controversy.
- 9\. Vaisnava cinha dharana - putting on the external marks of a Vaisnava.
- 10\. Harinamaksaradharana - Putting down the name of Hari on the body by means of sandal paste etc.
- 11\. Dandavan-natih - making obeisance by falling prostrate like a staff.
- 12\. Arcana - worship.
- 13\. Parikrama - going round the image of the Lord.
- 14\. Japa - muttering the holy name of the Lord.
- 15\. Gita - singing of the name.
- 16\. Kirtana - chanting the name in a loud voice.
- 17\. Naivedyasvada - partaking of the offering made to the Lord.
- 18\. Padyasvada - partaking of the water used in washing the feet of the holy image of the Lord.
- 19\. Harivasarasammana - observing the days of fasting, especially Ekadasi.
- 20\. Janmastami etc. - celebrating the birth festival of the Lord and similar festivals.
- 21\. Constant attendance upon Tulasi plant.
- 22\. Sri Krsna vimukhajana-sarigatyaga – shunning from the association of those who are averse to Sri Krsna.
- 23\. Residing in Mathura for all the time.
- 24\. Unfailing attendance on Vaisnava devotees.
- 25\. Constant dependence on Srimadbhagavata Purana.

Of these above mentioned acts of piety, the first three ways of cultivation are however, attached special value, but according to Krsnadasa Kaviraja, the chief ways of cultivation of Vaidhi-bhakti are as follows:

- 1\. Sadhusamga .e., association with saintly persons.
- 2\. Namakirtana - i.e., chanting of the name of Krsna.
- 3\. Bhagavata-sravana - i.e., listening to the reading of the Bhagavata-Purana.
- 4\. Mathura vasah- i.e., dwelling at Mathura.
- 5\. Srimurtir Sraddhavasevanam - i.e., serving the image of the Lord with reverence. Any one of these modes, even a little of the five, can create Bhakti.

According to Sri Jiva Gosvami’s Bhakti Sandarbha, the elements of Vaidhi-bhakti are enumerated as eleven: (1) Saranapatti, (2) Guruseva, (3) Sravana, (4) Kirtana, (5) Smarana, (6) Pada-seva, (7) Arcana, (8) Vandana, (9)Dasva, (10) Sakhva, and (11) Atmanivedanam.

1\. Saranapatti i.e., resorting to the Lord as the exclusive refuge. It arises either (i) through the inborn belief about the absence of any other refuge which is permanent or (ii) through giving up all other alternatives followed unwisely; This mental state is based on the six elements mentioned in Vaisnava-tantra, viz. (a) Anukulyasya Samkalpah i.e. firm determination of following the ways that are agreeable to the Lord, (b) pratikulyasya-vivarjanam i.e., forsaking of things which are not agreeable to Him, (c) raksisyatiti-visvasah i.e., having firm faith that He must protect, (d) Goptrtve-varanam i.e., choosing Him as the saviour, (e) atmaniksepa i.e., complete self surrender and (f) Karpanya i.e., humility as an object of sympathy or compassion.

2\. Guruseva or the service of the preceptor or the spiritual guide plays an important role in all the Vaisnavite traditions. He is regarded as high as the deity itself. Since the spiritual guide is supposed to have traversed long and secret way of spiritual illumination. By serving such a preceptor the Lord is pleased. It is also believed that the satisfaction or pleasure of the spiritual guide causes removing of all kinds of obstacles in the practice of the way of devotion. It also helps the disciple in the achievement of divine benediction of the Lord.

It is also pointed out that if a spiritual guide is incapable of guiding the disciple, has no real knowledge of the deities, and is self conceited and does not follow the true means of Vaisnava faith, should be abandoned by the disciple.

The next stage is (3) Sravana or listening to the name form, lila and attributes of the Lord. Listening to Sri Madbhagavata is regarded as the best way of hearing. Constant listening to the qualities etc. of the Lord causes a desire for loving service of the Lord in place of having emancipation.

The next stage which is closely allied to the previous is (4) Kirtanai.e., chanting or singing the name, form and attributes etc. of the Lord with full voice. It is considered as the most powerful means of effecting an attitude of devotion. Chanting of sweet and holy name of the Lord does not have any restrictions of time, place or person. Thus, it is highly efficacious means especially in Kaliyugai.e., the present decadent period. By chanting the name of the Lord, the devotee attains the stage of bhava i.e., there arises the sentiment of love and his heart begins to melt.

Thus, by following the previous stages, the devotee indulges in remembering the name form etc. of the Lord through his mental process. Hence (5) smarana implies the act of remembering through the concentration of mind. This also constitutes the purity of the heart. It is said to be of five kinds(i) samanyam - i.e., fixing up one’s mind on the deity in a passing way ; (ii) dharna i.e., fixing of one’s mind in a general way by withdrawing it from all other sides, (iii) dhyana i.e., special concentration of mind regarding the name, form and sport of the Lord, (iv) dhruvanusmrti i.e., uninterrupted flow of mind in remembring the name., form etc. of the Lord. This flow has been compared to a continuous stream of nectar, (v) samadhi implies the appearance of the object of thinking exclusively in the mind, but this state is different from the asamprajnata-Brahma-Samadhi.

(6) The next stage is padaseva which includes various acts of seeing, touching or going round (parikrama) the image of the deity, following the procession of the image (anuvrajanam). dwelling in holy place, pilgrimage etc.. Living in a holy place and pilgrimage etc. provide sufficient scope for associating with the holy and saintly persons.

(7) Arcana in which includes various rites of worship as directed by the spiritual guide and from the Agamas.

Although the Bhagavata Mahapurana does not agree with the Pancaratra scriptures in enjoining the performance of ceremonial worship as compulsory, yet there is no objection to such performance by those who like to follow the way of Pancaratra in accordance with Diksa received by them. It is mentioned in the Bhagavata that recourse to anyone of the modes of worship such as Saranapatti etc. is sufficient means in the achievement of final and the highest end55. It may be noted that the overt acts of ceremonial worship are intended specially for wealthy house-holders who can make use of their wealth in this way.

(8) Vandana implies the process of namaskara in falling prostrate like an unsupported stick (dandavat pranama). Vandana, though a part of Arcana the previous stage, yet is mentioned separately in order to show that it may be employed independently as smarana and kirtana etc.

(9) Dasyam which includes not only the actual service rendered by the devotee but also the feeling of servitude. If this feeling of servitude is fully realised and developed as an exclusive effort of worship, it is enough for the attainment of desired end. Hence Dasya is mentioned separately as a way of bhakti.

(10) Sakhya or the sense of friendship which like Dasya. follows from the very nature of the relation of the Jiva and Bhagavan. The last stage is reached in

(11) atmanivedanam i.e., complete self-surrender. It involves the feeling of one’s body, mind, senses and soul etc. all intended for the deity. This feeling is so cultivated that there remains no effort for one’s own self. Each and every action or effort is directed towards the deity. It thus involves the dedication of the self as a means and as an end also, to Bhagavan.

It is also pointed out by Sri Jiva Gosvami that these eleven elements are inter-related to one-another. Occasional praises of one or the other in the scripturesshould be taken to mean only that the one or the other should be adopted and practised according to the capacity of the individual sadhaka.

Inspite of its certain positive acts and practices, the Vaidhi-sadhana bhakti also includes in its negative aspect, the avoidance of offences such as sevaparadhasand namaparadhas. The lists of sevaparadhas have been accounted for differently by different Vaisnava Sastras. The least number of such offences counted are thirty-two viz. entering the temple without washing one’s feet, non-observance of the festivals of the Lord, and that of the silence during worship, violation of the duty of doing obeisance to the Lord, eating what is not offered to the deity, etc. are the instances of sevaparadhas.

namaparadhas are ten in number viz. (1) slandering saintly persons (satam-ninda). (2) regarding the names and attributes of Siva as independent of those of Visnu, (3) ignoring the spiritual preceptors, (4) finding fault with the scriptures, (5) taking the praises of the divine name as words of exaggeration, (6) cherishing desire to sin, knowing that all sins can be expiated through the chanting of the divine name, (7) equalising the effect of the divine name to the merit of the other pious acts, (8) holding an attitude of indifference to the divine name, (9) considering the interests of the ego superior to the divine name, even though informed of the efficacy of the name, (10) giving instruction about the efficacy of the name to persons devoid of faith.

Before describing the second variety of Sadhana-bhakti, it may be pointed out that a person who is qualified for the way of bhakti must be a sraddhalu or a man of faith. Further, he is unlike the man who is given to the way of works and who performs the Vedic-rites for the realisation of empirical desires. He is also unlike the man who has elected the path of knowledge for attaining liberation from the ills of mundane life. Also, he is not one who is not unattached to world and in this respect he is unlike the person who has chosen the way of knowledge as means to liberation. But this is not all. If such a man elects for the way of bhakti with the belief that though the ways of karma, inana and bhakti are all prescribed as the methods of spiritual discipline, yet, the last viz. the way of bhakti is considered by God Himself as the highest and the best of the three; it is only such a person as deserves the name of a man of real faith. Such a man of real faith, however, may belong to three categories of (i) uttama or the best qualified, (ii) madhyama or the middling and (iii) kanistha or the lowest qualified. One who has mastered the arguments prescribed in the scriptures regarding the ultimate reality and ultimate values and the effective means of realizing these values is qualified for the bhakti-marga. The second best or the middling is one who, though not expert, in the arguments of the scriputures, is yet have faith in the way of bhakti, and the lowest order of the qualified consists of those who do not possess the knowledge of the sastras as it is, and also have only a luke-warm faith in the way of bhakti.

The way of Vaidhibhakti is sometimes called the Marvada-marga or the way of convention, for, as already pointed out, this type of bhakti is conditioned by the injunctions of Vaisnava scriptures. As the devotional practices involved in this type of bhakti are all guided by the fear of transgression of scriptural injunctions and not by any feeling of attachment or devotion to the Lord, it is rightly characterised as formal and mechanical. The importance of Vaidhi-bhakti lies in the fact that if a sadhaka who is lacking in the natural inclination for turning to the Lord, it is indispensable and thus enables the Sadhaka who can pass on to raganuga bhakti through the rigid preparatory disciplines enjoined by the scriptures.

The second variety of sadhana-bhakti viz. Raganuga-bhakti is so called because it follows the line of ragatmika-bhakti, which characterises the residents of Vraia. They are all inspired by the sweetness of Lord Krsna and their devotion is thus a form of spontaneous love or affection for the sweetness that permeates Lord Krsna. This ragatmika bhakti serves the model for the Raganuga-bhakti.

The word raga in Raganuga-bhakti means spontaneous, deep and inseparable absorption in the desired object. According to Sri Jiva Gosvami, raga has been defined as the natural and intense attachment of a subject towards the object of desire. Just as, a sense organ, for instance, an eye has a natural inclination towards its object, so also, the devotee is irresistibly inclined towards the Lord. Distinguished from Vaidhi-bhakti, Raganuga consists in observance of the practice of sravana-kirtana etc. of the name, form and sport of the Lord, not out of reverence for the scriptural injunctions, but out of inner liking or lobha i.e., the keen desire of realizing the feeling of the people of Vraja. The difference between ragatmika-bhakti and raganuga bhakti lies in the fact that the love of Krsna in the inmates of Vraja expresses itself in the various acts of disinterested service of the Lord which is possible in the inmates of Vraja alone, while an ordinary mortal can at best, aspire to love and serve Lord Krsna in the wake of services of these inmates of Vraja. A mortal may use both his physical body and his mind which is better fitted for loving and serving the Lord in the wake of Vraja-vasis. In other words, a devotee must mentally recollect the object of his love viz., Krsna and also all those who are dear to the heart of Krsna, and if the physical body permits, shall live physically in Vrindavana and if not be fitted for physical residence, must live mentally in Vrindavana.

All the activities that are elements of the Vaidhi-bhakti such as sravana, kirtana etc. are also the elements or angas that constitute raganuga-bhakti.

As regards the keen desire or lobha that is, the motive force in the raganuga-bhakti, it is described in

Ragavartma candirka by Visvanatha Cakravarti as follows:

When however, such bhakti springs out of love of or attachment to the madhurya or sweetness of the Lord’s person and qualities, it is raganuga-bhakti. Raganugabhakti is thus motivated by lobha or keen desire for a taste of Lord’s sweet companionship as a near and dear one.

Rupa Gosvami has described this keen desire or lobha as follows :

When one hears the sweetness of the Lord’s person and qualities described, there arises a hankering in the mind for the experience of that sweetness, hankering which does not wait for scriptrual sanction or arguments to establish. This is how the lobha or keen desire appears in the mind. An object of keen desire or lobha arouses desires independently of the scriptural sanction and does not establish one’s fitness or otherwise for the attainment of the object. The mere fact of such an object being an object of sense perception, such as, hearing or seeing, is sufficient to arouse keen desire for its attainment.

Such desire again, for the taste of Lord’s sweetness and of the pleasure of His service arises in the bhakta or devotee either (1) through the grace of the Lord Himself or (2) through the grace of one of His devotees. Of these two again, the lobha or the keen desire that is inspired by the grace of a bhakta is either (1) praktana i.e., due to the grace of a bhakta in a previous life or (2) adhunika i.e., due to the grace of a bhakta in the present life. It is stated in the scriptures that the keen desire that is generated either by the grace of the Lord or by the grace of one of His devotees is the Bhakti-Sadhana that deserves the name of Raganuga-bhakti.

Vallabhacarya and his followers call this kind of bhakti to be Pusti-bhakti, while Vaidhi-bhakti or which is inspired by reverence for the scriptural injunctions is called maryada bhakti. Once, such a desire is aroused either by the Lord’s grace or by the grace of one of His devotees, the sadhaka is actuated to consult the scriptures that describe bhakti-marga or the way of bhakti, for nowhere else is the way described or shown. Hence even Raganuga -bhakti cannot ignore the bhakti-sastras though it is not the Sastra that inspires him to bhakti-sadhana in the first instance, but it is lobha or keen desire for the taste of Lord’s sweetness born of the grace of the Lord or one of His devotees.

In Raganuga-Sadhana bhakti the essential or most important of the activities of bhakti-sadhana is smarana or mental contemplation or rememberence of the Lord. This mental contemplation is the Lord’s representation in one sport or the other appearing in a particular apparel and in a particular mood or emotion in the midst of those who are dear to Him. Chanting etc. must also be followed in the same way. It is further pointed out that according to Lord Krsna’s teachings to His devotee Uddahava, the selfless worship of the Lord out of disinterested love even though lacking in mudranyasa and other accessories of worship does not lose even a portion of its effect so that all that is necessary for Bhakti-sadhana to bear its fruit, is worship of the Lord for its own sake.

It has been already said that the Raganuga-bhakta is motivated in the first place by hearing about the sweetness of the Lord’s close personal relations with the inmates of Vraja. This inspires him with a desire for love and service of the Lord in the wake of one or the other of the forms in which the Lord was loved and served by the said inmates; but, once the desire for such a love and service is awakened in the devotee, he has to consult the bhakti sastrasespecially, the Bhagavata that indicate the various ways of bhakti-sadhana. Such bhakti sadhana or bhajana is further shown to consist of such acts as are tadbhavamaya or saturated with feelings for the Lord or the acts that are tadbhava-sambandhi i.e., relative to such feelings or acts which are anukula or conducive thereto, or again, acts which are not inconsistent or incompatible with such feelings, only such acts which are pratikula or antagonistic to or incompatible with the feelings of Raga or love for Lord Krsna are not to be included in Raganuga-sadhanaespecially in Dwarakadhyana and mudranyasa etc.

It is already stated that the natural attachment to Krsna eternally displayed by the inmates of Vraja is called Ragatmika Bhakti which is the model or source (adarsa) of Raganuga, arises from desire (kama), enmity (dvesa), fear (bhava) or affection (sneha). The Gopis of Vraia were attached to Krsna through the bond of kama or desire, Kamsa was engrossed in the thought of Krsna because of his enemity with Him, Sisupala was possessed by Krsna through fear (bhava) and the Yadavas were bound to Him through a tie of affection. But, of all natural sentiments, enmity and fear are quite uncongenial to Prema-bhakti while affection (sneha) in the sense of fellowship is an aspect of Premabhakti itself. But affection, generally being a form of fellowship would come under the Vaidhi. or of Lord Krsna being an aspect of love, does not come at all under Sadhana bhakti. Therefore, the Ragatmika-Bhakti may be classified into two broad divisions viz., the Kamarupa and the Sambandharupa.

The Kamarupa consists of a desire for erotic enjoyment (sambhoga-trsna) inspired by an exclusive effort to please Krsna (krsna-saukhyarthameva kevalamudyamah)and resulting in the most genuine form of love common only to the Gopis of Vraja (Vraja-Devisu). Since the Gopis of Vraja never had the slightest feeling of desire for their own pleasure, but directed all their efforts towards bringing about the supreme pleasure of Krsna, their kama has been identified with Prema, Kubja too, had an intense desire for sporting with Krsna, but her desire was only for her own sensual pleasure. The attitude of Kubja in this respect has been described as Kama-prava-rati and not as kama-rupa-rati.

The second type of the Ragatmika bhakti, viz., Sambhandhrupa consists of an awareness of relationship of Krsna. It is found for instance, in Nanda, Yosoda, the Gopas etc. Thus, following the above two broad divisions of Ragatmika bhakti raganuga may be subdivided into kamanuga and sambandhanuga. The kamanuga which may again be either desire for enjoyment inspired by a sense of Krsna’s sport (keli-tatparvavatisambho-gecchamaya) or a desire to realise those particular bhavas (tad-tad-bhavecchatmika) of particular gopis especially the erotic feelings (bhava-madhurya-kamita)sambandhanuga may assume different forms according to the different models of personal relationship imitated by the devotee.

We have so far considered sadhanabhakti in its two forms of vaidhi and raganuga. Both these are of the nature of bhaktisadhana or the means to awakening of the feeling of devotion. When such feelings are aroused we pass from sadhana bhakti to sadhya Bhakti, that is, devotion as means to devotion as end to be accomplished. Sadhya Bhakti or Bhakti as end is either (1) Bhava Bhakti or bhakti as rudimentary love of Lord Krsna for His own sake or (2) Premabhakti or bhakti as fully developed love of the Lord.

In the stage of Bhava bhakti the devotion becomes firm (naisthaiki) or steadfast by practising sadhana bhakti again and again or by listening to the pious deeds and sports of the Lord, the evil-mentalities (i.e abhadras) are vanished and thus serving the saintly persons the devotion in the Lord (Bhagavan) becomes firmwhich removes all sorts of ignorance about the way of devotion. According to Jiva Gosvami, firm or steadfast devotion (naisthiki) arises only when the devotee is engaged in continuous remembreance and thought of the Lord without having any tinge of hurdles or klesas. As a result of this firm devotion the sadhaka or the aspirant rises above the level of rajas and tamas and rests in sattvaguna completely satisfied and peaceful, thereof. Thus the purged soul is eligible for tho wake of bhava Bhakti as further defined in the following way.

The bhava bhakti is of the nature of suddha-sattva vises atma or a specific kind of suddha-sattva which is pure sattva and unmixed with rajas and tamas.

Also, it is regarded as the Vrtti or the function of t he svarupasakti or the intrinsic power of the Lord. The svarupa-Sakti functions in the three aspects of (1) samvit-consciousness, or intelligence, (2) sandhini being and hladini or joy giving, suddha-sattva visesa here, means the hladini or joy-giving aspect of the Lord’s svarupasakti in its aspect of the self luminious, intelligence or consciousness. This consciousness reveals itself as joy giving in one of its specific form. This is suddha sattva, visesa as distinguished from suddha sattva as such. And this specific aspect or visesa also indicates the highest quality of joy that characterises the mahabhava or the supreme delight that characterises the love of Radha for Krsna. And thus, by suddha sattva-visesa is meant file desire for the pravrtti or will to serve the Lord in a way agreeable to the Lord that characterises the eternal attendants of the Lord in whose hearts it dwells as the essence of the samvit and the Hladini aspects of His Svarupa sakti. In other words, it is the hladini or the samvit aspect of His intrinsic essence that functions in the mind of the Lord’s eternal attedants as will to serve the Lord in a way that is pleasing to Him. That is to say, suddha sattva visesatma is the Lord’s own intrinsic essence or svarupa as dwelling in the hearts of His eternal beloved associates. Now bhava bhakti as above defined as suddha-sattva visesatma is related to prema-bhakti which is its full development as the rays of the Sun are to the Sun itself. In other words, what is in a germinal or embryonical state, at the stage of Bhava beomes fully formed prema or love at the stage of Premabhakti. In other words, bhakti continues as sadhanabhakti till the appearance of asakti or ruci and the stage between the appearance of a sakti or attachment and fully developed love or prema is a stage of bhava bhakti. Again, what is just the beginning of Bhakti as feeling or sentiment is Bhava-bhakti while prema-bhakti or bhakti as love is bhava bhakti or rudimentary sentiment that has come to its full growth as love or Prema.

Bhava may arise in a sadhaka in two ways, and accordingly, it has been said to be of two kinds ; (1) sadhanabhinivesaja-bhava i.e., sadhana way of worship and (2) Krsna-prasadaja-bhava i.e., due to the grace of Krsna. The former is again of two kinds arising as it does, by following either vaidhi marga or raganuga-margaas described above. The latter, i.e., due to the grace of Krsna may come either through His words (vacika) or through bestowal of His Light (alokadana) or through His cordiality (harda). All bhaktas however are, incapable of receiving grace from the Lord Himself. They, therefore, may receive grace through the followers of Krsna and this is known as Krsna-bhakta-prasadaja-bhava. In this, connection it may be pointed out that bhava Bhakti may arise all of a sudden in some sadhakas who belong to second category, that is, Krsna-bhakta-prasadaja-bhava receipients and as such this does not require any personal effort on the part of the sadhakas.

From what has been said above, it is clear that this bhava-bhakti which is based on inward emotion of devotion is the preliminary stage of development of Preman, which transforms itself into Bhakti-rasa. Bhava-bhakti may arise out of sadhana-bhakti but it is not necessarily resultant of extraneous ways and means of bhakti as it arises in the sadhaka as a personal feeling. Bhava Bhakti has been defined as a unique, pure and inborn feeling which is very much similar in nature to that of first dawn of love and which ushers in smoothness of mind in the sadhakas arising from ruci or relish. About ruci it has been said that this may take in sadhaka as a form of a desire for the Deity or for His favour for His friendship.

Whenever bhava-bhakti sprouts in the sadhakas, the attendant circumstances (anubhavas) automatically make their appearance in him. They, being the placidity of mind (ksanti) an effort to utilise one’s time to successful realization of this feeling, the dispassion for the objets of senses (virakti) lack of pride, bond of hope, eagerness, liking for singing of the name of deity, desire for recital of virtues of the Lord and pleasure in living in the place where the Lord Himself resided during His prakata-lila such as Mathura (Vraja), etc.

It has been stated that bhava-bhakti does not arise in its true form in all sadhakas owing to the continuance of thirst for worldly enjoyments and for liberation, bhava bhakti may take the form of a semblance of this feeling of attachment in such sadhakas which is known as ratvabhasa . This ratyabhasa may appear in the form of either reflection (pratibimba) or chaya or shadow. When ratyabhasa takes the form of pratibimba one or two characteristic signs such as tears etc. appear in the sadhaka and when it takes the form of chaya, only a short lived curiosity for the deity may occur in the sadhaka, ratyabhasa although, is a sembalance of Bhava-bhakti, it has one advantage over Bhava-bhakti i.e., it is attainable by sadhakas belonging to lower category without much difficulty. It has been said that this ratyabhasa may all of a sudden change into real bhava at any time.

Sri Visvanatha Cakravartin in his commentary on Bhakti-Rasamrta-Sindhu, has observed that prema-bhakti is the culmination of bhavabhakti. When bhava-bhakti develops fully or ripens or solidifies into the sentiment of love, i.e., preman, through a process of paripaka, it is known as Prema-bhakti. As a consequence, the sadhaka experiences a kind of smoothness of his heart as it were, which is characterised by exclusive and loving attachment.

It may be pointed out that Prema-bhakti may develop either from vaidhi-bhava or raganuga-bhava. but in some cases it may also arise from the grace of the Lord which is available to only the highest type of devotees . The grace of the Lord may flow to sadhakas independent of all circumstances (i.e., Kevala) and which is called pure grace, or may come as a result of the knowledge of His greatness or glory (mahatmyajnana); former being Raganuga latter following vaidhi-marga.

From what has been said above, it is clear that the steps leading to the development of Prema-bhakti are as follows ; (i) Sraddha or faith - -\> sadhusanga or the association of saintly perons - -\> bhajanakriya or the acts of worship - -\> anarthanivrtti or removal of undesirable barriers - -\> nistha or devoutedness - -\> ruci or relish for the practice of worship - -\> asakti or attachment to and a real craving for the practice of bhakti - -\> bhava or rudimentary feeling of love - -\> preman or fully developed love sentiment. The successive stages of preman will be described in the next chapter.

According to Gaudiyas Prema-bhakti is the highest type of Bhakti - the zenith of uttama-bhakti, which is the aim of all the sadhakas following Vaisnava mode of sadhana. It may be incidentally pointed out that Para-bhakti of Sandilya and Narada Bhakti-sutra are very much similar in essence to the Prema -bhakti of the Gaudiyas.

## Chapter VII - Concept Of Bhakti As Rasa According To The Gaudiyas

Bhakti in Sanskrit poetics has been regarded as a bhava only and therefore, has not been included in the enumeration of rasas1. Probably, under the influence of Vaisnavite traditions, Madhusudana Sarasvati in his Bhaktirasayana, has referred to bhakti as rasa. Before discussing the conception of bhakti as rasa, it would be worthwhile to point out the difference between bhava and Rasa.

Bhava is said to be a kind of feeling and as such is the function of the heart. Bhakti-bhava is the yearning for the Lord on the part of the devotee. The term rasa has been derived from the root ras - meaning a thing that may be tasted or enjoyed. It has also a causative usage in which form it signifies ‘that makes others enjoy’ (i.e., rasayati-iti-rasah).

In Sanskrit poetics, bhava, has been defined as those feelings which arise in heart for a short-time only and which cannot be transformed into rasa even when combined with vibhava-anubhava, etc., while rasa has been defined as a totality of feelings that result from the combination of vibhavas, sthavi-bhavas, sancari-hhavas, sattvika-bhavas anubhavasetc.

A comparison of the significance of these two terms would show that bhava is only a subjective feeling while rasa involves enjoyment which soothes and produces bliss in the subject. Further, bhava, has also been defined by Rupa Gosvami in his Bhakti-rasamarta Sindhu as that feeling which is cherished by an intellectual person with his concentration of mind through deep rooted impressions (sansakaras)while rasa is that wonderful experience which has been fully relished in the pure and undefiled heart transcending the stage of bhavabhava and rasa is that of dhyana (meditation) and samadhi (deep concentration). In other words, bhava is thus a primary stage while rasa is the culminating one.

We have so far discussed bhava and rasa in a general way. Now let us take up whether Bhakti has been accepted as rasa or not. According to the secular exponents of Sanskrit poetics, rati or attachment towards various gods, etc., has been described as bhava only and not rasa. But in reply to this, Madhusudana Sarasvati and other religious exponents have expressed their opinion that bhakti-bhava may develop into ‘rasa’ if it is directed towards the supreme God (i.e. Vasudeva) but as regards the attachment or rati towards other gods, kings, etc., bhakti would remain as bhava only. This is because other gods (except Vasudeva Krsna) belong to mundane plane and this does not apply to Lord Krsna or Vasudeva who is said to be the supra-mundane Godand bliss-incarnate, (akhilarasamrta-murttih)Jiva Gosvami also supported the above view in all respects. The Gaudiyas have, therefore, devoted so much for the development of the theory of Bhakti as rasa. Before we take up for discussion the Gaudiya-conception of bhakti as rasa, let us turn our attention towards the rhetoricians’ theory of rasa.

According to the Sanskrit poetics, the aesthetic delight in poetry is qualitatively different from the pleasure derived from the sense objects in our day-today life. It can be relished or experienced only by a few persons who are well-versed in literature,and are sahrdaya (i.e., possessing aesthetic sense of appreciation), since a man of ordinary intelligence can not raise his mind to identify himself with some idealistic delight. Such pleasure can be experienced after the complete forgetfulness and dissolution of one’s ego. The pleasure of poetry is essentially subtle in nature and only the contemplative mind can realise it. Our emotions are so aroused in the heart that we feel as it were, related to it. This is possible only depending on the manner of representation of things to us. It is also due to our contemplative self which is blissful in nature and free from desires for mundane pursuits and thus whatever it experiences is blissful and joy-giving.

Abhinavagupta in his Locana - a commentary on Dhvanyaloka, compares the pleasure of poetry to that of the realisation of Brahman, while Visvanatha Kaviraja also regards that the joy of poetry to be very much similar to the bliss of the realization of Brahman. Further, the rhetoricians in their treatment of rasa have laid a greater emphasis on camatkara or poetical charm. They hold a view that the very essence of rasa lies in the production of the feeling of astonishment. It is also said that if any experience gives rise to the feeling of wonder mixed with pleasure or bliss in which the activities of the internal organs are temporarily put in abeyance, such experience of pleasure is known as rasa. The relish of such an experience does not depend on external factors, it is rather related to the inner self.

It has, therefore, been held that rasa makes its appearance only in those hearts which are pure and undefiled and when the two gunas of prakrti viz. rajas and tamas are reduced to the minimum and the third viz. sattva becomes dominant. The preponderance of sattva is inseparably related to the production of rasa without which it cannot be experienced. The rhetoricians also consider the relish of poetry as supernatural or alaukika in nature.

The Gaudiyas in their description of rasa have agreed with the secular theorists on several points, viz. (i) the experience of camatkara or i.e., poetical charm, and (ii) sukha or saubhaga or bliss - as the two essential ingredients of rasa. But they also hold that the experience of bliss which is tasted by a bhakta is divine in nature; arising as it does, from the aprakrta-visuddha-sattva that which constitutes the body of the Lord, while the bliss of the secular theorists is one arising out of the preponderance of prakrta-sattva only.

The Gaudiyas, however, reject the view of the rhetoricians regarding the super-natural element in relish, on the ground that the ‘super-natural’ or alaukikatva in poetics is not aesthetic delight in the true sense of the term. It, in fact, is only a ‘super-normal’ experience of mundane joy. They, therefore, do not attach much importance to this kind of mundane experience, despite the fact that it contains an element of camatkara or astonishment.

In fact, the rhetoricians view that literary ‘rasa’ is on the same plane as the experience of bliss of Brahman, has been scoffed at by the Gaudiyas on the ground that the divine bliss flowing from Bhagavan is head and shoulder above the so called, experience of bliss of poetry as well as that of Brahman.

It has been further pointed out by the Gaudiyas that laukika rati is nothing but the modification of prakrta-sattva-guna, so pleasure derived from it cannot be everlasting, it is rather transient and slight. Also, it is material in nature and as being the effect of mayasakti it culminates into pain.

Jiva Gosvami further, says that the vibhavas, etc. or prakrta-rati can hardly arouse the genuine rasa. If at all, they are so presented that they can produce only the sentiment of disgust or bibhatsa-rasa and no other rasa is possible.

Hence the Gaudiyas contend that no material or earthly pleasure can be transmuted by any trick of alchemy in to the transcendental or super-natural or spiritual delight.
